Resolving the joint ergodicity problem for Hardy sequences Thanks: The first author was partially funded by Centro de Modelamiento Matemático (CMM) FB210005, BASAL funds for centers of excellence from ANID-Chile and ANID/Fondecyt/1241346. The third author was supported by the NCN Polonez Bis 3 grant No. 2022/47/P/ST1/00854 (H2020 MSCA GA No. 945339). The fourth author was partially supported by the NSF Grant DMS-2247331. The fifth author was supported by the Swiss National Science Foundation grant TMSGI2-211214. For the purpose of Open Access, the authors have applied a CC-BY public copyright licence to any Author Accepted Manuscript (AAM) version arising from this submission.
Sebastián Donoso, Andreas Koutsogiannis, Borys Kuca, Wenbo Sun, and Konstantinos Tsinas

Abstract
. The joint ergodicity classification problem aims to characterize those sequences which are jointly ergodic along an arbitrary dynamical system if and only if they satisfy two natural, simpler-to-verify conditions on this system. These two conditions, dubbed the difference and product ergodicity conditions, naturally arise from Berend and Bergelson’s pioneering work on joint ergodicity. Elaborating on our earlier work, we investigate this problem for Hardy sequences of polynomial growth, this time without making any independence assumptions on the sequences. Our main result establishes the “difficult” direction of the problem: if a Hardy family satisfies the difference and product ergodicity conditions on a given system, then it is jointly ergodic for this system. We also find that, surprisingly, the converse fails for certain pathological families of Hardy sequences, even though it holds for all “reasonable” Hardy families. We conclude by suggesting potential fixes to the statement of this problem. New ideas of independent interest developed in this paper include the structure theory of a family of factors generalizing Host-Kra and box factors; a strengthening of Tao-Ziegler’s concatenation results; and the most robust extension of a seminorm smoothing argument.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary: 37A44; Secondary: 11B30, 28D05

1.
Introduction

In 1977, Furstenberg gave his famous dynamical proof of Szemerédi’s theorem on arithmetic progressions, initiating a highly successful program of applying ergodic tools to problems in combinatorics and number theory
[
31
]
. As part of his proof, he showed that whenever
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T)
is a weakly mixing measure-preserving dynamical system, the averages

\displaystyle\E_{n\in[N]}T^{n}f_{1}\cdots T^{\ell n}f_{\ell}

(where
\E_{n\in[N]}:=\frac{1}{N}\sum\limits_{n=1}^{N}
and
[N]:=\{1,\ldots,N\}
for
N\in{\mathbb{N}}
) converge in
L^{2}(\mu)
to the product of integrals
\int f_{1}\;d\mu\cdots\int f_{\ell}\;d\mu
for all functions
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
. In doing so, he proved the first result on the
joint ergodicity
of multiple ergodic averages after the mean ergodic theorem.

Definition 1.1

(Joint ergodicity)
.

Let
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}:{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
be sequences and
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},

\ldots,T_{\ell})
be a system.
1
1

1

By which we mean commuting invertible measure preserving transformations
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell}
acting on a Lebesgue probability space
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu)
.
We say that
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}
are
jointly ergodic
for
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
(alternatively, the tuple
(T_{1}^{{\left\lfloor a_{1}(n)\right\rfloor}},\ldots,T_{\ell}^{{\left\lfloor a_{\ell}(n)\right\rfloor}})_{n}
is
jointly ergodic
on
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu)
) if

(1)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}\prod_{j=1}^{\ell}T_{j}^{{\left\lfloor a_{j}(n)\right\rfloor}}f_{j}-\prod_{j=1}^{\ell}\int f_{j}\,d\mu\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}=0

holds for all
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
. For
\ell=1,
we say that
a_{1}
is
ergodic
for
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1})
(and respectively we call
(T_{1}^{{\left\lfloor a_{1}(n)\right\rfloor}})_{n}

ergodic
).

In particular,
(T^{n})_{n}
is ergodic if and only if
T
is ergodic.

A few years after Furstenberg’s work, Bergelson and Berend inquired about the necessary and sufficient conditions for
(T_{1}^{n},\ldots,T_{\ell}^{n})_{n}
to be jointly ergodic, proving the following result.

Theorem 1.2

(
[
1
]
)
.

Let
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
be a system. Then
(T_{1}^{n},\ldots,T_{\ell}^{n})_{n}
is jointly ergodic if and only if the following two conditions hold:

(i)

T_{i}T_{j}^{-1}
is ergodic on
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu)
for all distinct
i,j\in[\ell]
;

(ii)

T_{1}\times\cdots\times T_{\ell}
is ergodic on the product system
(X^{\ell},{\mathcal{X}}^{\otimes\ell},\mu^{\ell})
.

It is interesting to understand the extent to which the Bergelson-Berend conditions for joint ergodicity can be extended to more general multiple ergodic averages, leading to the following definitions.

Definition 1.3
.

Let
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}:{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
be sequences and
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
be a system. We say that on the system, the sequences
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}
satisfy

(i)

the
difference ergodicity condition
if the sequence
(T_{i}^{{\left\lfloor a_{i}(n)\right\rfloor}}T_{j}^{-{\left\lfloor a_{j}(n)\right\rfloor}})_{n}
is ergodic
2
2

2

A sequence of transformations
(T_{n})_{n}
on a Lebesgue probability space
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu)
is called
ergodic
if

\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}T_{n}f-\int f\,d\mu\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}=0

for all
f\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
.
on
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu)
for all distinct
i,j\in[\ell]
; and

(ii)

the
product ergodicity condition
if
(T_{1}^{{\left\lfloor a_{1}(n)\right\rfloor}}\times\cdots\times T_{\ell}^{{\left\lfloor a_{\ell}(n)\right\rfloor}})_{n}
is ergodic on the product system
(X^{\ell},{\mathcal{X}}^{\otimes\ell},\mu^{\ell})
.

We then have the following natural problem whose variants were previously asked in
[
8
,
19
,
18
]
.

Problem 1.4

(Joint ergodicity classification problem)
.

Classify the sequences
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}:{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
with the following property: the sequences are jointly ergodic for a system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
if and only if they simultaneously satisfy the difference and product ergodicity conditions on the system.

Joint ergodicity has been a subject of intense scrutiny in the last decades
[
1
,
2
,
3
,
4
,
7
,
8
,
9
,
10
,
11
,
14
,
15
,
16
,
19
,
20
,
18
,
21
,
22
,
24
,
25
,
27
,
28
,
29
,
30
,
37
,
39
,
40
,
41
,
46
]
. Broadly speaking, the aforementioned works circulate around three themes:

(i)

establishing joint ergodicity and its variants for broad classes of sequences and systems;

(ii)

deriving easier-to-verify criteria for joint ergodicity;

(iii)

investigating Problem
1.4
for various classes of sequences.

So far, Problem
1.4
has been examined for integer polynomials, Hardy sequences, and generalized linear functions, and the equivalence postulated by Problem
1.4
has been verified affirmatively for the following classes of sequences:

•

linear functions
[
1
]
;

•

generalized linear functions
[
7
]
;

•

a single integer polynomial
a_{1}=\cdots=a_{\ell}

[
18
]
(see also
[
15
]
for a slight generalization);

•

pairwise independent integer polynomials
[
30
]
;

•

arbitrary integer polynomials
[
28
]
;

•

a single Hardy sequence
a_{1}=\cdots=a_{\ell}
of polynomial growth that stays logarithmically away from
{\mathbb{R}}[x]

[
19
]
;

•

pairwise independent Hardy sequence of polynomial growth
[
16
]
.

Of this rich variety of results, we particularly emphasize the resolution of Problem
1.4
for integer polynomials
[
28
]
and pairwise independent Hardy sequences of polynomial growth
[
16
]
. By identifying broad classes of sequences for which Problem
1.4
holds, the aforementioned works have given substantial credibility to why one should think of the difference and product ergodicity conditions as a right set of conditions guaranteeing joint ergodicity.

So far, the difficult part of Problem
1.4
has typically been to establish the converse direction, i.e. the sufficiency of the difference and product ergodicity conditions for joint ergodicity. The main result of this paper shows that the difficult direction in Problem
1.4
holds for all Hardy sequences of polynomial growth (whose collection is denoted below by
{\mathcal{H}}
; see Section
1.3
for the formal definition).

Theorem 1.5
.

Suppose that
a_{1},\dots,a_{\ell}\in\mathcal{H}
satisfy the difference and product ergodicity conditions on a system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
.
Then they are jointly ergodic for the system.

We also establish the following partial converse.

Definition 1.6
.

For
a\in\mathcal{H}
, we say that
a
is an
almost rational polynomial
if
a-p
converges for some
p
in
{\mathbb{Q}}[x]
. We say that
a

stays logarithmically away from
{\mathbb{Q}}[x]
(
resp.
{\mathbb{R}}[x]
) if
a-p\succ\log
for all
p
in
{\mathbb{Q}}[x]
(resp.
{\mathbb{R}}[x]
).

Theorem 1.7
.

Suppose that
a_{1},\dots,a_{\ell}\in\mathcal{H}
are jointly ergodic for a system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},

\ldots,T_{\ell})
. Then the following holds:

(i)

The sequences satisfy the product ergodicity condition on the system.

(ii)

Suppose additionally that for every
i\neq j
,
c_{i},c_{j}\in{\mathbb{R}}
, the function
c_{i}a_{i}-c_{j}a_{j}
is either an almost rational polynomial or stays logarithmically away from
{\mathbb{Q}}[x]
. Then the sequences satisfy the difference ergodicity condition on the system.

As a corollary, we provide an affirmative answer to Problem
1.4
for a large class of Hardy sequences.

Corollary 1.8
.

Let
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\in{\mathcal{H}}
be Hardy sequences with the property that for every
i\neq j
and
c_{i},c_{j}\in{\mathbb{R}}
the function
c_{i}a_{i}-c_{j}a_{j}
is either an almost rational polynomial or it stays logarithmically away from
{\mathbb{Q}}[x]
. Then the sequences are jointly ergodic for a system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
if and only if they satisfy the product and difference ergodicity conditions on the system.

In particular, we resolve
[
8
, Conjecture 6.1]
, i.e. Problem
1.4
for general real and fractional polynomials.

Corollary 1.9
.

Let
{\mathcal{F}}
be the class of functions of the form

a(x)=\beta_{d}x^{c_{d}}+\cdots+\beta_{1}x^{c_{1}}

for
\beta_{i}\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
c_{i}\geq 0
. Then any sequences
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\in{\mathcal{F}}
are jointly ergodic for a system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
if and only if they satisfy the product and difference ergodicity conditions on the system.

We also show that, surprisingly, joint ergodicity does not always imply the difference ergodicity condition, yielding a negative answer to Problem
1.4
in general. The failure of the (supposedly) easy direction in Problem
1.4
is instantiated by the following example.

Theorem 1.10
.

Let
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T)
be a system. Then
(T^{n},T^{n+{\left\lfloor\log_{2}n\right\rfloor}})_{n}
is jointly ergodic if and only if
T
is (strongly) mixing. By contrast,
(T^{{\left\lfloor\log_{2}n\right\rfloor}})_{n}
is ergodic if and only the system is conjugate to a one-point system, or equivalently
\mu
is concentrated on a single point. Consequently, if
T
is mixing but not conjugate to a one-point system, then
(T^{n},T^{n+{\left\lfloor\log_{2}n\right\rfloor}})_{n}
is jointly ergodic whereas
(T^{{\left\lfloor\log_{2}n\right\rfloor}})_{n}
is not ergodic.

To conclude the discussion, we remark that in this work we essentially resolve Problem
1.4
for polynomial and Hardy sequences of polynomial growth, i.e. classes for which we have a reduction-of-complexity argument based on van der Corput’s inequality. Up until now, we have had no results at all for general real polynomials and several different Hardy sequences (the case when all of them are the same has been partially dealt with in
[
19
,
18
]
).

Lastly, in Section
8
, we propose several ways of amending the statement of Problem
1.4
for Hardy sequences that would avoid the issues posed by the counterexample for Theorem
1.10
. While we obtain some preliminary results towards these modified versions of Problem
1.4
, more research is needed in order to see if they could serve as a good fix to the statement of Problem
1.4
.

1.1.
Outline of the argument

In
[
30
, Theorem 2.5]
, Frantzikinakis and the third author derived the following simpler-to-verify criteria for joint ergodicity.

Theorem 1.11

(Joint ergodicity criteria)
.

Let
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}:{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
be sequences and
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
be a system with each
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell}
ergodic. Then
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}
are jointly ergodic for
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
if and only if the following two conditions hold:

(i)

a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}
are
good for seminorm control
for the system in that there exists
s\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that for all 1-bounded
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}T_{1}^{{\left\lfloor a_{1}(n)\right\rfloor}}f_{1}\cdots T_{\ell}^{{\left\lfloor a_{\ell}(n)\right\rfloor}}f_{\ell}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}=0

whenever
\lvert\!|\!|f_{j}|\!|\!\rvert_{s,T_{j}}=0
for some
j\in[\ell]
;

(ii)

a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}
are
good for equidistribution
for the system
if for all eigenvalues
\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{\ell}
of
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell}
respectively, not all of which are 0, we have

(2)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\E_{n\in[N]}e(\lambda_{1}{\left\lfloor a_{1}(n)\right\rfloor}+\cdots+\lambda_{\ell}{\left\lfloor a_{\ell}(n)\right\rfloor})=0.

While the equidistribution properties of Hardy sequences are well-understood thanks to Boshernitzan
[
12
]
, establishing seminorm control over multiple ergodic averages of interest is a highly challenging problem.
Up to several years ago, few seminorm estimates were known for multiple ergodic averages considered in this paper. Then came two breakthroughs which dramatically changed the situation. On one hand, the fifth author significantly refined existing arguments and obtained seminorm estimates for essentially all possible (unweighted) multiple ergodic averages along Hardy sequences of a single transformation
[
46
]
.
3
3

3

In another interesting work that came out around the same time, Bergelson, Moreira, and Richter showed that one can get seminorm estimates for another large class of averages if one is willing to introduce a suitable weight
[
8
]
.
On the other hand, Frantzikinakis and the third author introduced a seminorm smoothing technique
[
28
,
30
]
that gave seminorm estimates for multiple ergodic averages of
commuting
transformations along integer polynomials. As a starting point, they used earlier box seminorm estimates obtained by the first, second and fourth author jointly with Ferré-Moragues
[
15
]
, based on a concatenation result of Tao and Ziegler
[
45
]
.

Generalizing all the aforementioned arguments, we have recently shown
[
16
]
that averages of commuting transformations along Hardy sequences can be controlled by certain seminorms that generalize the now classical Host-Kra and box seminorms. We then used a variant of the seminorm smoothing argument to obtain (quantitative) Host-Kra seminorm estimates for averages of commuting transformations along any family of pairwise independent Hardy sequences; from this we derived a number of corollaries of joint ergodicity, recurrence, and
L^{2}(\mu)
convergence. In this work, we drop the assumption of pairwise independence and show that under some necessary conditions which are good enough for the purpose of proving Theorem
1.5
, we can get Host-Kra seminorm control for averages along not necessarily pairwise independent Hardy sequences.

Theorem 1.12
.

Let
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\in{\mathcal{H}}
and
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
be a system with
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell}
ergodic. If the sequences satisfy the difference ergodicity condition, then they are good for seminorm control for the system.

The proof of Theorem
1.12
occupies a bulk of this paper. Starting with the generalized box seminorm estimates from
[
16
]
, we use a new variant of the seminorm smoothing argument to obtain another collection of seminorm estimates for the averages in question. For instance, the estimates from
[
16
]
allow us to control the limit of an average

(3)

\displaystyle\E_{n\in[N]}T_{1}^{{\lfloor n^{3/2}\rfloor}}f_{1}\cdot T_{2}^{{\lfloor n^{3/2}+\log n\rfloor}}f_{2}\cdot T_{3}^{{\lfloor n^{3/2}+n\log n\rfloor}}f_{3}\cdot T_{4}^{{\lfloor n^{3/2}+n\log n\rfloor}}f_{4}

by a box seminorm of
f_{1}
involving many copies of
T_{1},T_{1}T_{2}^{-1},T_{1}T_{3}^{-1},T_{1}T_{4}^{-1}
. By contrast, our smoothing argument gives us control over (the limit of) (
3
) by a seminorm of
f_{1}
along many copies of
T_{1},T_{1}T_{2}^{-1},T_{3}T_{4}^{-1}
.
A priori, there is no reason why the new seminorm
would be easier to work with than the original one. It is at this point where the difference ergodicity condition kicks in. It forces
T_{1}T_{2}^{-1},T_{3}T_{4}^{-1}
to be ergodic, hence the intermediate seminorm involving
T_{1},T_{1}T_{2}^{-1},T_{3}T_{4}^{-1}
can be replaced by a proper Host-Kra seminorm along
T_{1}
.

To carry out the argument above, we introduce a number of tools of independent interest. In Section
2.2
, we introduce factors related to the generalized box seminorms in much the same manner in which Host-Kra factors are related to Host-Kra seminorms (Theorem
3.1
). We also obtain a weak structure theorem for these factors, showing that they can be generated via dual functions weighed by nilsequences of appropriate step. In Section
3
, we derive a Tao-Ziegler-type concatenation result for such factors. In fact, our concatenation strengthens Tao and Ziegler’s results
[
45
]
in the following sense. While Tao and Ziegler show for example that

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{H_{1},\dots,H_{d}}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{H^{\prime}_{1},\dots,H^{\prime}_{d^{\prime}}}\subseteq\mathcal{Z}_{(H_{i}+H^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}})_{i\in[d],i^{\prime}\in[d^{\prime}]}}

for any finitely generated subgroups
H_{1},\ldots,H_{d},H^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,H^{\prime}_{d^{\prime}}\subseteq{\mathbb{Z}}^{\ell}
, we show that

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{G_{1},\dots,G_{s},H_{1},\dots,H_{d}}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{G_{1},\dots,G_{s},H^{\prime}_{1},\dots,H^{\prime}_{d^{\prime}}}\subseteq\mathcal{Z}_{G_{1},\dots,G_{s},(H_{i}+H^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}})_{i\in[d],i^{\prime}\in[d^{\prime}]}}

for any finitely generated subgroups
G_{1},\ldots,G_{s},H_{1},\ldots,H_{d},H^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,H^{\prime}_{d^{\prime}}\subseteq{\mathbb{Z}}^{\ell}
. Because we are able to fix the same set of subgroups
G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}
in all the factors that we compare, we refer to our concatenation result as
relative concatenation
.

Relative concatenation plays a key role in the seminorm smoothing argument carried out in Section
4
. The version of the smoothing argument developed in this paper (Theorem
4.10
) is more robust than previous variations from
[
16
,
28
,
30
]
. While performing smoothing for integer polynomials
[
28
]
, Frantzikinakis and the third author had to make some a priori ergodicity assumptions on the system, and then they had to deal with intermediate averages in which functions are invariant under various transformations. Not only did keeping track of all this information throughout an inductive argument require an unwieldy formalism; even worse, the argument could not be translated to the language of generalized box seminorms needed in the setting of Hardy sequences. Hence the need for a more flexible smoothing argument that we develop in this paper. Our argument is conceptually simpler than the one from
[
28
]
in that we make no a priori assumptions on the ergodicity of the transformations, and we have to track much less information throughout the proof than in
[
28
]
. The key innovation that makes it possible is the use of relative concatenation in the smoothing argument. On top of
ping
and
pong
, the two traditional steps of the smoothing argument, we add a third step, where we concatenate the original seminorm controlling our average (along
T_{1},T_{1}T_{2}^{-1},T_{1}T_{3}^{-1},T_{1}T_{4}^{-1}
in (
3
)) with an auxiliary one obtained in the
pong
step to get an upgraded seminorm (along
T_{1},T_{1}T_{2}^{-1},T_{3}T_{4}^{-1}
in (
3
)) controlling our average. The details of this innovation are explained with an explicit example in Section
4.2
.

Sections
5
and
6
are then dedicated to extracting information from the difference ergodicity assumption. The ergodicity of
(T_{i}^{{\left\lfloor a_{i}(n)\right\rfloor}}T_{j}^{-{\left\lfloor a_{j}(n)\right\rfloor}})_{n}
imposes strict conditions on the spectral measures of functions in
L^{2}(\mu)
since by the Herglotz-Bochner theorem, a spectral measure may not have a point mass on any
(t,s)
for which the exponential sum

(4)

\displaystyle\E_{n\in[N]}e(t{\left\lfloor a_{i}(n)\right\rfloor}-s{\left\lfloor a_{j}(n)\right\rfloor})

does not vanish as
N\to\infty
. The bulk of these two sections is then dedicated to a case-by-case study of exponential sums (
4
). While the details of the computations performed in these sections are not very illuminating, we manage to extract enough information to upgrade seminorm control obtained in smoothing to a Host-Kra seminorm control over our average. Section
7
then pieces everything together and derives the theorems stated in the introduction.

1.2.
Basic notation

We start with presenting basic notation used throughout the paper.

The symbols
{\mathbb{C}},{\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{R}}_{+},{\mathbb{Q}},{\mathbb{Z}},{\mathbb{N}}_{0},{\mathbb{N}}
respectively stand for the sets of complex numbers, reals, positive reals, rationals, integers, nonnegative integers, and positive integers. With
{\mathbb{T}}
, we denote the one dimensional torus, and we often identify it with
{\mathbb{R}}/{\mathbb{Z}}
or with
[0,1)
. For a ring
R
, we denote the collection of polynomials over
R
in variables
x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}
via
R[x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}]
. If
E\subseteq G
is a subset of a group
G
, then
\langle E\rangle
is the subgroup generated by
E
.

We frequently use the interval notation to mean either a real interval or its restriction to
{\mathbb{Z}}
; the meaning would typically be clear from the context. If we restrict to
{\mathbb{Z}}
, then we write
[M,N]=\{M,\ldots,N\}
and
[N]=\{1,\ldots,|N|\}
for integers
M\leq N
.

For a finite set
E,
we define the average of a sequence
a:E\to{\mathbb{C}}
as

\E_{n\in E}a(n):=\frac{1}{|E|}\sum_{n\in E}a(n).

We will mainly use finite averages along subsets of integers of the form
[N]^{s}
or
[\pm N]^{s}
, also known as
Cesàro averages
.

We let
{\mathcal{C}}z:=\overline{z}
be the complex conjugate of
z\in{\mathbb{C}}
.

For a set
E
, we define its indicator function by
1_{E}
. Whenever convenient, we also use the notation
1(E)
for an event
E
.

Typically, we use underlined symbols
{\underline{h}}
to denote elements of
{\mathbb{R}}^{s}
(which we think as tuples of parameters), and we employ the bold notation
{\mathbf{x}}
to denote elements of
{\mathbb{R}}^{\ell}
(which we think as points in a space); sometimes we also use bold notation to denote
s
-tuples of points in
{\mathbb{R}}^{\ell}
, e.g.,
{\mathbf{m}}=({\mathbf{m}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{m}}_{s})\in({\mathbb{R}}^{s})^{\ell}
. We denote the integer and fractional parts of
{\mathbf{x}}
via

\displaystyle{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{x}}\right\rfloor}=({\left\lfloor x_{1}\right\rfloor},\ldots,{\left\lfloor x_{\ell}\right\rfloor})\quad\textrm{and}\quad\left\{{\mathbf{x}}\right\}=(\left\{x_{1}\right\},\ldots,\left\{x_{\ell}\right\}).

We often write
{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s}
for a vector of 0s and 1s of length
s
. For
{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s}
and
{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{s}
, we set
{\underline{\epsilon}}\cdot{\underline{h}}:=\epsilon_{1}h_{1}+\cdots+\epsilon_{s}h_{s}
,
\mathopen{}\left|{\underline{h}}\mathclose{}\right|:=|h_{1}|+\cdots+|h_{s}|
and

(5)

\displaystyle h_{j}^{\epsilon_{j}}=\begin{cases}h_{j},\;&\epsilon_{j}=0\\
h_{j}^{\prime},\;&\epsilon_{j}=1\end{cases}\quad\textrm{for}\quad j\in[s].

Given a system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
and
{\mathbf{m}}=(m_{1},\dots,m_{\ell})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\ell}
, we define

T^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}\right\rfloor}}:=T_{1}^{{\left\lfloor m_{1}\right\rfloor}}\cdots T_{\ell}^{{\left\lfloor m_{\ell}\right\rfloor}}.

For
j\in[\ell]
, we set
{\mathbf{e}}_{j}
to be the unit vector in
{\mathbb{R}}^{\ell}
in the
j
-th direction, and we let
{\mathbf{e}}_{0}=\mathbf{0}
, so that
T^{{\mathbf{e}}_{j}}=T_{j}
for
j\in[\ell]
and
T^{{\mathbf{e}}_{0}}
is the identity transformation.

For
M>0
, we say that a function
f\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
is
M
-bounded
if
\left\|f\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\mu)}\leq M
.

For a
\sigma
-algebra
{\mathcal{A}}\subseteq{\mathcal{X}}
and
f\in L^{1}(\mu)
, we let
\E(f|{\mathcal{A}})
be the conditional expectation of
f
with respect to
{\mathcal{A}}
. By abuse of notation, we can identify the
\sigma
-algebra
{\mathcal{A}}
with the algebra of functions
L^{2}({\mathcal{A}})
. One algebra that we often work with is the
invariant factor

{\mathcal{I}}(T)=\{A\in{\mathcal{X}}:\;T^{-1}A=A\}

of a transformation
T
.

Given two functions
a,b:(x_{0},\infty)\to\mathbb{R}
for some
x_{0}\in{\mathbb{R}}
we write

(i)

a\ll b
or
a=O(b)
if there exists
C>0
and
x_{1}\geq x_{0}
such that
|a(x)|\leq C|b(x)|
for all
x\geq x_{1}
;

(ii)

a\asymp b
if
a\ll b
and
b\ll a
;

(iii)

a\prec b
or
a=o(b)
if
\lim\limits_{x\to\infty}\frac{a(x)}{b(x)}=0
.

If the absolute constant in (i) depends on some parameters
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
, then we write
a\ll_{x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}}b
,
a=O_{x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}}(b)
, etc. If in (ii) we want to emphasize what parameter we are taking to infinity, we can also write
a=o_{x\to\infty;x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}}(b)
to emphasize that
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
are kept fixed while
x
goes to infinity.

For a finitely generated abelian group
G
and a bounded function
\psi:G\to{\mathbb{C}}
, we define

\displaystyle\E_{{\mathbf{m}}\in G}\psi({\mathbf{m}})=\lim_{N\to\infty}\E_{{\mathbf{m}}\in I_{N}}\psi({\mathbf{m}})

to be the limit along any Følner sequence
(I_{N})_{N}
on
G
(if such a limit exists), and if
\psi
is nonnegative, then we define

\displaystyle\oE_{{\mathbf{m}}\in G}\psi({\mathbf{m}})=\sup_{(I_{N})_{N}}\limsup_{N\to\infty}\E_{{\mathbf{m}}\in I_{N}}\psi({\mathbf{m}}),

where the supremum is taken along any Følner sequence on
G
. Likewise, if
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu)
is a probability space and
f_{\mathbf{m}}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
for all
{\mathbf{m}}\in G
, then we define
\E_{{\mathbf{m}}\in G}f_{\mathbf{m}}
to be the
L^{2}(\mu)
limit (whenever it exists).

1.3.
Basic properties of Hardy sequences

Let
B
be the collection of germs at
\infty
of real valued functions defined on some halfline
(x_{0},\infty)
for
x_{0}\geq 0
(thus functions that eventually agree are considered the same). A
Hardy field
is a subfield of the ring
(B,+,\cdot)
that is closed under differentiation.

For various technical reasons, we will consider Hardy fields
\mathcal{H}_{0}
which are closed under composition and compositional inversion of functions, when defined. We say that
a\in{\mathcal{H}}
has
polynomial growth
if it satisfies
a(x)\ll x^{d}
for some
d>0
, and we then let

\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}=\{a\in{\mathcal{H}}_{0}:\;a(x)\ll x^{d}\;\;\textrm{for\; some}\;\;d>0\},

which is still a Hardy field closed under compositions, but not closed under inverses.

A prime example is the Hardy field
\mathcal{LE}
of
logarithmico-exponential functions
, which consists of functions defined on a half-line
(x_{0},+\infty)
by a finite combination of symbols
+,-,\times,\div,\sqrt[n]{\cdot},\exp,\log
acting on the real variable
t
and on real constants. While
\mathcal{LE}
is not closed under compositional inverses, it is contained in the Hardy field of Pfaffian functions that is. See
[
21
,
22
,
33
]
for more on Hardy fields in general and
\mathcal{LE}
in particular.

Theorem 1.13

(
[
12
, Theorem 1.3]
)
.

Let
a\in\mathcal{H}
. The sequence
(a(n))_{n}
is equidistributed
\mod~\!1
(meaning that

\lim_{N\to\infty}\E_{n\in[N]}F(a(n)\!\!\!\mod 1)=\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}F(t)\,dt

for any Riemann-integrable function
F\colon{\mathbb{T}}\to{\mathbb{C}}
) if and only if for every
p\in{\mathbb{Q}}[x]
we have

\lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{|a(x)-p(x)|}{\log x}=\infty.

Definition 1.14

(Independence properties of Hardy sequences)
.

Let
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\in{\mathcal{H}}
. We call them:

(i)

independent
if for any
c_{1},\ldots,c_{\ell}\in{\mathbb{R}}
not all 0, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{|c_{1}a_{1}(x)+\cdots+c_{\ell}a_{\ell}(x)|}{\log x}=\infty,

and
dependent
otherwise;

(ii)

pairwise independent
if
a_{i},a_{j}
are independent for any
1\leq i<j\leq\ell
, and
pairwise dependent
otherwise.

Definition 1.15

(Strongly nonpolynomial Hardy sequences)
.

We call
a\in{\mathcal{H}}

strongly nonpolynomial
if there exists
d\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
such that
x^{d}\prec a(x)\prec x^{d+1}
.

2.
Generalized box seminorms and factors

This section discusses generalized box seminorms along finitely generated subgroups
G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{\ell}
and associated factors, all of which extend the classical Host-Kra and box seminorms and factors defined for subgroups of
{\mathbb{Z}}^{\ell}
(see e.g.
[
15
,
34
,
35
,
36
,
45
]
). The generalized box seminorms have been defined in our earlier paper
[
16
]
, in which we proved their main properties; the notion of factors is new. For the duration of the entire section, we fix a system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
.

2.1.
Generalized box seminorms

Let
G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{\ell}
be finitely generated subgroups. For
f\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
and
{\mathbf{m}},{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\ell}
, let
\Delta_{({\mathbf{m}},{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime})}f=T^{{\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}\rfloor}}f\cdot T^{{\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime}\rfloor}}\overline{f}
. Recall from
[
16
]
that we can define two seminorms of
f
along
G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}
. The first one is given by

\displaystyle\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{\begin{subarray}{c}G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle=\mathopen{}\left(\E_{{\mathbf{m}}_{1},{\mathbf{m}}_{1}^{\prime}\in G_{1}}\cdots\E_{{\mathbf{m}}_{s},{\mathbf{m}}_{s}^{\prime}\in G_{s}}\int\Delta_{({{\mathbf{m}}_{1}},{{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime}_{1}}),\ldots,({{\mathbf{m}}_{s}},{{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime}_{s}})}f\,d\mu\mathclose{}\right)^{1/2^{s}}

\displaystyle=\mathopen{}\left(\E_{{\mathbf{m}}_{1},{\mathbf{m}}_{1}^{\prime}\in G_{1}}\cdots\E_{{\mathbf{m}}_{s},{\mathbf{m}}_{s}^{\prime}\in G_{s}}\int\prod_{{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s}}{\mathcal{C}}^{|{\underline{\epsilon}}|}T^{{\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}_{1}^{\epsilon_{1}}\rfloor}+\cdots+{\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}_{s}^{\epsilon_{s}}\rfloor}}f\,d\mu\mathclose{}\right)^{1/2^{s}},

while the second one is

\displaystyle\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert^{+}_{\begin{subarray}{c}G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle:=\mathopen{}\left(\E_{{\mathbf{m}}_{1},{\mathbf{m}}_{1}^{\prime}\in G_{1}}\cdots\E_{{\mathbf{m}}_{s},{\mathbf{m}}_{s}^{\prime}\in G_{s}}\mathopen{}\left|\int\Delta_{({{\mathbf{m}}_{1}},{{\mathbf{m}}_{1}^{\prime}}),\ldots,({{\mathbf{m}}_{s}},{{\mathbf{m}}_{s}^{\prime}})}f\,d\mu\mathclose{}\right|^{2}\mathclose{}\right)^{1/2^{s+1}}

\displaystyle=\mathopen{}\left(\E_{{\mathbf{m}}_{1},{\mathbf{m}}_{1}^{\prime}\in G_{1}}\cdots\E_{{\mathbf{m}}_{s},{\mathbf{m}}_{s}^{\prime}\in G_{s}}\int\Delta_{({{\mathbf{m}}_{1}},{{\mathbf{m}}_{1}^{\prime}}),\ldots,({{\mathbf{m}}_{s}},{{\mathbf{m}}_{s}^{\prime}})}f\otimes\overline{f}\,d(\mu\times\mu)\mathclose{}\right)^{1/2^{s+1}}

\displaystyle=\lvert\!|\!|f\otimes\overline{f}|\!|\!\rvert_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}^{1/2}.

See (
5
) for the convention regarding expressions like
T^{{\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}_{1}^{\epsilon_{1}}\rfloor}}
.

If
G_{i}=\langle{\mathbf{v}}_{i}\rangle
, we also write

\displaystyle\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{\begin{subarray}{c}G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}\end{subarray}}=\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\mathbf{v}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{v}}_{s}\end{subarray}}\quad\textrm{and}\quad\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert^{+}_{\begin{subarray}{c}G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}\end{subarray}}=\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert^{+}_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\mathbf{v}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{v}}_{s}\end{subarray}},

and if
G_{1}=\cdots=G_{s}=G
, then

\displaystyle\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{\begin{subarray}{c}G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}\end{subarray}}=\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{G^{\times s}}\quad\textrm{and}\quad\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert^{+}_{\begin{subarray}{c}G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}\end{subarray}}=\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert^{+}_{G^{\times s}}.

For instance, if
\ell=s=2
and
G_{i}=\langle\alpha_{i}{\mathbf{e}}_{i}\rangle
, then

\displaystyle\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{\begin{subarray}{c}G_{1},G_{2}\end{subarray}}^{4}=\E\limits_{m_{1},m^{\prime}_{1}\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\E\limits_{m_{2},m^{\prime}_{2}\in{\mathbb{Z}}}

\displaystyle\int T_{1}^{{\lfloor\alpha_{1}m_{1}\rfloor}}T_{2}^{{\lfloor\alpha_{2}m_{2}\rfloor}}f\cdot T_{1}^{{\lfloor\alpha_{1}m_{1}\rfloor}}T_{2}^{{\lfloor\alpha_{2}m^{\prime}_{2}\rfloor}}\overline{f}

\displaystyle\qquad\cdot T_{1}^{{\lfloor\alpha_{1}m^{\prime}_{1}\rfloor}}T_{2}^{{\lfloor\alpha_{2}m_{2}\rfloor}}\overline{f}\cdot T_{1}^{{\lfloor\alpha_{1}m_{1}^{\prime}\rfloor}}T_{2}^{{\lfloor\alpha_{2}m_{2}^{\prime}\rfloor}}f\,d\mu

and

\displaystyle\Bigl(\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert^{+}_{\begin{subarray}{c}G_{1},G_{2}\end{subarray}}\Bigr)^{8}=\E\limits_{m_{1},m^{\prime}_{1}\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\E\limits_{m_{2},m^{\prime}_{2}\in{\mathbb{Z}}}

\displaystyle\left|\int T_{1}^{{\lfloor\alpha_{1}m_{1}\rfloor}}T_{2}^{{\lfloor\alpha_{2}m_{2}\rfloor}}f\cdot T_{1}^{{\lfloor\alpha_{1}m_{1}\rfloor}}T_{2}^{{\lfloor\alpha_{2}m^{\prime}_{2}\rfloor}}\overline{f}\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\cdot\left.T_{1}^{{\lfloor\alpha_{1}m^{\prime}_{1}\rfloor}}T_{2}^{{\lfloor\alpha_{2}m_{2}\rfloor}}\overline{f}\cdot T_{1}^{{\lfloor\alpha_{1}m_{1}^{\prime}\rfloor}}T_{2}^{{\lfloor\alpha_{2}m_{2}^{\prime}\rfloor}}f\,d\mu\right|^{2}.

The following properties of generalized box seminorms have all been established in
[
16
, Section 3]
.

Lemma 2.1

(Basic properties of seminorms)
.

Let
G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{\ell}
be finitely generated subgroups and
f\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
. Then:

(i)

(Symmetry) For any permutation
\sigma:[s]\to[s]
, we have

\displaystyle\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}=\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{G_{\sigma(1)},\ldots,G_{\sigma(s)}}\quad\textrm{and}\quad\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}^{+}=\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{G_{\sigma(1)},\ldots,G_{\sigma(s)}}^{+}.

(ii)

(Monotonicity) For any finitely generated
G_{s+1}\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{\ell}
, we have

\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}\leq\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}^{+}\leq\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s+1}}\leq\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s+1}}^{+}.

(iii)

(Subgroup property) If
G^{\prime}_{i}\subseteq G_{i}
for all
i\in[s]
, then

\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}\leq\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{G^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,G^{\prime}_{s}}\quad\textrm{and}\quad\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert^{+}_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}\leq\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{G^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,G^{\prime}_{s}}^{+}.

Conversely, if the index of
G^{\prime}_{i}
in
G_{i}
is at most
D
for all
i\in[s]
, then

\displaystyle\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{G^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,G^{\prime}_{s}}^{+}\ll_{D,s}\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}^{+}.

(iv)

(Rescaling property) Let
K>0
. If for every
i\in[s]
we have

\displaystyle G_{i}=\langle{\mathbf{g}}_{i1},\ldots,{\mathbf{g}}_{iK_{i}}\rangle\quad\textrm{and}\quad G^{\prime}_{i}=\langle\alpha_{i1}{\mathbf{g}}_{i1},\ldots,\alpha_{iK_{i}}{\mathbf{g}}_{iK_{i}}\rangle

for some
K_{i}\leq K
and nonzero real
1/K\leq|\alpha_{ij}|\leq K
, then

\displaystyle\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert^{+}_{G^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,G^{\prime}_{s}}\asymp_{\ell,K,s}\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert^{+}_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}

and

\displaystyle\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{G^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,G^{\prime}_{s}}\asymp_{\ell,K,s}\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}\quad\textrm{(if\; additionally}\;s\geq 2).

(v)

(Equality of ergodic averages) For all finitely generated subgroups
G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}~\subseteq~{\mathbb{R}}^{\ell}
for which
\E\limits_{{\mathbf{m}}\in G_{i}}T^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}\right\rfloor}}f=\E\limits_{{\mathbf{m}}\in G^{\prime}_{i}}T^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}\right\rfloor}}f
holds for all
f\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
and
i\in[s]
, we have

\displaystyle\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}=\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{G^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,G^{\prime}_{s}}.

2.2.
Generalized box factors

Our goal now is to construct factors
{\mathcal{Z}}^{+}_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}
with the property that

(6)

\displaystyle\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}^{+}=0\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\E(f|{\mathcal{Z}}_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}^{+})=0.

If
G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}
lie inside
{\mathbb{Z}}^{\ell}
, then there exist factors
{\mathcal{Z}}_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}
with the property

(7)

\displaystyle\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}=0\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\E(f|{\mathcal{Z}}_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}})=0

(see for example
[
34
,
35
,
44
,
45
]
).
However, if
G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}
do not lie inside
{\mathbb{Z}}^{\ell}
, then in general there exists no suitable factor satisfying (
7
). For example,
[
17
, Proposition 4.4]
indicates that in general there is no factor corresponding to the seminorm
\lvert\!|\!|\cdot|\!|\!\rvert_{\langle\alpha\rangle}
for a system
(X,\mathcal{X},\mu,T)
and
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}\backslash\mathbb{Q}
. We also refer the readers to the discussion below Definition
2.10
for this topic.

Following e.g.
[
5
,
26
,
32
]
, we define multiparameter nilsequences.

Definition 2.2

(Multiparameter nilsequences)
.

A
degree-
s

{\mathbb{Q}}^{K}
-nilsequence
is a bounded sequence
\psi:{\mathbb{Q}}^{K}\to{\mathbb{C}}
, where:

•

G/\Gamma
is a nilmanifold (with
G
connected and simply connected) with a degree-
s
filtration
4
4

4

We recall that a filtration
G_{\bullet}=(G_{i})_{i=0}^{\infty}
on a group
G
is a collection of subgroups
G=G_{0}=G_{1}\supseteq G_{2}\supseteq\cdots
satisfying
[G_{i},G_{j}]\subseteq G_{i+j}
for all
i,j\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
, where
[A,B]
is the group generated by the commutators
[a,b]=a^{-1}b^{-1}ab
for
a\in A,b\in B
.

G_{\bullet}=(G_{i})_{i=0}^{\infty}
;

•

g:{\mathbb{Q}}^{K}\to G
is a polynomial sequence adapted to the filtration
G_{\bullet}
, meaning that

\partial_{h_{i}}\dots\partial_{h_{1}}g(n)\in G_{i}

for all
i\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
n,h_{1},\dots,h_{i}\in{\mathbb{Q}}^{K}
(here,
\partial_{h}g(n)=g(n)g(n+h)^{-1}
);

•

\psi({\underline{m}})=F(g({\underline{m}})\Gamma)
for a bounded Riemann integrable function
F:G/\Gamma\to{\mathbb{C}}
.

Note that since the group
G
is a connected, simply connected, nilpotent Lie group, it is divisible, and so every polynomial sequence
g:{\mathbb{Z}}^{K}\to G
naturally extends to
{\mathbb{Q}}^{K}
(in fact, to all of
{\mathbb{R}}^{K}
). We define nilsequences on
{\mathbb{Q}}^{K}
as opposed to
{\mathbb{Z}}^{K}
because the proof of Proposition
2.6
below uses basic linear algebra and requires us to have them defined over a vector space (rather than a group), and we choose
{\mathbb{Q}}^{K}
rather than
{\mathbb{R}}^{K}
so that the definition is compatible with the notion of coordinate maps below (which does not do the job if defined for real vector spaces).

Our next goal is to obtain a suitable notion of nilsequence on general finitely generated subgroups
G\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{\ell}
. To this end, we need a way to map
G
to a subgroup of
{\mathbb{Z}}^{K}
.

Definition 2.3

(Coordinate maps)
.

Let
G\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{\ell}
be a finitely generated subgroup and
K=\dim(\Span_{\mathbb{Q}}G)
. We call a vector space isomorphism
\phi:{\mathbb{Q}}^{K}\to\Span_{\mathbb{Q}}G
a
coordinate map
if
G
is a finite index subgroup of
\phi({\mathbb{Z}}^{K})
.

Examples 2.4
.

Here are some examples of coordinate maps:

(i)

if
G=\langle(\sqrt{2},\sqrt{3})\rangle\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
, then
\phi(m)=(\sqrt{2}m,\sqrt{3}m)
is a coordinate map;

(ii)

if
G=\langle\sqrt{2},\sqrt{3}\rangle\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}
, then
\phi(m_{1},m_{2})=\sqrt{2}m_{1}+\sqrt{3}m_{2}
is a coordinate map.

Note that we cannot in general take
\phi({\mathbb{Z}}^{K})=G
. Indeed, if
G=\langle(2,0),(0,2),(1,1)\rangle
, then we can embed it as a finite index subgroup into
\langle(1,0),(0,1)\rangle={\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
, but
G
itself is not group-isomorphic to
{\mathbb{Z}}^{K}
for any
K\in{\mathbb{N}}
.

With the help of coordinate maps, the notion of nilsequences can be extended to general finitely generated subgroups
G\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{\ell}
. We remind the reader that
K=\dim(\Span_{\mathbb{Q}}G)
in the entire ensuing discussion.

Definition 2.5

(Nilsequences on
G
)
.

Let
G\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{\ell}
be a finitely generated subgroup. Then a sequence
\psi:G\to{\mathbb{C}}
is a
degree-
s
nilsequence on
G
if there exists a coordinate map
\phi:{\mathbb{Q}}^{K}\to\Span_{\mathbb{Q}}G

and a degree-
s

{\mathbb{Q}}^{K}
-nilsequence
\theta\colon{\mathbb{Q}}^{K}\to{\mathbb{C}}
such that
\theta|_{\phi^{-1}(G)}=\psi\circ\phi|_{\phi^{-1}(G)}
.
We then define
\Nil_{s}(G)
to be the collection of all degree-
s
nilsequences on
G
.

Proposition 2.6
.

Let
G\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{\ell}
be a finitely generated subgroup. If
\psi
is a degree-
s
nilsequence on
G
with respect to some coordinate map, then it is a degree-
s
nilsequence on
G
with respect to any coordinate map.

Proof.

Let
\psi\colon G\to{\mathbb{C}}
be a map and
\phi_{1},\phi_{2}\colon{\mathbb{Q}}^{K}\to\Span_{{\mathbb{R}}}G
be coordinate maps. Suppose that
\psi\circ\phi_{1}|_{\phi_{1}^{-1}(G)}=\theta_{1}|_{\phi_{1}^{-1}(G)}
for some degree-
s

{\mathbb{Q}}^{K}
-nilsequence
\theta_{1}\colon{\mathbb{Q}}^{K}\to{\mathbb{C}}
. It suffices to show that
\psi\circ\phi_{2}|_{\phi_{2}^{-1}(G)}=\theta_{2}|_{\phi_{2}^{-1}(G)}
for some degree-
s

{\mathbb{Q}}^{K}
-nilsequence
\theta_{2}\colon{\mathbb{Q}}^{K}\to{\mathbb{C}}
.

Note that for all
n\in\phi_{2}^{-1}(G)
, we have

\psi\circ\phi_{2}(n)=\psi\circ(\phi_{1}\circ\phi_{1}^{-1})\circ\phi_{2}(n)=(\psi\circ\phi_{1})\circ(\phi_{1}^{-1}\circ\phi_{2})(n)=\theta_{1}\circ(\phi_{1}^{-1}\circ\phi_{2})(n).

Because
\phi:=\phi_{1}^{-1}\circ\phi_{2}
is a vector space isomorphism from
{\mathbb{Q}}^{K}
to itself, it suffices to show that degree-
s

{\mathbb{Q}}^{K}
-nilsequences are closed under compositions with vector space isomorphisms.

To see this, assume that
\theta_{1}=F(g(\cdot)\Gamma)
. Then
\theta_{1}\circ\phi=F(g\circ\phi(\cdot)\Gamma)
. Since
\phi:{\mathbb{Q}}^{K}\to{\mathbb{Q}}^{K}
is a vector space isomorphism, we have that

\partial_{h_{i}}\dots\partial_{h_{1}}g\circ\phi(n)=\partial_{\phi(h_{i})}\dots\partial_{\phi(h_{1})}g(\phi(n))

for all
i\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
n,h_{1},\dots,h_{i}\in{\mathbb{Q}}^{K}
. This implies that
g\circ\phi
is a polynomial sequence with the same filtration as
g
. So
\theta_{2}:=\theta_{1}\circ\phi
is a degree-
s

{\mathbb{Q}}^{K}
-nilsequence and we are done.
∎

As an immediate consequence, we have the following.

Corollary 2.7
.

Let
G\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{\ell}
be a finitely generated subgroup. Then
\Nil_{s}(G)
is a conjugation-closed
{\mathbb{C}}
-algebra.

Proof.

Let
\psi_{1},\psi_{2}\in\Nil_{s}(G)
. By the definition of
\Nil_{s}(G)
and Proposition
2.6
, we can find a coordinate map
\phi:{\mathbb{Q}}^{K}\to\Span_{\mathbb{Q}}G
and degree-
s

{\mathbb{Q}}^{K}
-nilsequences
\theta_{1},\theta_{2}\colon{\mathbb{Q}}^{K}\to{\mathbb{C}}
such that
\theta_{i}|_{\phi^{-1}(G)}=\psi_{i}\circ\phi|_{\phi^{-1}(G)}
for
i\in\{1,2\}
. Then for any
c\in{\mathbb{C}}
, the functions
\overline{\psi_{1}}
,
\psi_{1}+c\psi_{2}
, and
\psi_{1}\psi_{2}
are elements of
\Nil_{s}(G)
corresponding to degree-
s

{\mathbb{Q}}^{K}
-nilsequences
\overline{\theta_{1}}
,
\theta_{1}+c\theta_{2}
, and
\theta_{1}\theta_{2}
. This proved the claim.
∎

The following notion will play a crucial part in the forthcoming definition of the factors.

Definition 2.8

(Dual functions)
.

Set
G=G_{1}\times G_{1}\times\cdots\times G_{s}\times G_{s}
, and let
\psi\in\Nil_{s}(G)
.
Let
\{0,1\}^{s}_{*}=\{0,1\}^{s}\setminus\{\underline{0}\}
and assume that
f_{\underline{\epsilon}}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
for
{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s}_{*}
.
We then call a function

(8)

{\mathcal{D}}_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}((f_{\underline{\epsilon}})_{\{0,1\}^{s}_{*}};\psi):=\E_{({\mathbf{m}}_{1},{\mathbf{m}}_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,{\mathbf{m}}_{s},{\mathbf{m}}_{s^{\prime}})\in G}\;\psi({\mathbf{m}}_{1},{\mathbf{m}}_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,{\mathbf{m}}_{s},{\mathbf{m}}_{s}^{\prime})\\
\cdot\prod_{{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s}_{*}}T^{{\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}_{1}^{\epsilon_{1}}\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}_{1}\right\rfloor}+\cdots+{\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}_{s}^{\epsilon_{s}}\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}_{s}\right\rfloor}}f_{\underline{\epsilon}}

a
level-
s
dual function of
f
along
G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}
.

In a number of cases, we simplify the notation:

•

if
f_{\underline{\epsilon}}={\mathcal{C}}^{|{\underline{\epsilon}}|}f
for some
f
, then we also denote the dual function as
{\mathcal{D}}_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}(f;\psi)
;

•

if
\psi=1
, then we write
{\mathcal{D}}_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}((f_{\underline{\epsilon}})_{\{0,1\}^{s}_{*}})
;

•

if
G_{1}=\cdots=G_{s}
, then we use the label
{\mathcal{D}}_{s,G_{1}}((f_{\underline{\epsilon}})_{\{0,1\}^{s}_{*}};\psi)
, and if all the groups equal
\langle{\mathbf{e}}_{j}\rangle
for some
j\in[\ell]
, then we further write
{\mathcal{D}}_{s,T_{j}}((f_{\underline{\epsilon}})_{\{0,1\}^{s}_{*}};\psi)
.

We also combine all the following conventions freely, e.g. denoting the dual function by
{\mathcal{D}}_{s,T_{j}}(f)
when
G_{1}=\cdots=G_{s}=\langle{\mathbf{e}}_{j}\rangle
,
f_{\underline{\epsilon}}={\mathcal{C}}^{|{\underline{\epsilon}}|}f
, and
\psi=1
.

Lemma 2.9
.

The limit (
8
) exists in
L^{2}(\mu)
. Moreover, the single limit along
G
can be replaced by an iterated limit along
G_{1}\times G_{1}
, …,
G_{s}\times G_{s}
.

Proof.

Let
\phi:{\mathbb{Q}}^{K}\to\Span_{\mathbb{Q}}G
be a coordinate map,
\widetilde{G}=\phi({\mathbb{Z}}^{K})
, and
E\subseteq\widetilde{G}
be a finite of size
|E|=|\widetilde{G}/G|
that contains 0 and satisfies
\widetilde{G}=G+E
. We first show that the average along
G
can be replaced by an average along
\widetilde{G}
so that later on, we can replace it by an average over
{\mathbb{Z}}^{K}
. Indeed, if
(I_{N})_{N}
is a Følner sequence on
G
, then
(I_{N}+E)_{N}
is a Følner sequence on
\widetilde{G}
, and so

\displaystyle\E_{{{\mathbf{m}}}\in I_{N}}\;\psi({\mathbf{m}})F({\mathbf{m}})=|E|\cdot\E_{{{\mathbf{m}}}\in I_{N}+E}\;\psi({\mathbf{m}})1_{G}({\mathbf{m}})F({\mathbf{m}})

for

\displaystyle F({\mathbf{m}})=\prod_{{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s}_{*}}T^{{\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}_{1}^{\epsilon_{1}}\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}_{1}\right\rfloor}+\cdots+{\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}_{s}^{\epsilon_{s}}\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}_{s}\right\rfloor}}f_{\underline{\epsilon}}.

By Fourier expanding
1_{G}\circ\phi=1_{\phi^{-1}(G)}
along the finite group
{\mathbb{Z}}^{K}/\phi^{-1}(G)
, we see that it is a degree-1
{\mathbb{Q}}^{K}
-nilsequence, and hence
1_{G}\in\Nil_{1}(\widetilde{G})
.
5
5

5

To illustrate this maneuver with an example, consider
G=\langle(2\alpha,0),(0,2\beta),(\alpha,\beta)\rangle
with the coordinate map
\phi:{\mathbb{Q}}^{2}\to\Span_{\mathbb{Q}}((\alpha,0),(0,\beta))
given by
\phi(x,y)=(x/\alpha,y/\beta)
. Then on
\widetilde{G}=\phi({\mathbb{Z}}^{2})
, the map
1_{G}(x,y)
equals the 1-step nilsequence
\frac{1}{2}\mathopen{}\left(1+e(\frac{x}{2\alpha}+\frac{y}{2\beta})\mathclose{}\right)
.

From this and Corollary
2.7
it follows that
\psi\cdot 1_{G}\in\Nil_{s}\widetilde{G}
.
This allows us to recast the original average over
G
as the average

(9)

\displaystyle|E|\cdot\E_{{{\underline{m}}}\in\phi^{-1}(I_{N}+E)}\;(\psi\cdot 1_{G})(\phi({\underline{m}}))\cdot F(\phi({\underline{m}}))

over
{\mathbb{Z}}^{K}
in which
\psi({\mathbf{m}})
is replaced by a
{\mathbb{Q}}^{K}
-nilsequence
(\psi\cdot 1_{G})\circ\phi
and iterates
T^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}_{i}\right\rfloor}}
inside
F
are replaced by expressions like
T^{{\left\lfloor\phi({\underline{m}}_{i})\right\rfloor}}=T^{{\lfloor{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1}m_{i,1}+\cdots+{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{K_{i}}m_{i,K_{i}}\rfloor}}
, where
G_{i}=\langle{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{K_{i}}\rangle
.

From then on, the proof of the
L^{2}(\mu)
convergence is completely analogous to the arguments in
[
38
, Section 3]
. The multiparameter variant of the suspension flow trick
[
38
, Theorem 3.2]
combined with the celebrated convergence result of Walsh
[
47
]
allows us to obtain the
L^{2}(\mu)
convergence of (
9
) whenever
\psi\cdot 1_{G}=1
.
To treat the general form of (
9
), it suffices to approximate the nilsequence
\psi\cdot 1_{G}
in uniform density with the special weights considered in
[
26
, Proposition 4.2]
.
6
6

6

The paper
[
26
]
defines nilsequences in terms “linear sequences”
\tau_{1}^{n_{1}}\cdots\tau_{K}^{n_{K}}
rather than general polynomial sequences, and so here we also implicitly use the argument from
[
42
, Section 2.11]
to convert the original “polynomial nilsequence” into a “linear nilsequence” as defined in
[
26
]
.

The claim then follows by arguments similar to those in
[
38
, Section 3]
.

The convergence of the iterated limit can be proved analogously, and the equality of a single and iterated limits can be established using the Fubini principle for ergodic averages
[
6
, Lemma 1.1]
much like in
[
16
, Lemma 3.1]
.
∎

Dual functions play a crucial role in the following definition of the closed space of functions “structured” with respect to the groups
G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}
.

Definition 2.10
.

Let
G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{\ell}
be finitely generated subgroups.
Given a factor
{\mathcal{Y}}\subseteq{\mathcal{X}}
, let
Z_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}^{+}({\mathcal{Y}})\subseteq L^{\infty}(\mu)
be the
L^{2}(\mu)
-closed linear span of functions of the form

{\mathcal{D}}_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}((f_{\underline{\epsilon}})_{\{0,1\}^{s}_{*}};\psi)
for
f_{\underline{\epsilon}}\in L^{\infty}({\mathcal{Y}},\mu)
and
\psi\in\Nil_{s}(G)

(where we recall that
G=G_{1}\times G_{1}\times\ldots\times G_{s}\times G_{s}
). If
{\mathcal{Y}}={\mathcal{X}}
, then we simply write
Z_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}^{+}({\mathcal{X}})=Z_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}^{+}
.

This is a good place to explain the presence of a nilsequence
\psi
in the definition (
8
) as well as the reason why we consider a factor associated with the seminorm
\lvert\!|\!|\cdot|\!|\!\rvert_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}^{+}
rather than
\lvert\!|\!|\cdot|\!|\!\rvert_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}
. To this end, consider the group
{G_{1}}=\langle(\alpha,\beta)\rangle\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
for some irrational
\alpha,\beta\in{\mathbb{R}}
. If we wanted to find an algebra
Z_{G_{1}}\subseteq L^{\infty}(\mu)
with the property (
7
), then it would have to be the closed linear span of functions of the form

\displaystyle{\mathcal{D}}_{G_{1}}(f)=\E_{m,m^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{Z}}}T_{1}^{{\lfloor\alpha m^{\prime}\rfloor}-{\lfloor\alpha m\rfloor}}T_{2}^{{\lfloor\beta m^{\prime}\rfloor}-{\lfloor\beta m\rfloor}}f

(with no
1
-step-nilsequence weight). However, the resulting vector space may not be an algebra (i.e. it may not be closed under multiplication). The reason is that the product
{\mathcal{D}}_{G_{1}}(f)\cdot{\mathcal{D}}_{G_{1}}(g)
equals

{\mathcal{D}}_{G_{1}}(f)\cdot{\mathcal{D}}_{G_{1}}(g)=\sum_{r_{1},r_{2}=-1}^{1}\E_{n,n^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\E_{m,m^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\psi_{r_{1},r_{2}}(m,m^{\prime},n,n^{\prime})\\
T_{1}^{{\lfloor\alpha m^{\prime}\rfloor}-{\lfloor\alpha m\rfloor}}T_{2}^{{\lfloor\beta m^{\prime}\rfloor}-{\lfloor\beta m\rfloor}}(f\cdot T_{1}^{{\lfloor\alpha n^{\prime}\rfloor}-{\lfloor\alpha n\rfloor}+r_{1}}T_{2}^{{\lfloor\beta n^{\prime}\rfloor}-{\lfloor\beta n\rfloor}+r_{2}}g)

for some nontrivial weights
\psi_{r_{1},r_{2}}
that take care of the error terms coming from comparing expressions like
{\lfloor\alpha(m+n)\rfloor}
with
{\lfloor\alpha m\rfloor}+{\lfloor\alpha n\rfloor}
. These weights, which can easily be seen to be degree-1 nilsequences, cannot in general be removed.

Once we allow for the presence of a degree-1 nilsequence weight in the definition of
{\mathcal{D}}_{G_{1}}(f)
, then we get an algebra
Z_{G_{1}}^{+}
corresponding to the seminorm
\lvert\!|\!|\cdot|\!|\!\rvert_{{G_{1}}}^{+}
rather than
\lvert\!|\!|\cdot|\!|\!\rvert_{{G_{1}}}
(see Lemma
2.11
and Proposition
2.13
below). More generally, if we want to find a factor associated with the seminorm
\lvert\!|\!|\cdot|\!|\!\rvert_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}^{+}
, then our definition of dual functions needs to include more complicated weights that turn out to be well-approximable by degree-
s
nilsequences.

Lemma 2.11
.

Let
G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{\ell}
be finitely generated subgroups. Then
Z_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}^{+}
is a closed
{\mathbb{C}}
-algebra invariant under
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell}
and closed under conjugation.

Proof.

Recall that
Z_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}^{+}\subseteq L^{\infty}(\mu)
is defined to be an
L^{2}(\mu)
-closed linear span. It is easy to observe from the definition that it is invariant under
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell}
and closed under conjugation. The only property that remains is that it is closed under multiplication. That is, given

\displaystyle D_{1}

\displaystyle=\E_{({\mathbf{m}},{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime})\in G}\;\psi_{1}({\mathbf{m}},{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime})\prod_{{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s}_{*}}T^{{\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}_{1}^{\epsilon_{1}}\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}_{1}\right\rfloor}+\cdots+{\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}_{s}^{\epsilon_{s}}\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}_{s}\right\rfloor}}f_{1,{\underline{\epsilon}}}

\displaystyle D_{2}

\displaystyle=\E_{({\mathbf{n}},{\mathbf{n}}^{\prime})\in G}\;\psi_{2}({\mathbf{n}},{\mathbf{n}}^{\prime})\prod_{{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s}_{*}}T^{{\lfloor{\mathbf{n}}_{1}^{\epsilon_{1}}\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{n}}_{1}\right\rfloor}+\cdots+{\lfloor{\mathbf{n}}_{s}^{\epsilon_{s}}\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{n}}_{s}\right\rfloor}}f_{2,{\underline{\epsilon}}},

we want to show that
D_{1}\cdot D_{2}
is also in
Z_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}^{+}
(above, we set
G=G_{1}\times G_{1}\times\cdots\times G_{s}\times G_{s}
,
({\mathbf{m}},{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime})=({\mathbf{m}}_{1},{\mathbf{m}}_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,{\mathbf{m}}_{s},{\mathbf{m}}_{s}^{\prime})
and
({\mathbf{n}},{\mathbf{n}}^{\prime})=({\mathbf{n}}_{1},{\mathbf{n}}_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,{\mathbf{n}}_{s},{\mathbf{n}}_{s}^{\prime})
, and we take
f_{j,{\underline{\epsilon}}}
and
\psi_{j}
to be 1-bounded).
We note first that the averages converge along any Følner sequence, and so
D_{2}
will converge to the same limit if we replace all
{\mathbf{n}}_{i}
and
{\mathbf{n}}_{i}^{\prime}
by
{\mathbf{m}}_{i}+{\mathbf{n}}_{i}
and
{\mathbf{m}}_{i}^{\prime}+{\mathbf{n}}_{i}^{\prime}
respectively for any
{\mathbf{n}}_{i},{\mathbf{n}}_{i}^{\prime}\in G_{i}
.
Multiplying the two limits together, we therefore get

D_{1}\cdot D_{2}=\sum_{{\mathbf{r}}\in\{-1,0,1\}^{\ell s}}\E_{({\mathbf{m}},{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime})\in G}\E_{({\mathbf{n}},{\mathbf{n}}^{\prime})\in G}\psi_{1}({\mathbf{m}},{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime})\psi_{2}({\mathbf{m}}+{\mathbf{n}},{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime}+{\mathbf{n}}^{\prime})1_{E_{\mathbf{r}}}({\mathbf{m}},{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime},{\mathbf{n}},{\mathbf{n}}^{\prime})\\
\prod_{{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s}_{*}}T^{{\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}_{1}^{\epsilon_{1}}\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}_{1}\right\rfloor}+\cdots+{\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}_{s}^{\epsilon_{s}}\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}_{s}\right\rfloor}}(f_{1,{\underline{\epsilon}}}\cdot T^{{\lfloor{\mathbf{n}}_{1}^{\epsilon_{1}}\rfloor}-{\lfloor{\mathbf{n}}_{1}\rfloor}+\cdots+{\lfloor{\mathbf{n}}_{s}^{\epsilon_{s}}\rfloor}-{\lfloor{\mathbf{n}}_{s}\rfloor}+{\underline{\epsilon}}\cdot{\mathbf{r}}}f_{2,{\underline{\epsilon}}}),

where
{\underline{\epsilon}}\cdot{\mathbf{r}}=\epsilon_{1}\cdot{\mathbf{r}}_{1}+\cdots+\epsilon_{s}\cdot{\mathbf{r}}_{s}
, and

E_{{\mathbf{r}}}=\{({\mathbf{m}},{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime},{\mathbf{n}},{\mathbf{n}}^{\prime})\in G\times G:\;{\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}_{i}^{\epsilon_{i}}+{\mathbf{n}}_{i}^{\epsilon_{i}}\rfloor}-{\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}_{i}+{\mathbf{n}}_{i}\rfloor}\\
={\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}_{i}^{\epsilon_{i}}\rfloor}-{\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}_{i}\rfloor}+{\lfloor{\mathbf{n}}_{i}^{\epsilon_{i}}\rfloor}-{\lfloor{\mathbf{n}}_{i}\rfloor}+\epsilon_{i}\cdot{\mathbf{r}}_{i}\;\textrm{for\; all}\;i,\epsilon_{i}\}.

Swapping the order of the averages along
({\mathbf{m}},{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime})
and
({\mathbf{n}},{\mathbf{n}}^{\prime})
by the Fubini principle for ergodic averages
[
6
, Lemma 1.1]
, the limit will follow if we can establish that the sequence

\displaystyle\psi_{{\mathbf{r}},{\mathbf{n}},{\mathbf{n}}^{\prime}}({\mathbf{m}},{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime})=\psi_{1}({\mathbf{m}},{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime})\psi_{2}({\mathbf{m}}+{\mathbf{n}},{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime}+{\mathbf{n}}^{\prime})1_{E_{\mathbf{r}}}({\mathbf{m}},{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime},{\mathbf{n}},{\mathbf{n}}^{\prime})

is in
\Nil_{s}(G)
. Since
\psi_{1},\psi_{2}\in\Nil_{s}(G)
by assumption,
the result will follow from Corollary
2.7
once we show that
({\mathbf{m}},{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime})\mapsto 1_{E_{\mathbf{r}}}({\mathbf{m}},{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime},{\mathbf{n}},{\mathbf{n}}^{\prime})\in\Nil_{1}(G)
.
Such a sequence is a product of sequences coming from the constraints

\displaystyle{\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}_{i}^{\epsilon_{i}}+{\mathbf{n}}_{i}^{\epsilon_{i}}\rfloor}-{\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}_{i}+{\mathbf{n}}_{i}\rfloor}={\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}_{i}^{\epsilon_{i}}\rfloor}-{\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}_{i}\rfloor}+{\lfloor{\mathbf{n}}_{i}^{\epsilon_{i}}\rfloor}-{\lfloor{\mathbf{n}}_{i}\rfloor}+\epsilon_{i}\cdot{\mathbf{r}}_{i},

and so it suffices to show that the indicator function of the set of
({\mathbf{m}}_{i},{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime}_{i})
satisfying such a constraint for fixed
i,\epsilon_{i},{\mathbf{n}},{\mathbf{n}}^{\prime}
is a nilsequence. A standard argument shows that such an indicator function takes the form
F_{{\mathbf{n}}_{i},{\mathbf{n}}_{i}^{\prime}}(\{{\mathbf{m}}_{i}\},\{{\mathbf{m}}_{i}^{\prime}\})
for some 1-bounded Riemann integrable function
F_{{\mathbf{n}}_{i},{\mathbf{n}}_{i}^{\prime}}:[0,1)^{2\ell}\to{\mathbb{R}}
, and hence it is indeed a degree-1 nilsequence.
∎

Using a standard identification between factors of a dynamical system and closed invariant subalgebras of its Hilbert space, we obtain the following corollary.

Corollary 2.12
.

Let
G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{\ell}
be finitely generated subgroups. There exists a factor
{\mathcal{Z}}_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}^{+}\subseteq{\mathcal{X}}
such that
L^{\infty}({\mathcal{Z}}_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}^{+})=Z_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}^{+}
.

Whenever
G_{i}=\langle{\mathbf{v}}_{i}\rangle
, we also write
{\mathcal{Z}}^{+}_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}={\mathcal{Z}}^{+}_{{\mathbf{v}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{v}}_{s}}
, and if
G_{1}=\cdots=G_{s}=G
, we additionally abbreviate
{\mathcal{Z}}^{+}_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}={\mathcal{Z}}^{+}_{G^{\times s}}
.

The next result relates the factor
{\mathcal{Z}}_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}^{+}
to the seminorm
\lvert\!|\!|\cdot|\!|\!\rvert_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}^{+}
.

Proposition 2.13

(Weak structure theorem for
\lvert\!|\!|\cdot|\!|\!\rvert_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}^{+}
)
.

Let
G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{\ell}
be finitely generated subgroups. For any
f\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
, the relationship (
6
) holds.

Before we prove Proposition
2.13
, we need to understand the structure of multicorrelation sequences appearing in the definition of generalized box seminorms.

Definition 2.14

(Nullsequences on
G
)
.

Let
G\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{\ell}
be a finitely generated subgroup. For
\varepsilon>0
, we call
\psi:G\to{\mathbb{C}}
an
\varepsilon
-nullsequence on
G
if

\displaystyle\oE_{{\mathbf{m}}\in G}|\psi({\mathbf{m}})|^{2}\leq\varepsilon.

Proposition 2.15

(Decomposition of multicorrelation sequences)
.

Let
G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{\ell}
be finitely generated subgroups and set
G=G_{1}\times G_{1}\times\cdots\times G_{s}\times G_{s}
. For
({\mathbf{m}},{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime})\in G
and 1-bounded functions
f_{\underline{\epsilon}}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
, define

\displaystyle\psi({\mathbf{m}},{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime})=\int\prod_{{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s}}T^{{\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}_{1}^{\epsilon_{1}}\rfloor}+\cdots+{\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}_{s}^{\epsilon_{s}}\rfloor}}f_{\underline{\epsilon}}\;d\mu.

Then for every
\varepsilon>0
, we can decompose
\psi=\psi_{1}+\psi_{2}
, where
\psi_{1}
is a 1-bounded degree-
s
nilsequence on
G
and
\psi_{2}
is an
\varepsilon
-nullsequence on
G
.

Proof.

Proposition
2.15
can be proved via a straightforward but tedious adaptation of the methods of
[
23
,
26
,
38
]
.

Let
\phi:{\mathbb{Q}}^{K}\to\Span_{\mathbb{Q}}G
be a coordinate map and
\widetilde{G}=\phi({\mathbb{Z}}^{K})
.
We then define
\widetilde{\psi}:\widetilde{G}\to{\mathbb{C}}
by the same formula as
\psi
except we let
{\mathbf{m}}
take values in
\widetilde{G}
rather than
G
. We first want to show the claimed decomposition for
\widetilde{\psi}
and then specialize to
\psi
.

By a natural multiparameter extension of
[
38
, Theorem 2.1]
, it suffices to show that the sequence
\widetilde{\psi}\circ\phi|_{{\mathbb{Z}}^{K}}:{\mathbb{Z}}^{K}\to{\mathbb{C}}
satisfies two conditions:

(i)

it is
(s+1)
-weak-anti-uniform
in that for every uniformly bounded
b:{\mathbb{Z}}^{K}\to{\mathbb{C}}
, the average

\displaystyle\oE_{{\underline{m}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{K}}\widetilde{\psi}\circ\phi({\underline{m}})b({\underline{m}})

is quantitatively controlled by a degree-
(s+1)
uniformity seminorm on
{\mathbb{Z}}^{K}
(in the sense of
[
38
]
);

(ii)

it is
(s+1)
-regular
in that the uniform limit

\displaystyle\E_{{\underline{m}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{K}}\widetilde{\psi}\circ\phi({\underline{m}})b({\underline{m}})

exists for any
s
-step nilsequence
b:{\mathbb{Z}}^{K}\to{\mathbb{C}}
.

The condition of
(s+1)
-weak-anti-uniformity follows by combining a suspension flow trick
[
38
, Theorem 4.1]
(which allows us to remove integer parts from expressions like
T^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}\right\rfloor}}
) with a standard van der Corput argument (which can be found e.g. at the end of the proof of
[
26
, Theorem 2.6]
). Similarly,
(s+1)
-regularity can be deduced by combining
[
38
, Theorem 4.1]
with
[
26
, Proposition 2.1]
. For every
\varepsilon>0
, the natural multiparameter extension of
[
38
, Theorem 2.1]
then allows us to decompose
\widetilde{\psi}\circ\phi|_{{\mathbb{Z}}^{K}}=\psi_{1}^{\prime}+\psi_{2}^{\prime}
, where
\psi_{1}^{\prime}
is a degree-
s
nilsequence
7
7

7

A priori,
\psi_{1}^{\prime}
is only defined on
{\mathbb{Z}}^{K}
, but since the underlying nilpotent Lie group is connected and simply-connected, we can lift
\psi_{1}^{\prime}
to
{\mathbb{Q}}^{K}
(in fact, to all of
{\mathbb{R}}^{K}
).
on
{\mathbb{Q}}^{K}
and
\psi_{2}^{\prime}
is an
\varepsilon
-nullsequence on
{\mathbb{Z}}^{K}
. This gives the claimed result for
\widetilde{\psi}.

We now specialize to
G
, defining
\psi_{j}=\psi_{j}^{\prime}\circ\phi^{-1}|_{\phi^{-1}(G)}:G\to{\mathbb{C}}
for
j=1,2
so that
\psi=\psi_{1}+\psi_{2}
. It is immediate from the definition that the sequence
\psi_{1}
is a degree-
s
nilsequence on
G
. It is however less immediate that
\psi_{2}
defines a nullsequence on
G
. What we get from our assumption is that

\displaystyle\oE_{{\mathbf{m}}\in\widetilde{G}}\mathopen{}\left|\psi_{2}^{\prime}\circ\phi^{-1}({\mathbf{m}})\mathclose{}\right|^{2}=\oE_{{\underline{m}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{K}}\mathopen{}\left|\psi_{2}^{\prime}({\underline{m}})\mathclose{}\right|^{2}\leq\varepsilon,

whereas what we want to show is the smallness of

\displaystyle\oE_{{\mathbf{m}}\in{G}}\mathopen{}\left|\psi_{2}({\mathbf{m}})\mathclose{}\right|^{2}=\oE_{{\mathbf{m}}\in{G}}\mathopen{}\left|\psi_{2}^{\prime}\circ\phi^{-1}({\mathbf{m}})\mathclose{}\right|^{2}.

To see that the latter average is still small, let
(I_{N})_{N}
be a Følner sequence on
G
and
E\subseteq\widetilde{G}
be a finite of size
|E|=|\widetilde{G}/G|
that contains 0 and satisfies
\widetilde{G}=G+E
. Then
(I_{N}+E)_{N}
is a Følner sequence on
\widetilde{G}
. The nonnegativity of the summands implies that

\displaystyle\E_{{\mathbf{m}}\in I_{N}}\mathopen{}\left|\psi_{2}^{\prime}\circ\phi^{-1}({\mathbf{m}})\mathclose{}\right|^{2}\leq\frac{1}{|I_{N}|}\sum_{{\mathbf{m}}\in I_{N}+E}\mathopen{}\left|\psi_{2}^{\prime}\circ\phi^{-1}({\mathbf{m}})\mathclose{}\right|^{2}\leq|E|\cdot\E_{{\mathbf{m}}\in I_{N}+E}\mathopen{}\left|\psi_{2}^{\prime}\circ\phi^{-1}({\mathbf{m}})\mathclose{}\right|^{2}+o(1),

and hence taking the limit as
N\to\infty
and supremum over all Følner sequences, we deduce that

\displaystyle\oE_{{\mathbf{m}}\in{G}}\mathopen{}\left|\psi_{2}({\mathbf{m}})\mathclose{}\right|^{2}\leq|E|\cdot\oE_{{\mathbf{m}}\in\widetilde{G}}\mathopen{}\left|\psi_{2}^{\prime}\circ\phi^{-1}({\mathbf{m}})\mathclose{}\right|^{2}\leq|E|\cdot\varepsilon.

Hence
\psi_{2}
is a
|E|\varepsilon
-nullsequence on
G
. Since
\varepsilon>0
was arbitrary, the result follows.
∎

Proof of Proposition
2.13
.

We show that
\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}^{+}=0
if and only if
f
is orthogonal to dual functions
{\mathcal{D}}_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}((f_{\underline{\epsilon}})_{\{0,1\}^{s}_{*}};\psi)
with the functions
f_{\underline{\epsilon}}
and degree-
s
nilsequence being 1-bounded. The invariance of the measure gives

\int f\cdot{\mathcal{D}}_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}((f_{\underline{\epsilon}})_{\{0,1\}^{s}_{*}};\psi)\;d\mu\\
=\E_{{\mathbf{m}}_{1},{\mathbf{m}}_{1}^{\prime}\in G_{1}}\cdots\E_{{\mathbf{m}}_{s},{\mathbf{m}}_{s}^{\prime}\in G_{s}}\psi({\mathbf{m}},{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime})\cdot\int\prod_{{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s}}T^{{\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}_{1}^{\epsilon_{1}}\rfloor}+\cdots+{\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}_{s}^{\epsilon_{s}}\rfloor}}f_{\underline{\epsilon}}\,d\mu,

where
f_{\underline{0}}=f
.
Using the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, we can remove the nilsequence and bound

\mathopen{}\left|\int f\cdot{\mathcal{D}}_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}((f_{\underline{\epsilon}})_{\{0,1\}^{s}_{*}};\psi)\;d\mu\mathclose{}\right|^{2}\\
\leq\E_{{\mathbf{m}}_{1},{\mathbf{m}}_{1}^{\prime}\in G_{1}}\cdots\E_{{\mathbf{m}}_{s},{\mathbf{m}}_{s}^{\prime}\in G_{s}}\mathopen{}\left|\int\prod_{{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s}}T^{{\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}_{1}^{\epsilon_{1}}\rfloor}+\cdots+{\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}_{s}^{\epsilon_{s}}\rfloor}}f_{\underline{\epsilon}}\;d\mu\mathclose{}\right|^{2}.

By the Gowers-Cauchy-Schwarz inequality (e.g.
[
16
, Lemma 3.3]
), the last expression is bounded by
(\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}^{+})^{1/2^{s}}
. In particular, if the seminorm is 0, then
f
is orthogonal to the function
{\mathcal{D}}_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}((f_{\underline{\epsilon}})_{\{0,1\}^{s}_{*}};\psi)
.

In the direction covered so far,
\psi({\mathbf{m}},{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime})
could be any bounded weight; its nilsequence structure will be used in the converse direction. Expanding the definition of
\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}^{+}
, we get

\displaystyle(\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}^{+})^{2^{s+1}}=\int f\cdot{\mathcal{D}}_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}(f;\psi)\;d\mu

for

\displaystyle\psi({\mathbf{m}},{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime})=\int\prod_{{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s}}{\mathcal{C}}^{|{\underline{\epsilon}}|}T^{{\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}_{1}^{\epsilon_{1}}\rfloor}+\cdots+{\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}_{s}^{\epsilon_{s}}\rfloor}}\overline{f}\,d\mu.

Fix
\varepsilon>0
, and let
\psi_{\varepsilon}
be a 1-bounded degree-
s
nilsequence coming from Proposition
2.15
such that

\displaystyle\oE_{({\mathbf{m}},{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime})\in G}\mathopen{}\left|\psi({\mathbf{m}},{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime})-\psi_{\varepsilon}({\mathbf{m}},{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime})\mathclose{}\right|\leq\varepsilon^{2^{s+1}}.

Then (assuming
f
is 1-bounded, which we may)

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\left|(\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}^{+})^{2^{s+1}}-\int f\cdot{\mathcal{D}}_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}(f;\psi_{\varepsilon})\;d\mu\mathclose{}\right|\leq\oE_{({\mathbf{m}},{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime})\in G}\mathopen{}\left|\psi({\mathbf{m}},{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime})-\psi_{\varepsilon}({\mathbf{m}},{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime})\mathclose{}\right|\leq\varepsilon^{2^{s+1}}.

The orthogonality of
f
to
{\mathcal{D}}_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}(f;\psi_{\varepsilon})
gives the upper bound
\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}^{+}\leq\varepsilon
, and the result follows by letting
\varepsilon\to 0
.
∎

As a consequence of relevant properties of seminorms (see Lemma
2.1
), we obtain the following properties of factors.

Lemma 2.16

(Basic properties of factors)
.

Let
G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{\ell}
be finitely generated subgroups. Then:

(i)

(Symmetry) For any permutation
\sigma:[s]\to[s]
, we have

{\mathcal{Z}}_{G_{\sigma(1)},\ldots,G_{\sigma(s)}}^{+}={\mathcal{Z}}_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}^{+};

(ii)

(Monotonicity) For any finitely generated
G_{s+1}\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{\ell}
, we have

{\mathcal{Z}}_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}^{+}\subseteq{\mathcal{Z}}_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s+1}}^{+};

(iii)

(Subgroup property) If
G^{\prime}_{i}\subseteq G_{i}
for all
i\in[s]
, then

{\mathcal{Z}}_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}^{+}\subseteq{\mathcal{Z}}_{G_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,G_{s}^{\prime}}^{+},

with equality whenever
G_{i}/G_{i}^{\prime}
has finite index for all
i\in[s]
;

(iv)

(Rescaling property) If for every
i\in[s]
we have

\displaystyle G_{i}=\langle{\mathbf{g}}_{i1},\ldots,{\mathbf{g}}_{iK_{i}}\rangle\quad\textrm{and}\quad G^{\prime}_{i}=\langle\alpha_{i1}{\mathbf{g}}_{i1},\ldots,\alpha_{iK_{i}}{\mathbf{g}}_{iK_{i}}\rangle

for some
K_{i}\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
\alpha_{ij}\neq 0
, then

\displaystyle{\mathcal{Z}}^{+}_{G^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,G^{\prime}_{s}}={\mathcal{Z}}^{+}_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}.

3.
Relative concatenation

Having defined generalized box factors and established their various properties, we now show that factors along smaller groups can be merged (“concatenated”) into (higher-degree) factors along larger groups. Once again, we fix a system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
throughout this section. The main result of this section is as follows.

Theorem 3.1

(Relative concatenation)
.

Let
s\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
,
d,d^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}
, and

G_{1},\ldots,G_{s},H_{1},\ldots,H_{d},H^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,H^{\prime}_{d^{\prime}}\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{\ell}

be finitely generated subgroups. Then the following statements hold:

(i)

We have

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{+}_{G_{1},\dots,G_{s},H_{1},\dots,H_{d}}\cap\mathcal{Z}^{+}_{G_{1},\dots,G_{s},H^{\prime}_{1},\dots,H^{\prime}_{d^{\prime}}}\subseteq\mathcal{Z}^{+}_{G_{1},\dots,G_{s},(H_{i}+H^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}})_{i\in[d],i^{\prime}\in[d^{\prime}]}}.

(ii)

If moreover there exists a subgroup
H\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{\ell}
such that
H_{i}+H^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}=H
for all
i\in[d]
and
i^{\prime}\in[d^{\prime}]
, then

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{+}_{G_{1},\dots,G_{s},H_{1},\dots,H_{d}}\cap\mathcal{Z}^{+}_{G_{1},\dots,G_{s},H^{\prime}_{1},\dots,H^{\prime}_{d^{\prime}}}\subseteq{\mathcal{Z}}^{+}_{G_{1},\dots,G_{s},H^{\times(d+d^{\prime}-1)}},

where we recall that
H^{\times d}
denotes
d
-copies of
H
.

When
s=0
and
H_{i},H^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}\subseteq{\mathbb{Z}}^{\ell}
, Theorem
3.1
has been established by Tao and Ziegler in
[
45
, Theorems 1.15 and 1.16]
;
8
8

8

Tao-Ziegler’s results apply more broadly to box factors along subgroups that are sums of finitely generated and profinite groups. For the second statement, their result additionally assumes that
H_{1}=\cdots=H_{d}
and
H^{\prime}_{1}=\cdots=H_{d^{\prime}}^{\prime}
.
it is their influential paper that introduced the idea of concatenating factors and seminorms to ergodic theory, additive combinatorics, and analytic number theory. We extend Tao-Ziegler’s result in two ways: by relativizing it (i.e. allowing
s>0
groups that do not get concatenated), and by extending it to generalized box factors.

We shall use the following consequence of Theorem
3.1
, which generalizes
[
45
, Corollaries 1.20 and 1.21]
, and which can be deduced from Theorem
3.1
in the same way in which
[
45
, Corollaries 1.20 and 1.21]
are derived from
[
45
, Theorems 1.15 and 1.16]
.

Corollary 3.2
.

Let
s\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
,
d,d^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}
, and

G_{1},\ldots,G_{s},H_{1},\ldots,H_{d},H^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,H^{\prime}_{d^{\prime}}\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{\ell}

be finitely generated subgroups. Let
f\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
. Then the following statements hold:

(i)

If
\E(f|\mathcal{Z}^{+}_{G_{1},\dots,G_{s},(H_{i}+H^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}})_{i\in[d],i^{\prime}\in[d^{\prime}]}})=0
, then we can decompose
f=g_{1}+g_{2}
for some
g_{i}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
satisfying

\displaystyle\E(g_{1}|\mathcal{Z}^{+}_{G_{1},\dots,G_{s},H_{1},\dots,H_{d}})=0\quad\textrm{and}\quad\E(g_{2}|\mathcal{Z}^{+}_{G_{1},\dots,G_{s},H^{\prime}_{1},\dots,H^{\prime}_{d^{\prime}}})=0.

(ii)

If moreover there exists a subgroup
H\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{\ell}
such that
H_{i}+H^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}=H
for all
i\in[d]
and
i^{\prime}\in[d^{\prime}]
, and if
\E(f|{\mathcal{Z}}^{+}_{G_{1},\dots,G_{s},H^{\times(d+d^{\prime}-1)}})=0
, then we can decompose
f=g_{1}+g_{2}
for some
g_{i}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
satisfying

\displaystyle\E(g_{1}|\mathcal{Z}^{+}_{G_{1},\dots,G_{s},H_{1},\dots,H_{d}})=0\quad\textrm{and}\quad\E(g_{2}|\mathcal{Z}^{+}_{G_{1},\dots,G_{s},H^{\prime}_{1},\dots,H^{\prime}_{d^{\prime}}})=0.

If we specialize to subgroups of
{\mathbb{Z}}^{\ell}
in Theorem
3.1
and Corollary
3.2
, the factors
{\mathcal{Z}}^{+}
can be replaced by the more classical factors
{\mathcal{Z}}
, defined e.g. in
[
35
,
45
]
and satisfying the property (
7
) (recall that in this case
Z_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}({\mathcal{Y}})\subseteq L^{\infty}(\mu)
is defined to be the
L^{2}(\mu)
-closed linear span of functions of the form

{\mathcal{D}}_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}((f_{\underline{\epsilon}})_{\{0,1\}^{s}_{*}})
for
f_{\underline{\epsilon}}\in L^{\infty}({\mathcal{Y}},\mu)
). The proof in this case is almost identical; the only difference is that since we only need to consider dual functions with nilsequences
\psi=1
, we can control expressions like
\int g\cdot{\mathcal{D}}_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}f\;d\mu
by
\lvert\!|\!|g|\!|\!\rvert_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}
rather than
\lvert\!|\!|g|\!|\!\rvert_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}^{+}
. We leave the details to the interested readers.

For general finitely generated subgroups
G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{\ell}
, such factors in general do not exist; see the discussions at the beginning of Section
2.2
and below Definition
2.10
.

3.1.
Fundamental concatenation theorem

Theorem
3.1
will be deduced by iterating the following result whose proof is the main subject of this subsection.

Theorem 3.3

(Fundamental concatenation theorem)
.

Let
G_{1},\ldots,G_{s},G_{s}^{\prime}\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{\ell}
be finitely generated subgroups.
Then

\mathcal{Z}^{+}_{G_{1},\dots,G_{s-1},G_{s}}\cap\mathcal{Z}^{+}_{G_{1},\dots,G_{s-1},G^{\prime}_{s}}=\mathcal{Z}^{+}_{G_{1},\dots,G_{s-1},G_{s}+G^{\prime}_{s}}.

Before we supply the proof of Theorem
3.3
, we state several lemmas that will show up in the proof. The first one is a consequence of Proposition
2.13
and follows from the same proof as
[
45
, Corollary 2.5]
.

Lemma 3.4

(Restriction to a factor)
.

Let
G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{\ell}
be finitely generated subgroups. For any factor
{\mathcal{Y}}\subseteq{\mathcal{X}}
, we have

\displaystyle{\mathcal{Y}}\cap{\mathcal{Z}}^{+}_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}({\mathcal{X}})={\mathcal{Z}}^{+}_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}({\mathcal{Y}}).

Before we state the second lemma, let
{\mathcal{B}}_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}
be the closed convex hull of dual functions
{\mathcal{D}}_{G_{1},\dots,G_{s}}((f_{{\underline{\epsilon}}})_{{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s}_{\ast}};\psi)
for 1-bounded functions
f_{\underline{\epsilon}}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
and 1-bounded degree-
s
nilsequences
\psi
on
G=G_{1}\times G_{1}\times\cdots\times G_{s}\times G_{s}
.

Lemma 3.5

(Approximate multiplicative closedness of
{\mathcal{B}}_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}
)
.

Let
G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{\ell}
be finitely generated subgroups.
If
D_{1},D_{2}\in{\mathcal{B}}_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}
, then
D_{1}\cdot D_{2}\in 3^{\ell s}\cdot{\mathcal{B}}_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}
.

Proof.

By convexity, it suffices to prove the result when
D_{1},D_{2}
are themselves dual functions
{\mathcal{D}}_{G_{1},\dots,G_{s}}((f_{{\underline{\epsilon}}})_{{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s}_{\ast}};\psi)
for 1-bounded functions
f_{\underline{\epsilon}}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
and 1-bounded
\psi\in\Nil_{s}(G)
for
G=G_{1}\times G_{1}\times\cdots\times G_{s}\times G_{s}
. By inspecting the proof of Lemma
2.11
, we see that
D_{1}\cdot D_{2}
is a sum of at most
3^{\ell s}
elements in
{\mathcal{B}}_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}
, corresponding to
3^{\ell s}
possible values of
{\mathbf{r}}\in\{-1,0,1\}^{\ell s}
.
∎

Lemma 3.6

(Anti-uniformity of
{\mathcal{B}}_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}
)
.

Let
G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{\ell}
be finitely generated subgroups. If
f\in{\mathcal{B}}_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}
,
g\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
, then
\mathopen{}\left|\int f\cdot g\;d\mu\mathclose{}\right|\leq\lvert\!|\!|g|\!|\!\rvert_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}^{+}
.

Proof.

By convexity, it suffices to consider the case when
f
is a dual function, in which case the result follows from the Gowers-Cauchy-Schwarz inequality much like in the proof of Proposition
2.13
.
∎

We are now ready to prove Theorem
3.3
.

Proof of Theorem
3.3
.

The converse inclusion is a straightforward consequence of the monotonicity property of factors (Lemma
2.16
(ii)). For the forward inclusion, it suffices to show via Proposition
2.13
that any

\displaystyle f\in L^{\infty}(\mathcal{Z}^{+}_{G_{1},\dots,G_{s-1},G_{s}}\cap\mathcal{Z}^{+}_{G_{1},\dots,G_{s-1},G^{\prime}_{s}})

is orthogonal to
g\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
with
\lvert\!|\!|g|\!|\!\rvert^{+}_{G_{1},\dots,G_{s-1},G_{s}+G_{s}^{\prime}}=0
. Indeed, if the latter statement holds, then

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\int f\cdot(\bar{f}-\mathbb{E}(\bar{f}|{\mathcal{Z}}^{+}_{G_{1},\dots,G_{s-1},G_{s}+G_{s}^{\prime}}))\ d\mu

\displaystyle=\left\|f\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{2}-\left\|\mathbb{E}(f|{\mathcal{Z}}^{+}_{G_{1},\dots,G_{s-1},G_{s}+G_{s}^{\prime}})\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{2},

and so
f=\mathbb{E}(f|{\mathcal{Z}}^{+}_{G_{1},\dots,G_{s-1},G_{s}+G_{s}^{\prime}})\in Z^{+}_{G_{1},\dots,G_{s-1},G_{s}+G_{s}^{\prime}}
.

By an
L^{2}(\mu)
approximation argument, it suffices to consider

f={\mathcal{D}}_{G_{1},\dots,G_{s-1},G_{s}}((f_{{\underline{\epsilon}}})_{{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s}_{\ast}};\psi)

for 1-bounded functions
f_{\underline{\epsilon}}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
and
\psi\in\Nil_{s}(G)
for
G=G_{1}\times G_{1}\times\cdots\times G_{s}\times G_{s}
. Since the nilsequence
\psi
can be approximated in

\left\|v\right\|_{\ell^{2}(G)}:=\Bigl(\oE_{{\mathbf{m}}\in G}|v({\mathbf{m}})|^{2}\Bigr)^{1/2}

by a linear combination of nilsequences

\displaystyle\psi_{s}({\mathbf{m}}_{s},{\mathbf{m}}_{s}^{\prime})\psi_{s-1}({\mathbf{m}}_{1},{\mathbf{m}}_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,{\mathbf{m}}_{s-1},{\mathbf{m}}_{s-1}^{\prime})

(where
{\mathbf{m}}_{i},{\mathbf{m}}_{i}^{\prime}\in G_{i}
),
we can assume that
\psi
takes such form.

Applying Lemma
3.4
with
{\mathcal{Y}}=\mathcal{Z}^{+}_{G_{1},\dots,G_{s-1},G^{\prime}_{s}}
,
we can take every
f_{\varepsilon}
to be
\mathcal{Z}^{+}_{G_{1},\dots,G_{s-1},G^{\prime}_{s}}
-measurable.
A similar approximation argument as before allows us to take each
f_{\underline{\epsilon}}
to equal
