f_{{\underline{\epsilon}}}=\mathcal{D}_{G_{1},\dots,G_{s-1},G_{s}^{\prime}}((f_{{\underline{\epsilon}},{\underline{\epsilon}}^{\prime}})_{{\underline{\epsilon}}^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}^{s}_{\ast}};\psi_{{\underline{\epsilon}}})
for some
\mathcal{Z}^{+}_{G_{1},\dots,G_{s-1},G^{\prime}_{s}}
-measurable functions and nilsequences
\psi_{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\Nil_{s}(G)
, all of which can be assumed to be 1-bounded.

Inspecting the definition of dual functions, we observe that
f
can be recast as

f=\E_{{\mathbf{m}}_{s},{\mathbf{m}}_{s}^{\prime}\in G_{s}}\psi_{s}({\mathbf{m}}_{s},{\mathbf{m}}_{s}^{\prime})\cdot T^{{\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}_{s}^{\prime}\rfloor}-{\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}_{s}\rfloor}}f_{\underline{0}1}\\
{\mathcal{D}}_{G_{1},\dots,G_{s-1}}((f_{{\underline{\epsilon}}0}\cdot T^{{\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}_{s}^{\prime}\rfloor}-{\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}_{s}\rfloor}}f_{{\underline{\epsilon}}1})_{{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s-1}_{\ast}};\psi_{s-1}).

Arguing similarly, we can recast
f_{\underline{0}1}
as

f_{\underline{0}1}=\E_{{\mathbf{n}}_{s},{\mathbf{n}}_{s}^{\prime}\in G_{s}^{\prime}}\psi_{\underline{0}1,s}({\mathbf{n}}_{s},{\mathbf{n}}_{s}^{\prime})\cdot T^{{\lfloor{\mathbf{n}}_{s}^{\prime}\rfloor}-{\lfloor{\mathbf{n}}_{s}\rfloor}}f_{\underline{0}1,\underline{0}1}\\
{\mathcal{D}}_{G_{1},\dots,G_{s-1}}((f_{\underline{0}1,{\underline{\epsilon}}0}\cdot T^{{\lfloor{\mathbf{n}}_{s}^{\prime}\rfloor}-{\lfloor{\mathbf{n}}_{s}\rfloor}}f_{\underline{0}1,{\underline{\epsilon}}1})_{{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s-1}_{\ast}};\psi_{\underline{0}1,s-1}).

Plugging in the formula for
f_{\underline{0}1}
into the formula for
f
and setting

D_{{\mathbf{m}}_{s},{\mathbf{m}}_{s}^{\prime},{\mathbf{n}}_{s},{\mathbf{n}}_{s}^{\prime}}=\psi_{s}({\mathbf{m}}_{s},{\mathbf{m}}_{s}^{\prime})\cdot{\mathcal{D}}_{G_{1},\dots,G_{s-1}}((f_{{\underline{\epsilon}}0}\cdot T^{{\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}_{s}^{\prime}\rfloor}-{\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}_{s}\rfloor}}f_{{\underline{\epsilon}}1})_{{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s-1}_{\ast}};\psi_{s-1})\\
\psi_{\underline{0}1,s}({\mathbf{n}}_{s},{\mathbf{n}}_{s}^{\prime})\cdot T^{{\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}_{s}^{\prime}\rfloor}-{\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}_{s}\rfloor}}{\mathcal{D}}_{G_{1},\dots,G_{s-1}}((f_{\underline{0}1,{\underline{\epsilon}}0}\cdot T^{{\lfloor{\mathbf{n}}_{s}^{\prime}\rfloor}-{\lfloor{\mathbf{n}}_{s}\rfloor}}f_{\underline{0}1,{\underline{\epsilon}}1})_{{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s-1}_{\ast}};\psi_{\underline{0}1,s-1}),

we deduce that

\displaystyle f=\E_{{\mathbf{m}}_{s},{\mathbf{m}}_{s}^{\prime}\in G_{s}}\E_{{\mathbf{n}}_{s},{\mathbf{n}}_{s}^{\prime}\in G_{s}^{\prime}}T^{{\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}_{s}^{\prime}\rfloor}+{\lfloor{\mathbf{n}}_{s}^{\prime}\rfloor}-{\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}_{s}\rfloor}-{\lfloor{\mathbf{n}}_{s}\rfloor}}f_{\underline{0}1,\underline{0}1}\cdot D_{{\mathbf{m}}_{s},{\mathbf{m}}_{s}^{\prime},{\mathbf{n}}_{s},{\mathbf{n}}_{s}^{\prime}}.

By incorporating emerging error terms into
D_{{\mathbf{m}}_{s},{\mathbf{m}}_{s}^{\prime},{\mathbf{n}}_{s},{\mathbf{n}}_{s}^{\prime}}
, we can reduce to the case where
f
equals

\displaystyle f=\E_{{\mathbf{m}}_{s},{\mathbf{m}}_{s}^{\prime}\in G_{s}}\E_{{\mathbf{n}}_{s},{\mathbf{n}}_{s}^{\prime}\in G_{s}^{\prime}}T^{{\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}_{s}^{\prime}+{\mathbf{n}}_{s}^{\prime}\rfloor}-{\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}_{s}+{\mathbf{n}}_{s}\rfloor}}f_{\underline{0}1,\underline{0}1}\cdot D_{{\mathbf{m}}_{s},{\mathbf{m}}_{s}^{\prime},{\mathbf{n}}_{s},{\mathbf{n}}_{s}^{\prime}}.

The point of all these maneuvers is that we have by now expressed
f
as (essentially) an ergodic average along
G_{s}+G_{s}^{\prime}
twisted by 1-bounded
{\mathcal{Z}}^{+}_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s-1}}
-measurable functions
D_{{\mathbf{m}}_{s},{\mathbf{m}}_{s}^{\prime},{\mathbf{n}}_{s},{\mathbf{n}}_{s}^{\prime}}
. By removing the dual function in a more or less standard manner, we will be able to control
\int f\cdot g\;d\mu
by
\lvert\!|\!|g|\!|\!\rvert^{+}_{G_{1},\dots,G_{s-1},G_{s}+G_{s}^{\prime}}
, and this will conclude the proof of the forward direction.

Using the invariance of measure and the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, we can bound

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\left|\int f\cdot g\;d\mu\mathclose{}\right|

\displaystyle=\mathopen{}\left|\int f_{\underline{0}1,\underline{0}1}\cdot\E_{{\mathbf{m}}_{s},{\mathbf{m}}_{s}^{\prime}\in G_{s}}\E_{{\mathbf{n}}_{s},{\mathbf{n}}_{s}^{\prime}\in G_{s}^{\prime}}T^{{\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}_{s}+{\mathbf{n}}_{s}\rfloor}-{\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}_{s}^{\prime}+{\mathbf{n}}_{s}^{\prime}\rfloor}}(g\cdot D_{{\mathbf{m}}_{s},{\mathbf{m}}_{s}^{\prime},{\mathbf{n}}_{s},{\mathbf{n}}_{s}^{\prime}})\;d\mu\mathclose{}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\E_{{\mathbf{m}}_{s},{\mathbf{m}}_{s}^{\prime}\in G_{s}}\E_{{\mathbf{n}}_{s},{\mathbf{n}}_{s}^{\prime}\in G_{s}^{\prime}}T^{{\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}_{s}+{\mathbf{n}}_{s}\rfloor}-{\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}_{s}^{\prime}+{\mathbf{n}}_{s}^{\prime}\rfloor}}(g\cdot D_{{\mathbf{m}}_{s},{\mathbf{m}}_{s}^{\prime},{\mathbf{n}}_{s},{\mathbf{n}}_{s}^{\prime}})\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}.

Applying the van der Corput lemma (e.g.
[
18
, Lemma 2.1]
), composing the resulting integral with
T^{{\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}_{s}^{\prime}+{\mathbf{n}}_{s}^{\prime}\rfloor}-{\lfloor{\mathbf{m}}_{s}+{\mathbf{n}}_{s}\rfloor}}
, handling error terms, and then pigeonholing in
{\mathbf{m}}_{s},{\mathbf{m}}_{s}^{\prime},{\mathbf{n}}_{s},{\mathbf{n}}_{s}^{\prime}
, we deduce that the left-hand side vanishes whenever

(10)

\displaystyle\E_{{\mathbf{h}}_{s},{\mathbf{h}}_{s}^{\prime}\in G_{s}}\E_{{\mathbf{k}}_{s},{\mathbf{k}}_{s}^{\prime}\in G_{s}^{\prime}}\mathopen{}\left|\int D_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\mathbf{h}}_{s},{\mathbf{h}}_{s}^{\prime},{\mathbf{k}}_{s},{\mathbf{k}}_{s}^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\cdot g\cdot T^{{\lfloor{\mathbf{h}}_{s}+{\mathbf{k}}_{s}\rfloor}-{\lfloor{\mathbf{h}}_{s}^{\prime}+{\mathbf{k}}_{s}^{\prime}\rfloor}}\overline{g}\;d\mu\mathclose{}\right|^{2}

vanishes for some
1-bounded
{\mathcal{Z}}^{+}_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s-1}}
-measurable
D_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\mathbf{h}}_{s},{\mathbf{h}}_{s}^{\prime},{\mathbf{k}}_{s},{\mathbf{k}}_{s}^{\prime}\end{subarray}}
.

The last step is to apply Lemma
3.6
and
[
16
, Lemma 4.1]
in order to bound the expression above in terms of

\displaystyle\E_{{\mathbf{h}}_{s},{\mathbf{h}}_{s}^{\prime}\in G_{s}}\E_{{\mathbf{k}}_{s},{\mathbf{k}}_{s}^{\prime}\in G_{s}^{\prime}}\mathopen{}\left(\lvert\!|\!|g\cdot T^{{\lfloor{\mathbf{h}}_{s}+{\mathbf{k}}_{s}\rfloor}-{\lfloor{\mathbf{h}}_{s}^{\prime}+{\mathbf{k}}_{s}^{\prime}\rfloor}}\overline{g}|\!|\!\rvert_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s-1}}^{+}\mathclose{}\right)^{2^{s}}=\mathopen{}\left(\lvert\!|\!|g|\!|\!\rvert_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s-1},G_{s}+G_{s}^{\prime}}^{+}\mathclose{}\right)^{2^{s+1}}.

Analyzing how we arrived at (
10
), we see that each
D_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\mathbf{h}}_{s},{\mathbf{h}}_{s}^{\prime},{\mathbf{k}}_{s},{\mathbf{k}}_{s}^{\prime}\end{subarray}}
is a linear combination of
O_{\ell,s}(1)
1-bounded degree-
s
nilsequences times 4 dual functions from
{\mathcal{B}}_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s-1}}
. Using Lemma
3.5
, we can therefore find absolute
C=C(\ell,s)>0
such that

D_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\mathbf{h}}_{s},{\mathbf{h}}_{s}^{\prime},{\mathbf{k}}_{s},{\mathbf{k}}_{s}^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\in C\cdot{\mathcal{B}}_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s-1}},

and so by Lemma
3.6
, the average (
10
) vanishes whenever
\lvert\!|\!|g|\!|\!\rvert_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s-1},G_{s}+G_{s}^{\prime}}^{+}=0.
This concludes the proof.
∎

3.2.
Induction scheme

The next goal is to present an induction scheme that allows us to upgrade Theorem
3.3
to Theorem
3.1
. Our induction is based on the following formalism.

Definition 3.7
.

Let
G
be an abelian group,
\text{sub}(G)
be the set of all subgroups of
G
, and
\text{sub}(G)^{\omega}:=\sqcup_{n=1}^{\infty}\text{sub}(G)^{n}
. We say that a subset
\mathcal{C}
of
\text{sub}(G)^{\omega}
is a
CMS-family
if the followings properties hold:

(i)

(Concatenation) For all
d\in\mathbb{N}
and subgroups
H_{1},\dots,H_{d},H^{\prime}_{d}\subseteq G
,

(H_{1},\dots,H_{d-1},H_{d}),(H_{1},\dots,H_{d-1},H^{\prime}_{d})\in\mathcal{C}\Rightarrow(H_{1},\dots,H_{d-1},H_{d}+H^{\prime}_{d})\in\mathcal{C}.

(ii)

(Monotonicity) For all
d\in\mathbb{N}
and subgroups
H_{1},\dots,H_{d}\subseteq G
,

(H_{1},\dots,H_{d-1})\in\mathcal{C}\Rightarrow(H_{1},\dots,H_{d-1},H_{d})\in\mathcal{C}.

(iii)

(Symmetry) For all
d\in\mathbb{N}
, subgroups
H_{1},\dots,H_{d}\subseteq G
and permutations
\tau:[d]\to[d]
, we have

(H_{1},\dots,H_{d})\in\mathcal{C}\Rightarrow(H_{\tau(1)},\dots,H_{\tau(d)})\in\mathcal{C}.

For
f\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
, consider the following family of subgroups:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{C}}_{f}\colon=\{(G_{1},\ldots,G_{s})\in\text{sub}({\mathbb{R}}^{\ell})^{\omega}:\;f\in Z^{+}_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}}\}.

As it turns out,
{\mathcal{C}}_{f}
satisfies the conditions of Definition
3.7
.

Proposition 3.8
.

For every
f\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
, the collection
\mathcal{C}_{f}
is a CMS-family.

Proof.

The Monotonicity and Symmetry properties follow from Lemma
2.16
while the Concatenation property follows from Theorem
3.3
.
∎

We will derive Theorem
3.1
by combining Proposition
3.8
(which one could see as the “base case” of induction) with Proposition
3.9
below (which provides the inductive scheme).

Proposition 3.9
.

Let
s\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
,
d,d^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}
, and
G_{1},\ldots,G_{s},H_{1},\ldots,H_{d},H_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,H_{d^{\prime}}^{\prime}
be subgroups of an abelian group
G
.
Suppose that
\mathcal{C}
is a CMS-family. Then the following statements hold:

(i)

We have

\displaystyle(G_{1},\dots,G_{s},H_{1},\dots,H_{d}),(G_{1},\dots,G_{s},H^{\prime}_{1},\dots,H^{\prime}_{d^{\prime}})\in\mathcal{C}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow(G_{1},\dots,G_{s},(H_{i}+H^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}})_{i\in[d],i^{\prime}\in[d^{\prime}]})\in\mathcal{C}.

(ii)

If
H_{i}+H^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}=H
for all
i\in[d],i^{\prime}\in[d^{\prime}]
for some subgroup
H\subseteq G
, then

\displaystyle(G_{1},\ldots,G_{s},H_{1},\dots,H_{d}),(G_{1},\ldots,G_{s},H^{\prime}_{1},\dots,H^{\prime}_{d^{\prime}})\in\mathcal{C}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow(G_{1},\ldots,G_{s},H^{\times(d+d^{\prime}-1)})\in\mathcal{C}.

Proof of Theorem
3.1
assuming Proposition
3.9
.

Let
f\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
. To prove the first claim in the language of CMS-families, it suffices to show that if
{\mathcal{C}}_{f}
contains

\displaystyle(G_{1},\dots,G_{s},H_{1},\dots,H_{d})\quad\textrm{and}\quad(G_{1},\dots,G_{s},H^{\prime}_{1},\dots,H_{d}^{\prime}),

then it also contains

\displaystyle(G_{1},\dots,G_{s},(H_{i}+H^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}})_{i\in[d],i^{\prime}\in[d^{\prime}]}).

This follows from the fact that
{\mathcal{C}}_{f}
is a CMS-family (Proposition
3.8
) and the induction scheme given by the first part of Proposition
3.9
. Similarly, the second claim follows from Proposition
3.8
and the second part of Proposition
3.9
.
∎

We now prove Proposition
3.9
.

Proof of Proposition
3.9
.

To simplify the notation, we write
[H_{1},\dots,H_{d}]
if
(H_{1},\dots,H_{d})
belongs to
\mathcal{C}
, and we write
A+B
to indicate that claims
A
and
B
both hold.

We start with the first part of the proposition. Our claim can then be rephrased in this language as

\displaystyle[G_{1},\ldots,G_{s},H_{1},\ldots,H_{d}]+[G_{1},\ldots,G_{s},H^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,H_{d}^{\prime}]\Rightarrow[G_{1},\ldots,G_{s},(H_{i}+H^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}})_{i\in[d],i^{\prime}\in[d^{\prime}]}].

For any
(d,d^{\prime})\in{\mathbb{N}}^{2}
, we say that
(d,d^{\prime})
is
good
if the implication

(11)

\begin{split}[\mathbb{J},H_{1},\dots,H_{d}]+[\mathbb{J},H^{\prime}_{1},\dots,H^{\prime}_{d^{\prime}}]\Rightarrow[\mathbb{J},(H_{i}+H^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}})_{i\in[d],i^{\prime}\in[d^{\prime}]}]\end{split}

holds for any finite multifamily of subgroups
\mathbb{J}=(K_{j})_{j\in J}
of
G
(that is,
|J|<\infty
, elements are counted with multiplicity, and the family may be empty).
Then our goal is to show that every
(d,d^{\prime})\in{\mathbb{N}}^{2}
is good. We will prove this by double induction, first showing that all pairs
(d,1)
and
(1,d^{\prime})
are good and then establishing the statement for arbitrary
(d,d^{\prime})\in{\mathbb{N}}^{2}
.

That
(1,1)
is good is equivalent to the Concatenation property. Suppose that
(d,1)
is good for some
d\in{\mathbb{N}}
, i.e.

(12)

\displaystyle[{\mathbf{J}},H_{1},\ldots,H_{d}]+[{\mathbf{J}},H_{1}^{\prime}]\Rightarrow[{\mathbf{J}},(H_{i}+H_{1}^{\prime})_{i\in[d]}]

holds for all
{\mathbf{J}}
. We want to show that
(d+1,1)
is good as well. Applying Monotonicity, and then Symmetry as well as the implication (
12
) for the family
{\mathbf{J}}\cup\{H_{d+1}\}
, we obtain the implication

\displaystyle[{\mathbf{J}},H_{1},\ldots,H_{d+1}]+[{\mathbf{J}},H_{1}^{\prime}]

\displaystyle\Rightarrow[{\mathbf{J}},H_{1},\ldots,H_{d+1}]+[{\mathbf{J}},H_{1}^{\prime},H_{d+1}]

\displaystyle\Rightarrow[{\mathbf{J}},H_{d+1},(H_{i}+H_{1}^{\prime})_{i\in[d]}].

It remains to concatenate
H_{1}^{\prime}
with
H_{d+1}
. By Monotonicity, followed by Symmetry and Concatenation,
we deduce that

\displaystyle[{\mathbf{J}},H_{d+1},(H_{i}+H_{1}^{\prime})_{i\in[d]}]+[{\mathbf{J}},H_{1}^{\prime}]

\displaystyle\Rightarrow[{\mathbf{J}},H_{d+1},(H_{i}+H_{1}^{\prime})_{i\in[d]}]+[{\mathbf{J}},H_{1}^{\prime},(H_{i}+H_{1}^{\prime})_{i\in[d]}]

\displaystyle\Rightarrow[{\mathbf{J}},(H_{i}+H_{1}^{\prime})_{i\in[d+1]}].

Hence
(d+1,1)
is good. By induction and symmetry,
(d,1)
and
(1,d^{\prime})
are good for any
d,d^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}
.

Now, suppose that
(d,d^{\prime})\in{\mathbb{N}}^{2}
is good; we want to show that
(d+1,d^{\prime})
is good. In doing so, we will inductively apply the goodness of
(d,d^{\prime})
and
(1,d^{\prime})
. Applying Monotonicity first and then Symmetry and (
11
) for the family
{\mathbf{J}}\cup\{H_{d+1}\}
(this is where we apply the case
(d,d^{\prime})
), we get

\displaystyle[\mathbb{J},H_{1},\dots,H_{d+1}]+[\mathbb{J},H^{\prime}_{1},\dots,H^{\prime}_{d^{\prime}}]

\displaystyle\Rightarrow[\mathbb{J},H_{1},\dots,H_{d+1}]+[\mathbb{J},H^{\prime}_{1},\dots,H^{\prime}_{d^{\prime}},H_{d+1}]

\displaystyle\Rightarrow[\mathbb{J},H_{d+1},(H_{i}+H^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}})_{i\in[d],i^{\prime}\in[d^{\prime}]}].

It remains to concatenate
H_{d+1}
with the subgroups
H^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}
. By Monotonicity, followed by Symmetry and (
11
) for the family
{\mathbf{J}}\cup\{H_{i}+H^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}:\;i\in[d],i^{\prime}\in[d^{\prime}]\}
(this is where we apply the case
(1,d^{\prime})
), we obtain

[\mathbb{J},H_{d+1},(H_{i}+H^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}})_{i\in[d],i^{\prime}\in[d^{\prime}]}]+[\mathbb{J},H^{\prime}_{1},\dots,H^{\prime}_{d^{\prime}}]\\
\Rightarrow[\mathbb{J},H_{d+1},(H_{i}+H^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}})_{i\in[d],i^{\prime}\in[d^{\prime}]}]+[\mathbb{J},H^{\prime}_{1},\dots,H^{\prime}_{d^{\prime}},(H_{i}+H^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}})_{i\in[d],i^{\prime}\in[d^{\prime}]}]\\
\Rightarrow[\mathbb{J},(H_{i}+H^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}})_{i\in[d+1],i^{\prime}\in[d^{\prime}]}].

This establishes the case
(d+1,d^{\prime})
. The case
(d,d^{\prime}+1)
follows by an identical argument, and hence the claim holds by induction.

We move on to the second part of the proposition. Our starting assumptions can then be rephrased as
[{\mathbf{J}},H_{1},\dots,H_{d}]
and
[{\mathbf{J}},H^{\prime}_{1},\dots,H^{\prime}_{d^{\prime}}]
, where this time we fix
{\mathbf{J}}=\{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}\}
.
For
(i,i^{\prime})\in([0,d]\times[0,d^{\prime}])\backslash\{(0,0)\}
, we say that
(i,i^{\prime})
is
good
if

[{\mathbf{J}},H_{1},\dots,H_{d-i},H^{\prime}_{1},\dots,H^{\prime}_{d^{\prime}-i^{\prime}},H^{\times(i+i^{\prime}-1)}].

Then our goal is to show that
(d,d^{\prime})
is good, which will follow from the following subclaims:

(i)

if
(i+1,i^{\prime})
and
(i,i^{\prime}+1)
are good for some
i\in[0,d-1],i^{\prime}\in[0,d^{\prime}-1]
, then so is
(i+1,i^{\prime}+1)
;

(ii)

(i,0)
and
(0,i^{\prime})
are good for all
i\in[d],i^{\prime}\in[d^{\prime}]
.

It is not hard to see that both claims imply together that
(d,d^{\prime})
is good; to see this, visualize
(i,i^{\prime})
as lattice points on the rectangle
([0,d]\times[0,d^{\prime}])\backslash\{(0,0)\}
.

For the first subclaim, we infer from the Symmetry and Concatenation properties that

\begin{split}&\quad[{\mathbf{J}},H_{1},\dots,H_{d-(i+1)},H^{\prime}_{1},\dots,H^{\prime}_{d^{\prime}-(i^{\prime}+1)},H^{\prime}_{d^{\prime}-i^{\prime}},H^{\times(i+i^{\prime})}]\\
&+[{\mathbf{J}},H_{1},\dots,H_{d-(i+1)},H^{\prime}_{1},\dots,H^{\prime}_{d^{\prime}-(i^{\prime}+1)},H_{d-i},H^{\times(i+i^{\prime})}]\\
&\Rightarrow[{\mathbf{J}},H_{1},\dots,H_{d-(i+1)},H^{\prime}_{1},\dots,H^{\prime}_{d^{\prime}-(i^{\prime}+1)},H^{\times(i+i^{\prime}+1)}],\end{split}

which implies that
(i+1,i^{\prime}+1)
is good whenever
(i+1,i^{\prime})
and
(i,i^{\prime}+1)
are.

To show that
(0,i^{\prime})
is good, we use the Monotonicity property to deduce that

[{\mathbf{J}},H_{1},\dots,H_{d}]\Rightarrow[{\mathbf{J}},H_{1},\dots,H_{d},H^{\prime}_{1},\dots,H^{\prime}_{d^{\prime}-i^{\prime}},H^{\times(i^{\prime}-1)}]

for all
i^{\prime}\in[d^{\prime}]
. Since
[{\mathbf{J}},H_{1},\dots,H_{d}]
by assumption, we deduce that
(0,i^{\prime})
is good for all
i^{\prime}\in[d^{\prime}]
. By an analogous argument,
(i,0)
is good for all
i\in[d]
. This proves the second subclaim and thus finishes the proof.
∎

4.
Seminorm smoothing

Our next goal is to derive improved seminorm estimates on arbitrary multiple ergodic averages of commuting transformations along Hardy sequences of polynomial growth. In
[
16
, Theorem 10.1]
, we obtained very general estimates on such averages; in this section, we upgrade them to ones that are more useful for our applications via a new variant of the seminorm smoothing method from
[
16
,
28
,
30
]
. This is the first step in proving that the difference and product conditions in Definition
1.3
are
sufficient
for joint ergodicity.

To state our main results, we need the following definitions.

Definition 4.1

(Ordered Hardy family)
.

Let
{\mathcal{Q}}=(q_{1},\ldots,q_{m})\subseteq{\mathcal{H}}
be a family of Hardy sequences such that
q_{i}\succ 1
for all
i\in[m]
. We say that
{\mathcal{Q}}
is
an ordered Hardy family
(or simply
ordered
) if there exist integers
1\leq m_{1}\leq m_{2}\leq m_{3}\leq m
such that the following holds:

(i)

1\prec q_{1}(x)\prec\cdots\prec q_{m_{1}}(x)\ll\log x
;

(ii)

\log x\prec q_{m_{1}+1}(x)\prec\cdots\prec q_{m_{2}}(x)\prec x^{\varepsilon}
for every
\varepsilon>0
;

(iii)

for
m_{2}<i\leq m_{3}
, we have
q_{i}(x)=x^{l_{i}}
for positive integers
l_{m_{2}+1}<\cdots~<~l_{m_{3}}
;

(iv)

x^{\varepsilon}\prec q_{m_{3}+1}(x)\prec\cdots\prec q_{m}(x)
for some
\varepsilon>0
, and for each
m_{3}<i\leq m
, the function
q_{i}
is strongly nonpolynomial.

For instance, the family
(\log x,(\log x)^{2},x,x^{2},x^{2/3},x\log x,x^{3/2})
is ordered. It is a priori strange to put monomials
x,x^{2}
before slower growing strongly nonpolynomial functions like
x^{2/3}
of positive fractional degree, but this ordering turns out to come out naturally in the seminorm bounds from
[
16
]
stated below. More specifically, strongly nonpolynomial functions of positive fractional degree like
x^{2/3}
can be Taylor-expanded as polynomials of arbitrarily high degree on short intervals, and in this sense they attain higher degrees than polynomials
x,x^{2},\ldots
whose Taylor expansions are polynomials of the same degree.

Definition 4.2

(Extended span)
.

Let
{\mathcal{Q}}=(q_{1},\ldots,q_{m})\subseteq{\mathcal{H}}
be an ordered Hardy family. Then the
extended span
of
{\mathcal{Q}}
, denoted by
\extspan_{\mathbb{R}}{\mathcal{Q}}
, is the collection of sequences
a\in{\mathcal{H}}
satisfying

\lim_{x\to\infty}\mathopen{}\left|a(x)-\sum\limits_{i=1}^{m}\beta_{i}q_{i}(x)-\beta_{0}\mathclose{}\right|={0}

for some
\beta_{0},\ldots,\beta_{m}\in{\mathbb{R}}
.

Definition 4.3

(Leading coefficients)
.

Let
{\mathcal{Q}}=(q_{1},\ldots,q_{m})\subseteq{\mathcal{H}}
be an ordered Hardy family, and suppose that
\mathbf{a}\in(\extspan_{\mathbb{R}}{\mathcal{Q}})^{\ell}
satisfies

\lim_{x\to\infty}\mathopen{}\left|\mathbf{a}(x)-\sum\limits_{i=1}^{m}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{i}q_{i}(x)-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{0}\mathclose{}\right|={0}

for some
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{0},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{m}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\ell}
. Then we call
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{i}
the
leading coefficient
of
\mathbf{a}
if
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{i}\neq\mathbf{0}
and
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{i^{\prime}}=\mathbf{0}
for all
i<i^{\prime}\leq m
(note that the choices of
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{i}
are unique). If
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{0}=\cdots={\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{m}=\bf{0}
, then we say that
\mathbf{a}
has leading coefficient
\bf{0}.

All the concepts in this section involving degrees, leading coefficients and independence are defined with respect to a fixed ordered family. We will not specify the family as it is always clear from the context.

Then,
[
16
, Theorem 10.1]
gives the following seminorm bounds.

Theorem 4.4
.

Let
\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}
. Let
{\mathcal{Q}}\subseteq{\mathcal{H}}
be an ordered Hardy family, and let
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\in\extspan_{\mathbb{R}}{\mathcal{Q}}
with
a_{1}\succ\log
. Then there exists a positive integer
s=O_{\ell,{\mathcal{Q}}}(1)
and vectors
{\mathbf{v}}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{v}}_{s}
coming from the set of the leading coefficients of

\{a_{1}{\mathbf{e}}_{1},\;a_{1}{\mathbf{e}}_{1}-a_{2}{\mathbf{e}}_{2},\;\ldots,\;a_{1}{\mathbf{e}}_{1}-a_{\ell}{\mathbf{e}}_{\ell}\},

such that for all systems
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
and functions
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}\prod_{j\in[\ell]}T_{j}^{{\lfloor a_{j}(n)\rfloor}}f_{j}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}=0

whenever
\lvert\!|\!|f_{1}|\!|\!\rvert_{{\mathbf{v}}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{v}}_{s}}=0
.

The upgraded seminorm estimates that we prove in this paper can then be restated as follows. We recall that various notions of independence of Hardy sequences are defined in Definition
1.14
.

Theorem 4.5
.

Let
\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}
. Let
{\mathcal{Q}}\subseteq{\mathcal{H}}
be an ordered Hardy family, and let
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\in\extspan_{\mathbb{R}}{\mathcal{Q}}
with
a_{1}\succ\log
. Then there exists a positive integer
s=O_{\ell,{\mathcal{Q}}}(1)
and vectors
{\mathbf{v}}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{v}}_{s}
coming from the set of the leading coefficients of

\displaystyle\{a_{1}{\mathbf{e}}_{1}\}\cup\{a_{i}{\mathbf{e}}_{i}-a_{j}{\mathbf{e}}_{j}:\;i\neq j,\;a_{i},a_{j}\;\textrm{are\; dependent},\;a_{i},a_{j}\succ\log\},

such that for all systems
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
and functions
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
, we have

(13)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}\prod_{j\in[\ell]}T_{j}^{{\lfloor a_{j}(n)\rfloor}}f_{j}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}=0

whenever
\lvert\!|\!|f_{1}|\!|\!\rvert_{{\mathbf{v}}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{v}}_{s}}=0
.

In fact, a close inspection of Theorem
4.10
, from which this result follows, shows that we only need to consider the leading coefficient of
a_{i}{\mathbf{e}}_{i}-a_{j}{\mathbf{e}}_{j}
for those dependent
a_{i},a_{j}
of the same or higher degree than
a_{1}
in the sense of Definition
4.7
.

A priori, there seems to be no good reason why the vectors featuring in Theorem
4.5
should be any better than those from Theorem
4.4
. However, using the difference ergodicity condition, we will later be able to further replace all
{\mathbf{v}}_{i}
’s in Theorem
4.5
by copies of
{\mathbf{e}}_{1}
. This will give us Host-Kra seminorm control over the average in Theorem
4.5
assuming the difference ergodicity condition.

Example 4.6
.

For comparison, consider the tuple

(T_{1}^{{\lfloor n^{3/2}\rfloor}},T_{2}^{{\lfloor n^{3/2}+\log n\rfloor}},T_{3}^{{\lfloor n^{3/2}+n\log n\rfloor}},T_{4}^{{\lfloor n^{3/2}+n\log n\rfloor}})_{n}.

Then Theorem
4.4
gives that the corresponding multiple ergodic average would be controlled by a seminorm of
f_{1}
involving many copies of
{\mathbf{e}}_{1},\;{\mathbf{e}}_{1}-{\mathbf{e}}_{2},\;{\mathbf{e}}_{1}-{\mathbf{e}}_{3},\;{\mathbf{e}}_{1}-{\mathbf{e}}_{4}
. The iterates can be divided into two families of pairwise dependent functions,
\{n^{3/2},\;n^{3/2}+\log n\}
and
\{n^{3/2}+n\log n,\;n^{3/2}+n\log n\}
, hence Theorem
4.5
gives us control in terms of a seminorm of
f_{1}
involving many copies of
{\mathbf{e}}_{1},\;{\mathbf{e}}_{1}-{\mathbf{e}}_{2},\;{\mathbf{e}}_{3}-{\mathbf{e}}_{4}
. If the difference ergodicity condition holds, i.e. the sequences

(14)

(T_{1}^{{\lfloor n^{3/2}\rfloor}}T_{2}^{-{\lfloor n^{3/2}+\log n\rfloor}})_{n}\quad\textrm{and}\quad(T_{3}^{{\lfloor n^{3/2}+n\log n\rfloor}}T_{4}^{-{\lfloor n^{3/2}+n\log n\rfloor}})_{n}

are ergodic, then we can replace each of the vectors
{\mathbf{e}}_{1}-{\mathbf{e}}_{2},\;{\mathbf{e}}_{3}-{\mathbf{e}}_{4}
in the seminorm by many copies of
{\mathbf{e}}_{1}
. For
{\mathbf{e}}_{3}-{\mathbf{e}}_{4}
, this is immediate: the ergodicity of the second action in (
14
)
implies that
T_{3}T_{4}^{-1}
is ergodic, hence
\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{{\mathbf{e}}_{3}-{\mathbf{e}}_{4}}\leq\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{{\mathbf{e}}_{1}}
by e.g.
[
30
, Lemma 2.2]
. For
{\mathbf{e}}_{1}-{\mathbf{e}}_{2}
, this requires extra work, and the general method for unpacking the ergodicity of sequences like
(T_{1}^{{\lfloor n^{3/2}\rfloor}}T_{2}^{-{\lfloor n^{3/2}+\log n\rfloor}})_{n}
will be presented in the next sections.

It is worth noting that one can use the concatenation theorem to merge Theorems
4.4
and
4.5
into a stronger result. For instance, the previous example gives one estimate involving the vectors
{\mathbf{e}}_{1},{\mathbf{e}}_{1}-{\mathbf{e}}_{2},{\mathbf{e}}_{1}-{\mathbf{e}}_{3},{\mathbf{e}}_{1}-{\mathbf{e}}_{4}
and another one involving
{\mathbf{e}}_{1},{\mathbf{e}}_{1}-{\mathbf{e}}_{2},{\mathbf{e}}_{3}-{\mathbf{e}}_{4}
. Concatenating them, we get an estimate involving
{\mathbf{e}}_{1},{\mathbf{e}}_{1}-{\mathbf{e}}_{2},\langle{\mathbf{e}}_{1}-{\mathbf{e}}_{3},{\mathbf{e}}_{1}-{\mathbf{e}}_{4}\rangle
. However, we do not require strengthenings like this in the current paper.

4.1.
General formalism

The proof of Theorem
4.5
uses no properties of Hardy functions except the initial seminorm estimates provided by Theorem
4.4
. We therefore phrase its proof in a more general language that makes no reference to Hardy functions.

Our basic setup is captured by the following definition.

Definition 4.7
.

[Ordered family, degree, and independence]
An
ordered family of sequences
is an ordered collection
{\mathcal{Q}}=(q_{1},\ldots,q_{m})
of sequences
q_{i}:{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
whose germs are affinely independent.
9
9

9

That means that for every
N\in{\mathbb{N}}
, the restrictions of
1,q_{1},\ldots,q_{m}
to
[N,\infty)
are
{\mathbb{R}}
-independent.
Additionally, we assume that each ordered family of sequences comes with a fixed integer
0\leq m_{1}<m
.

Suppose that
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}:{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
satisfy

\lim_{x\to\infty}\mathopen{}\left|a_{j}(x)-\sum\limits_{i=1}^{m}\beta_{j,i}q_{i}(x)-\beta_{j,0}\mathclose{}\right|={0}

for some
\beta_{ji}\in{\mathbb{R}}
; like before, we call the collection of such sequences the
extended span
of
{\mathcal{Q}}
and denote it via
\extspan_{\mathbb{R}}{\mathcal{Q}}
.
We define the
degree
of
a_{j}
via

\deg a_{j}:=\max\{i\in[0,m]:\;\beta_{j,i}\neq 0\}

(the notion is well-defined since the choices of
\beta_{j,i}
are unique),
letting it be 0 if
\beta_{j,m}=\cdots=\beta_{j,0}=0
. Lastly, we call
\beta_{j,\deg a_{j}}
the
leading coefficient
of
a_{j}
.
We call the sequences:

•

a_{j},a_{j}^{\prime}

independent
if
(\beta_{j,m_{1}+1},\ldots,\beta_{j,m})
and
(\beta_{j^{\prime},m_{1}+1},\ldots,\beta_{j^{\prime},m})
are
{\mathbb{R}}
-linearly independent, otherwise we call them
dependent
;

•

a_{1},\ldots,a_{m}

pairwise independent
if for every
1\leq j<j^{\prime}\leq\ell
, the sequences
a_{j},a_{j^{\prime}}
are independent.

In other words, pairwise independence means that no nontrivial linear combination
c_{j}a_{j}+c_{j^{\prime}}a_{j^{\prime}}
for
1\leq j<j^{\prime}\leq\ell
lies in
\extspan_{\mathbb{R}}\{a_{1},\ldots,a_{m_{1}}\}
. For Hardy sequences defined with respect to an ordered Hardy basis, the notion of pairwise independence above coincides with the analogous notion from Definition
1.14
.

The definitions of degree, leading coefficients and pairwise (in)dependence naturally extend to sequences
{\mathbb{N}}^{\ell}\to{\mathbb{R}}
defined as
{\mathbb{R}}^{\ell}
-linear combinations of elements of the ordered basis
{\mathcal{Q}}
.

The averages studied in this section will typically be twisted by dual sequences defined below. For the definition of dual functions
{\mathcal{D}}_{s,T_{j}}(f)
, see Definition
2.8
.

Definition 4.8

(Dual sequences)
.

Let
d,\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
be a system. Then
{\mathfrak{D}}_{d}
denotes the class of sequences
{\mathcal{D}}:{\mathbb{Z}}\to L^{\infty}(\mu)
given by
{\mathcal{D}}(n)=T_{j}^{n}{\mathcal{D}}_{s,T_{j}}(f)
for some
s\in[d]
,
j\in[\ell]
, and
f\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
. We refer to elements of
{\mathfrak{D}}_{d}
as
level-
d
dual sequences
.

The initial seminorm estimates instantiated by Theorem
4.4
are a special case of the following definition.

Definition 4.9

(Families good for smoothing)
.

We call an ordered family of sequences
{\mathcal{Q}}

good for smoothing
if for all
d,\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
J\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
, sequences
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}
,
b_{1},\ldots,b_{J}\in\extspan_{\mathbb{R}}{\mathcal{Q}}

satisfying

\deg a_{1}=\max_{j}\deg a_{j}>m_{1},

systems
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
, and functions
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
, we have

(15)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\mathcal{D}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathcal{D}}_{J}\in{\mathfrak{D}}_{d}\end{subarray}}\sup_{|c_{n}|\leq 1}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}c_{n}\cdot\prod_{j\in[\ell]}T_{j}^{{\left\lfloor a_{j}(n)\right\rfloor}}f_{j}\cdot\prod_{j\in[J]}{\mathcal{D}}_{j}({\left\lfloor b_{j}(n)\right\rfloor})\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}=0

whenever
\lvert\!|\!|f_{1}|\!|\!\rvert_{{\mathbf{v}}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{v}}_{s}}=0

for some
s=O_{d,J,\ell,{\mathcal{Q}}}(1)
and vectors
{\mathbf{v}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{v}}_{s}
from the set of the leading coefficients of

\{a_{1}{\mathbf{e}}_{1},\;a_{1}{\mathbf{e}}_{1}-a_{2}{\mathbf{e}}_{2},\;\ldots,\;a_{1}{\mathbf{e}}_{1}-a_{\ell}{\mathbf{e}}_{\ell}\}.

Any ordered Hardy family
{\mathcal{Q}}
is good for smoothing via
[
16
, Corollary 6.4 & Theorem 10.2]
, where
m_{1}
in Definition
4.7
is the same as
m_{1}
in Definition
4.1
. Theorem
4.5
is then a consequence of the following general result.

Theorem 4.10
.

Let
\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}
. Suppose that an ordered family of sequences
{\mathcal{Q}}
is good for smoothing, and let
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\in\extSpan_{\mathbb{R}}{\mathcal{Q}}
with
\deg(a_{1})>m_{1}
.
Then there exists a positive integer
s=O_{\ell,{\mathcal{Q}}}(1)
and vectors
{\mathbf{v}}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{v}}_{s}
coming from the set of the leading coefficients of

\displaystyle\{a_{1}{\mathbf{e}}_{1}\}\cup\{a_{i}{\mathbf{e}}_{i}-a_{j}{\mathbf{e}}_{j}:\;i\neq j,\;a_{i},a_{j}\;\textrm{are\; dependent and }\deg(a_{i})=\deg(a_{j})\geq\deg(a_{1})\},

such that for all systems
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
and functions
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}\prod_{j\in[\ell]}T_{j}^{{\lfloor a_{j}(n)\rfloor}}f_{j}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}=0

whenever
\lvert\!|\!|f_{1}|\!|\!\rvert_{{\mathbf{v}}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{v}}_{s}}=0
.

In the proof of Theorem
4.10
, it will matter a lot whether pairs of sequences
a_{i},a_{j}
are dependent or not. We therefore state a slight generalization of Theorem
4.10
that makes it easier to read off the dependence or independence of particular sequences and better matches with the way the proof goes. In what follows, let
\hat{a}_{i}
denote the leading coefficient of
a_{i}
.

Proposition 4.11
.

Let
d,r,\ell_{1},\dots,\ell_{r}\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
\ell=\ell_{1}+\dots+\ell_{r}
, and
J\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
. Suppose that an ordered family of sequences
{\mathcal{Q}}

is good for smoothing, and let
a_{i,j}\in\extSpan_{\mathbb{R}}{\mathcal{Q}}
(for
i\in[r],j\in[\ell_{i}]
) be sequences
with the following properties:

(i)

a_{i,j}
is independent from
a_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}
if and only if
i\neq i^{\prime}
;

(ii)

there exists
r^{\prime}\in[r]
such that for all
i\in[r]
,
j\in[\ell_{1}]
, and
j^{\prime}\in[\ell_{i}]
, we have that
\deg(a_{1,j})>\deg(a_{i,j^{\prime}})
if and only if
i>r^{\prime}
;

(iii)

\deg(a_{1,1})>m_{1}
.

Then there exists a positive integer
s=O_{d,J,\ell,{\mathcal{Q}}}(1)
10
10

10

Note that
r,r^{\prime}\leq\ell
, and so the dependence of
s
and other parameters on
r,r^{\prime}
is implicit in their dependence on
\ell
.
and vectors
{\mathbf{v}}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{v}}_{s}
coming from the set

\displaystyle{\mathcal{G}}:=\{\hat{a}_{1,1}{\mathbf{e}}_{1,1},\hat{a}_{i,j}{\mathbf{e}}_{i,j}-\hat{a}_{i,j^{\prime}}{\mathbf{e}}_{i,j^{\prime}}:\;i\in[r^{\prime}],\;j,j^{\prime}\in[\ell_{i}],\;j\neq j^{\prime}\}

such that for all systems
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,(T_{i,j})_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in[r],j\in[\ell_{i}]\end{subarray}})
, functions
f_{i,j}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
and sequences
b_{1},\ldots,b_{J}\in\Span_{\mathbb{R}}{\mathcal{Q}}
, we have

(16)

\limsup_{N\to\infty}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\mathcal{D}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathcal{D}}_{J}\in{\mathfrak{D}}_{d}\end{subarray}}\sup_{|c_{n}|\leq 1}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}c_{n}\cdot\prod_{i\in[r]}\prod_{j\in[\ell_{i}]}T_{i,j}^{{\left\lfloor a_{i,j}(n)\right\rfloor}}f_{i,j}\cdot\prod_{j\in[J]}{\mathcal{D}}_{j}({\left\lfloor b_{j}(n)\right\rfloor})\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}

is zero whenever
\lvert\!|\!|f_{1}|\!|\!\rvert_{{\mathbf{v}}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{v}}_{s}}=0
.

We observe that the set
{\mathcal{H}}
does not depend on the “lower-degree” sequences
a_{i,j}
with
r^{\prime}<i\leq r
and
j\in[\ell_{i}]
. This is one way in which Proposition
4.11
is stronger than Theorem
4.10
; the other is the presence of dual functions in (
16
).

4.2.
A motivating example

Before we give the proof of Proposition
4.11
in full, we discuss its strategy for the average

(17)

\displaystyle\limsup_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}T_{1,1}^{a_{1}(n)}f_{1,1}\cdot T_{1,2}^{a_{1}(n)}f_{1,2}\cdot T_{2,1}^{a_{2}(n)}f_{2,1}\cdot T_{2,2}^{a_{2}(n)}f_{2,2}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)},

where, say,
a_{1}(n)={\lfloor n^{3/2}\rfloor}
and
a_{2}(n)={\lfloor n^{3/2}+n\log n\rfloor}
(what matters is that
a_{1},a_{2}
are independent and have the same leading term
n^{3/2}
). Throughout, all the functions are 1-bounded. While we strive to make this section as self-contained as possible, we strongly encourage the reader to first familiarize themselves with the examples of smoothing carried out in
[
30
, Section 8.1]
and
[
28
, Section 4]
for more details on various technical steps (e.g. dual-difference interchange) on which we will not elaborate in this expository section.

Theorem
4.4
gives us the following control over (
17
), which serves as a starting point of our argument.

Proposition 4.12
.

There exists
s\in{\mathbb{N}}
and vectors

(18)

\displaystyle{\mathbf{v}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{v}}_{s}\in\{{\mathbf{e}}_{1,1},\;{\mathbf{e}}_{1,1}-{\mathbf{e}}_{1,2},\;{\mathbf{e}}_{1,1}-{\mathbf{e}}_{2,1},\;{\mathbf{e}}_{1,1}-{\mathbf{e}}_{2,2}\}.

such that the average (
17
) is
0
whenever
\lvert\!|\!|f_{1,1}|\!|\!\rvert_{{\mathbf{v}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{v}}_{s}}=0
.

To get the conclusion of Theorem
4.5
(or Proposition
4.11
) we want the following.

Proposition 4.13
.

There exists
s\in{\mathbb{N}}
and vectors

(19)

\displaystyle{\mathbf{v}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{v}}_{s}\in\{{\mathbf{e}}_{1,1},\;{\mathbf{e}}_{1,1}-{\mathbf{e}}_{1,2},\;{\mathbf{e}}_{2,1}-{\mathbf{e}}_{2,2}\}.

such that the average (
17
) is
0
whenever
\lvert\!|\!|f_{1,1}|\!|\!\rvert_{{\mathbf{v}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{v}}_{s}}=0
.

The goal thus is to show that each vector
{\mathbf{e}}_{1,1}-{\mathbf{e}}_{2,j}
among (
18
) can be replaced by a bounded number of vectors from (
19
) so that the resulting seminorm still controls (
17
). In doing so, we will pass to auxiliary averages of the form

(20)

\displaystyle\limsup_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}T_{\eta(1,1)}^{a_{1}(n)}f_{1,1}\cdot T_{\eta(1,2)}^{a_{1}(n)}f_{1,2}\cdot T_{\eta(2,1)}^{a_{2}(n)}f_{2,1}\cdot T_{\eta(2,2)}^{a_{2}(n)}f_{2,2}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}

for various choices
\eta:I\to I
(here,
I=\{(1,1),(1,2),(2,1),(2,2)\}
), possibly twisted by weights
c_{n}
or dual terms
{\mathcal{D}}_{j}
. For convenience, we say that (
20
) has
type

\eta

or

(\eta(1,1),\eta(1,2);\eta(2,1),\eta(2,2)).

Much like in previous smoothing arguments
[
16
,
28
,
30
]
, in order to prove Proposition
4.13
, we will induct on two parameters:

•

a “complexity” of the type
\eta
, which can be measured in terms of the variance
\sum\limits_{i,j\in[2]}|\eta^{-1}(i,j)|^{2}
(the higher the variance, the lower the complexity, hence averages in which all transformations are distinct are most complex);

•

the length of the average, measured by the number of functions appearing in the average (weights
c_{n}
or dual terms
{\mathcal{D}}_{j}
do not count).

Hence Proposition
4.13
can be deduced inductively from the following two statements, one providing seminorm control for averages of types
((2,j),(1,2);(2,1),(2,2))
(for
j\in[2]
), and the other dealing with averages of length 3.

Claim 4.14
.

Fix
j\in[2]
. There exists
s\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that

\displaystyle\limsup_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}T_{2,j}^{a_{1}(n)}f_{1,1}\cdot T_{1,2}^{a_{1}(n)}f_{1,2}\cdot T_{2,1}^{a_{2}(n)}f_{2,1}\cdot T_{2,2}^{a_{2}(n)}f_{2,2}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}=0

whenever
\lvert\!|\!|f_{1,2}|\!|\!\rvert_{{\mathbf{e}}_{1,2}^{\times s},({\mathbf{e}}_{1,2}-{\mathbf{e}}_{2,j})^{\times s},({\mathbf{e}}_{2,1}-{\mathbf{e}}_{2,2})^{\times s}}=0
.

Claim 4.15
.

Fix
j\in[2]
and
d\in{\mathbb{N}}
. There exists
s\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that for every level-
d
dual function
{\mathcal{D}}
, we have

\displaystyle\limsup_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}T_{1,1}^{a_{1}(n)}f_{1,1}\cdot{\mathcal{D}}({a_{1}(n)})\cdot T_{2,1}^{a_{2}(n)}f_{2,1}\cdot T_{2,2}^{a_{2}(n)}f_{2,2}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}=0

whenever
\lvert\!|\!|f_{1,1}|\!|\!\rvert_{{\mathbf{e}}_{1,1}^{\times s},({\mathbf{e}}_{2,1}-{\mathbf{e}}_{2,2})^{\times s}}=0
.

Note that the seminorm appearing in both claims above are of the form provided by Proposition
4.11
: we can therefore think of both claims as the induction hypothesis.

Sketch of proof of Proposition
4.13
assuming Claims
4.14
and
4.15
.

We now outline the proof of Proposition
4.13
assuming the preceding auxiliary results. Our focus will be on the sequence of necessary reductions to averages of lower complexity or length rather than on the exact way in which these reductions are carried out. To get the sense of the technicalities involved in the latter, the reader should consult the examples from
[
30
, Section 8.1]
and
[
28
, Section 4]
.

Our goal is to show that (
17
) is 0 if
\lvert\!|\!|f_{1,1}|\!|\!\rvert_{{\mathbf{v}}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{v}}_{s}}=0
for some
s\in{\mathbb{N}}
and

{\mathbf{v}}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{v}}_{s}\in\mathcal{G}:=\{{\mathbf{e}}_{1,1},\;{\mathbf{e}}_{1,1}-{\mathbf{e}}_{1,2},\;{\mathbf{e}}_{2,1}-{\mathbf{e}}_{2,2}\}.

By Proposition
4.12
, we have a preliminary control which asserts that (
17
) is controlled by

\lvert\!|\!|f_{1,1}|\!|\!\rvert_{{\mathbf{v}}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{v}}_{t},{\mathbf{u}}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{u}}_{k}}
for some
k,t\in{\mathbb{N}}
and vectors
{\mathbf{v}}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{v}}_{t}\in\mathcal{G}
(in fact, they lie in
\{{\mathbf{e}}_{1,1},\;{\mathbf{e}}_{1,1}-{\mathbf{e}}_{1,2}\}
) and
{\mathbf{u}}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{u}}_{k}\in\{{\mathbf{e}}_{1,1}-{\mathbf{e}}_{2,1},{\mathbf{e}}_{1,1}-{\mathbf{e}}_{2,2}\}
.
Our aim is to remove
{\mathbf{u}}_{k}
at the cost of enlarging the number
t
, and then iterate this procedure with other
{\mathbf{u}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{u}}_{k-1}
.

Step 1: Ping.

This step emulates the Ping step from the earlier smoothing arguments
[
16
,
28
,
30
]
. Assume for concreteness that
{\mathbf{u}}_{k}={\mathbf{e}}_{1,1}-{\mathbf{e}}_{2,1}
(the case
{\mathbf{u}}_{k}={\mathbf{e}}_{1,1}-{\mathbf{e}}_{2,2}
being handled analogously). Then the Ping step essentially asserts that if some
\lvert\!|\!|f_{1,1}|\!|\!\rvert_{\mathbb{w}_{1},\dots,\mathbb{w}_{r},{\mathbf{u}}_{k}}
controls (
17
) and if some
\lvert\!|\!|f_{i,j}|\!|\!\rvert_{\mathbb{w}^{\prime}_{1},\dots,\mathbb{w}^{\prime}_{r^{\prime}}}
with
(i,j)\neq(1,1)
controls

(21)

\limsup_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}T_{2,1}^{a_{1}(n)}f_{1,1}\cdot T_{1,2}^{a_{1}(n)}f_{1,2}\cdot T_{2,1}^{a_{2}(n)}f_{2,1}\cdot T_{2,2}^{a_{2}(n)}f_{2,2}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}

(which is an expression derived from (
17
) by replacing the transformation
T_{1,1}
with
T_{2,1}
), then (
17
) is controlled by
\lvert\!|\!|f_{i,j}|\!|\!\rvert_{\mathbb{w}_{1},\dots,\mathbb{w}_{r},\mathbb{w}^{\prime}_{1},\dots,\mathbb{w}^{\prime}_{r^{\prime}}}
.

In our case, Claim
4.14
gives us that (
21
) is controlled by
\lvert\!|\!|f_{1,2}|\!|\!\rvert_{{\mathbf{u}}^{\prime}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{u}}^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}},{\mathbf{e}}_{1,2}^{\times d}}
for some
d,k^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
{\mathbf{u}}^{\prime}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{u}}^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}}\in\{{\mathbf{e}}_{1,2}-{\mathbf{e}}_{2,1},{\mathbf{e}}_{2,1}-{\mathbf{e}}_{2,2}\}
. Therefore, we conclude that (
17
) is controlled by

(22)

\displaystyle\lvert\!|\!|f_{1,2}|\!|\!\rvert_{{\mathbf{v}}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{v}}_{t},{\mathbf{u}}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{u}}_{k-1},{\mathbf{u}}^{\prime}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{u}}^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}},{\mathbf{e}}_{1,2}^{\times d}}.

Step 2: Pong.

This step again emulates the Pong step from
[
16
,
28
,
30
]
, and it essentially asserts that if some
\lvert\!|\!|f_{1,2}|\!|\!\rvert_{\mathbb{w}_{1},\dots,\mathbb{w}_{r},{\mathbf{e}}_{i,j}^{\times d}}
controls (
17
), and if
\lvert\!|\!|f_{1,1}|\!|\!\rvert_{\mathbb{w}^{\prime}_{1},\dots,\mathbb{w}^{\prime}_{r^{\prime}}}
controls

(23)

\limsup_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}T_{1,1}^{a_{1}(n)}f_{1,1}\cdot{\mathcal{D}}(a_{1}(n))\cdot T_{2,1}^{a_{2}(n)}f_{2,1}\cdot T_{2,2}^{a_{2}(n)}f_{2,2}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)},

where
{\mathcal{D}}
is a level-
d
dual sequence of
T_{1,2}
,
then
\lvert\!|\!|f_{1,1}|\!|\!\rvert_{\mathbb{w}_{1},\dots,\mathbb{w}_{r},\mathbb{w}^{\prime}_{1},\dots,\mathbb{w}^{\prime}_{r^{\prime}}}
controls (
17
).

In our case, it follows from the Ping step that (
17
) is controlled by (
22
). On the other hand, Claim
4.15
gives control of (
23
) by
\lvert\!|\!|f_{1,1}|\!|\!\rvert_{{\mathbf{u}}^{\prime\prime}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{u}}^{\prime\prime}_{k^{\prime\prime}}}
for some
k^{\prime\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
{\mathbf{u}}^{\prime\prime}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{u}}^{\prime\prime}_{k^{\prime\prime}}\in\mathcal{G}
. So we have that (
17
) is controlled by

(24)

\displaystyle\lvert\!|\!|f_{1,1}|\!|\!\rvert_{{\mathbf{v}}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{v}}_{t},{\mathbf{u}}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{u}}_{k-1},{\mathbf{u}}^{\prime}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{u}}^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}},{\mathbf{u}}^{\prime\prime}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{u}}^{\prime\prime}_{k^{\prime\prime}}}.

Step 3: Relative concatenation
.

This step is a completely new contribution to the seminorm smoothing machinery, and it crucially relies on the relative concatenation results from Section
3
. The preliminary estimate and the conclusion from the Pong step asserts that (
17
) is zero when
f_{1,1}
is orthogonal to one of

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{+}_{{\mathbf{v}}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{v}}_{t},{\mathbf{u}}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{u}}_{k}}\quad\textrm{or}\quad\mathcal{Z}^{+}_{{\mathbf{v}}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{v}}_{t},{\mathbf{u}}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{u}}_{k-1},{\mathbf{u}}^{\prime}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{u}}^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}},{\mathbf{u}}^{\prime\prime}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{u}}^{\prime\prime}_{k^{\prime\prime}}}.

It then follows from Corollary
3.2
that (
17
) is zero if
f_{1,1}
is orthogonal to

\mathcal{Z}^{+}_{{\mathbf{v}}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{v}}_{t},{\mathbf{u}}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{u}}_{k-1},\{\langle{\mathbf{u}}_{k},{\mathbf{u}}\rangle:\;u\in\mathcal{R}\}},

or equivalently if

\lvert\!|\!|f_{1,1}|\!|\!\rvert^{+}_{{\mathbf{v}}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{v}}_{t},{\mathbf{u}}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{u}}_{k-1},\{\langle{\mathbf{u}}_{k},{\mathbf{u}}\rangle:\;u\in\mathcal{R}\}}=0,

where
\mathcal{R}
is the multi-set
\{{\mathbf{u}}^{\prime}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{u}}^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}},{\mathbf{u}}^{\prime\prime}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{u}}^{\prime\prime}_{k^{\prime\prime}}\}
.
However, for any
{\mathbf{u}}\in\mathcal{R}
, either
{\mathbf{u}}\in\mathcal{G}
or
{\mathbf{u}}={\mathbf{e}}_{1,2}-{\mathbf{e}}_{2,1}
. Now, for any such
{\mathbf{u}}
, the group
\langle{\mathbf{u}}_{k},{\mathbf{u}}\rangle
contains an element of
{\mathcal{G}}
. In the former case, this is immediate, while in the latter case, the group
\langle{\mathbf{u}}_{k},{\mathbf{u}}\rangle=\langle{\mathbf{e}}_{1,1}-{\mathbf{e}}_{2,1},{\mathbf{e}}_{1,2}-{\mathbf{e}}_{2,1}\rangle
contains
{\mathbf{e}}_{1,1}-{\mathbf{e}}_{1,2}
. By Lemma
2.1
, this implies that (
17
) is controlled by
\lvert\!|\!|f_{1,1}|\!|\!\rvert_{{\mathbf{v}}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{v}}_{t^{\prime}},{\mathbf{u}}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{u}}_{k-1}}=0
for some
t^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
{\mathbf{v}}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{v}}_{t^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{G}
.

We may then repeat this process to remove all of
{\mathbf{u}}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{u}}_{k-1}
and end up with a control of (
17
) by
\lvert\!|\!|f_{1,1}|\!|\!\rvert_{{\mathbf{v}}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{v}}_{t^{\prime\prime}}}=0
for some
t^{\prime\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
{\mathbf{v}}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{v}}_{t^{\prime\prime}}\in\mathcal{G}
, as desired.
∎

4.3.
Proof of Proposition
4.11

We move on to prove Proposition
4.11
. From now on, we fix a system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,(T_{i,j})_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in[r],j\in[\ell_{i}]\end{subarray}})
.

Case 1:
a_{1,1}
has the maximum degree.

We first prove this result under the assumption that
a_{1,1}
has the maximum degree.
In this case, all of
a_{i,j}
’s (for
i\in[r^{\prime}]
and
j\in[\ell_{i}]
) have the same degree greater than
m_{1}
.
We first consider the case when
\ell_{1}+\dots+\ell_{r^{\prime}}=1
. Then
a_{1,1}
has a higher degree than all other
a_{i,j}
’s, and so the maps
a_{1,1}
and
a_{1,1}-a_{i,j}
have the same leading coefficients. So the fact that
{\mathcal{Q}}
is good for smoothing implies that (
16
) is zero if
\lvert\!|\!|f_{1}|\!|\!\rvert_{{\mathbf{e}}_{1,1}^{\times s}}=0
for some
s=O_{d,J,\ell,{\mathcal{Q}}}(1)
.

Now suppose that the conclusion holds when
\ell_{1}+\dots+\ell_{r^{\prime}}=\ell^{\prime}-1
for some
\ell^{\prime}\geq 2
; our aim is to show that it also holds when
\ell_{1}+\dots+\ell_{r^{\prime}}=\ell^{\prime}
. The argument below will make use of the following definitions.

Definition 4.16

(Types, minority images and pre-images, and good terms)
.

Let

I:=\{(i,j)\in{\mathbb{N}}^{2}\colon\;i\in[r],j\in[\ell_{i}]\}\quad\text{and}\quad I^{\prime}:=\{(i,j)\in{\mathbb{N}}^{2}\colon\;i\in[r^{\prime}],j\in[\ell_{i}]\}.

We call a map
\eta\colon I\to I
a
type
, and we call it
proper
if it satisfies the following conditions:

(i)

\eta(I^{\prime})\subseteq I^{\prime}
;

(ii)

\eta|_{I\backslash I^{\prime}}
is an identity;

(iii)

\eta(i,j)\neq\eta(i,j^{\prime})
for all
i\in[r^{\prime}]
and distinct
j,j^{\prime}\in[\ell_{i}]
.

We say that
\tilde{i}\in I^{\prime}
is a
minority image
of
\eta
if

|\eta^{-1}(\tilde{i})|=\min_{\tilde{i}^{\prime}\in I^{\prime}}\max(|\eta^{-1}(\tilde{i}^{\prime})|,1).

In other words,
{\mathbf{e}}_{\tilde{i}}
is a vector that appears least frequently in the multi-set

\{{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta(i,j)}\colon\;i\in[r^{\prime}],\;j\in[\ell_{i}]\}

(but it does appear at least once). We call every element in
\eta^{-1}(\tilde{i})
a
minority pre-image
of
\eta
. Lastly, we say that
(i_{0},j_{0})
is a
good term of

\eta
if
there exists a positive integer
s=O_{d,J,\ell,{\mathcal{Q}}}(1)
and vectors
{\mathbf{v}}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{v}}_{s}
coming from the set

\{\hat{a}_{\eta(i_{0},j_{0})}{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta(i_{0},j_{0})},\hat{a}_{\eta(i,j)}{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta(i,j)}-\hat{a}_{\eta(i,j^{\prime})}{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta(i,j^{\prime})}:\;i\in[r^{\prime}],\;j,j^{\prime}\in[\ell_{i}],\;j\neq j^{\prime}\}

such that for all 1-bounded functions
f_{i,j}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
and sequences
b_{1},\ldots,b_{J}\in\Span_{\mathbb{R}}{\mathcal{Q}}
, the average

(25)

\limsup_{N\to\infty}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\mathcal{D}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathcal{D}}_{J}\in{\mathfrak{D}}_{d}\end{subarray}}\sup_{|c_{n}|\leq 1}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}c_{n}\cdot\prod_{i\in[r]}\prod_{j\in[\ell_{r}]}T_{\eta(i,j)}^{{\left\lfloor\frac{\hat{a}_{\eta(i,j)}}{\hat{a}_{i,j}}a_{i,j}(n)\right\rfloor}}f_{i,j}\cdot\prod_{j\in[J]}{\mathcal{D}}_{j}({\left\lfloor b_{j}(n)\right\rfloor})\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}

is zero whenever
\lvert\!|\!|f_{i_{0},j_{0}}|\!|\!\rvert_{{\mathbf{v}}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{v}}_{s}}=0
.

To prove the statement, it suffices to show that any minority pre-image of any proper
\eta
is good, and then apply this to the identity map
\eta(\tilde{i})=\tilde{i}
, which is proper and in which every term is minority.

The next definition explains how we pass from more to less complex types.

Definition 4.17

(Descendance)
.

For
\tilde{i},\tilde{i}^{\prime}\in I^{\prime}
, let
\sigma_{\tilde{i}\to\tilde{i}^{\prime}}(\eta)\colon I\to I
be the map given by
\sigma_{\tilde{i}\to\tilde{i}^{\prime}}(\eta)(\tilde{i})=\eta(\tilde{i}^{\prime})
and
\sigma_{\tilde{i}\to\tilde{i}^{\prime}}(\eta)(\tilde{j})=\eta(\tilde{j})
for all
\tilde{j}\in I\backslash\{\tilde{i}\}
. For proper maps
\eta
and
\eta^{\prime}
, we say that
\eta

descends to

\eta^{\prime}
(write as
\eta\searrow\eta^{\prime}
) if
\eta^{\prime}=\sigma_{\tilde{i}\to\tilde{i}^{\prime}}(\eta)
for some
\tilde{i},\tilde{i}^{\prime}\in I^{\prime}
such that
\eta(\tilde{i})\neq\eta(\tilde{i}^{\prime})
and
|\eta^{-1}(\eta(\tilde{i}))|\leq|\eta^{-1}(\eta(\tilde{i}^{\prime}))|
.

It is not hard to see that

\eta\searrow\eta^{\prime}\Rightarrow\sum_{\tilde{i}\in I}|\eta^{-1}(\tilde{i})|^{2}<\sum_{\tilde{i}\in I}|{\eta^{\prime}}^{-1}(\tilde{i})|^{2},

as an immediate consequence of which we have that the relation
\searrow
is
loopless
, meaning that there exist no
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and no proper types
\eta_{1},\dots,\eta_{n}
with
\eta_{1}\searrow\eta_{2}\searrow\dots\searrow\eta_{n}\searrow\eta_{1}
.

The base case of induction will come from averages of the following type.
11
11

11

A reader familiar with the earlier seminorm smoothing argument might inquire about the connection between extreme and basic types, the latter of which were playing the role of the base case in the smoothing arguments from
[
16
,
28
,
30
]
. Despite superficial similarity, these two notions are rather different. For instance, the smoothing argument for pairwise dependent integer polynomials from
[
28
]
would allow a descendance
((1,1),(1,2);(2,1),(2,2))\searrow((2,1),(1,2);(2,1),(2,2))\searrow((2,1),(2,1);(2,1),(2,2))
. The latter reduction is not permissible in this smoothing argument even though it is perfectly allowed in
[
28
]
. The type
((2,1),(2,1);(2,1),(2,2))
reached this way is basic in the sense of
[
28
]
, but neither proper not extreme according to this paper’s convention. Conversely, the arguments of this paper allow us to reduce types via the descendance
((1,1),(1,2),(1,3);(2,1),(2,2))\searrow((1,1),(1,2),(2,1);(2,1),(2,2))\searrow((1,1),(1,2),(2,1);(2,1),(1,2))
. The type reached at the end is extreme but not basic as defined in
[
28
]
.

Definition 4.18

(Extreme types)
.

We say that a proper map
\eta
is
extreme
if there is no proper map
\eta^{\prime}
with
\eta\searrow\eta^{\prime}
.

For any proper map
\eta
, denote
w(\eta)=0
if
\eta
is extreme. Otherwise,
let
w(\eta)
denote the largest number
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that
\eta=\eta_{0}\searrow\eta_{1}\searrow\dots\searrow\eta_{n}
for some proper maps
\eta_{0},\dots,\eta_{n}
with
\eta_{n}
being extreme. Since the relation
\searrow
is loopless,
w(\eta)
is well defined and is at most
O_{\ell}(1)
.

Using the fact that
\eta\searrow\eta^{\prime}\Rightarrow w(\eta^{\prime})<w(\eta)
,
to complete the proof, it suffices to prove the following claim (and then apply it repeatedly
O_{\ell}(1)
times).

Claim 4.19

(Minority pre-images are good)
.

Let
\eta
be a proper type. Suppose that for any proper type
\eta^{\prime}
with
\eta\searrow\eta^{\prime}
, any minority pre-image of
\eta^{\prime}
is good. Then any minority pre-image of
\eta
is good (in particular, any minority pre-image of
\eta
is good when
\eta
is extreme).

Example 4.20
.

This example shows how Claim
4.19
is used iteratively in the proof of Proposition
4.13
. Phrased in the language of this section, the purpose of this proposition was to show that
(1,1)
is a good term for
\eta=((1,1),(1,2);(2,1),(2,2))
. Note that
\eta\searrow\eta^{\prime}
by changing the first entry of
\eta
if and only if
\eta^{\prime}
is of type
((2,1),(1,2);(2,1),(2,2))
or
((2,2),(1,2);(2,1),(2,2))
(the other possible type
((1,2),(1,2);(2,1),(2,2))
is not proper). Claim
4.14
, which showed that
(1,2)
is good for either of these two proper types, can be seen as a special case of Claim
4.19
.

Recall from the proof of Proposition
4.13
that Claim
4.14
was applied in the Ping step of the proof. This will remain true more generally: Claim
4.19
will always turn up in the Ping step of the smoothing argument.

To prove Claim
4.14
, we would similarly want to show that the second term of
\eta^{\prime}=((2,1),(1,2);(2,1),(2,2))
is good (and analogously with
((2,2),(1,2);(2,1),(2,2))
). Note that
\eta^{\prime}\searrow\eta^{\prime\prime}
by changing the second entry of
\eta
if and only if
\eta^{\prime\prime}=((2,1),(2,2);(2,1),

(2,2))
(the other possible type
((2,1),(2,1);(2,1),(2,2))
is not proper). This is an extreme type in which all four terms are minorities. Seminorm estimates for this type that are in line with Proposition
4.11
will follow directly from the assumption of being good for smoothing.
Indeed, the expression we wish to control is of the form

\limsup_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}T_{2,1}^{a_{1}(n)}f_{1,1}\cdot T_{2,2}^{a_{1}(n)}f_{1,2}\cdot T_{2,1}^{a_{2}(n)}f_{2,1}\cdot T_{2,2}^{a_{2}(n)}f_{2,2}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)},

and since
{\mathcal{Q}}
is good for smoothing, this average is 0 if
\lvert\!|\!|f_{1,1}|\!|\!\rvert_{{\mathbf{e}}_{2,1}^{\times s},({\mathbf{e}}_{2,1}-{\mathbf{e}}_{2,2})^{\times s}}=0
for some
s\in{\mathbb{N}}
. This implies that the first term of
\eta^{\prime\prime}
is good, and an analogous argument would give desired seminorm control in terms of seminorms of all the other terms.

We proceed to prove Claim
4.19
. Assume without loss of generality that
(1,1)
is a minority pre-image of
\eta
.
Let

\displaystyle{\mathcal{G}}_{\eta}:=\{\hat{a}_{\eta(1,1)}{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta(1,1)},\hat{a}_{\eta(i,j)}{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta(i,j)}-\hat{a}_{\eta(i,j^{\prime})}{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta(i,j^{\prime})}:\;i\in[r^{\prime}],\;j,j^{\prime}\in[\ell_{i}],\;j\neq j^{\prime}\}.

The properness of
\eta
implies in particular that the elements of
{\mathcal{G}}_{\eta}
are nonzero.
It suffices to show that (
25
) can be controlled by a box seminorm of
f_{1,1}
involving only transformations from
{\mathcal{G}}_{\eta}
, which we will do by establishing the following property for
k=0
.

Definition 4.21

(Property
k
)
.

We say that
Property
k
holds if there exist
t\in{\mathbb{N}}
and vectors
{\mathbf{v}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{v}}_{t},{\mathbf{u}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{u}}_{k}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\ell}
(where
{\mathbf{v}}_{i}\in{\mathcal{G}}_{\eta}
while
{\mathbf{u}}_{i}
’s are the leading coefficients of
\frac{\hat{a}_{\eta(1,1)}}{\hat{a}_{1,1}}a_{1,1}{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta(1,1)}-\frac{\hat{a}_{\eta(i,j)}}{\hat{a}_{i,j}}a_{i,j}{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta(i,j)}
for some
(i,j)\in I^{\prime}\backslash\{(1,1)\}
), such that for all functions
f_{i,j}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
and sequences
b_{1},\ldots,b_{J}\in\Span_{\mathbb{R}}{\mathcal{Q}}
, the average (
25
) is 0 whenever

\lvert\!|\!|f_{1,1}|\!|\!\rvert_{{\mathbf{v}}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{v}}_{t},{\mathbf{u}}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{u}}_{k}}=0.

Since
{\mathcal{Q}}
is good for smoothing and since

\deg(a_{1,1})=\dots=\deg(a_{r^{\prime},1})>\max_{r^{\prime}<i\leq r}\max_{j\in[\ell_{i}]}\deg(a_{i,j}),

Property
k
holds for some
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
.
Our goal is to show that Property 0 holds. To achieve this, it suffices to show that if Property
k
holds for some
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
, then Property
k-1
also holds.

Assume that

{\mathbf{u}}_{k}
is the leading coefficient of

\frac{\hat{a}_{\eta(1,1)}}{\hat{a}_{1,1}}a_{1,1}{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta(1,1)}-\frac{\hat{a}_{\eta(i_{0},j_{0})}}{\hat{a}_{i_{0},j_{0}}}a_{i_{0},j_{0}}{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta(i_{0},j_{0})}

for some
(i_{0},j_{0})\in I^{\prime}\backslash\{(1,1)\}
. We consider several possible scenarios.

If
\eta(i_{0},j_{0})=\eta(1,1)
, then since
\eta
is proper, we have that
i_{0}\geq 2
and thus
a_{1,1}
is independent from
a_{i_{0},j_{0}}
. This implies that
{\mathbf{u}}_{k}
is a nonzero multiple of
{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta(1,1)}
. Since
{\mathcal{G}}_{\eta}
contains
\hat{a}_{\eta(1,1)}{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta(1,1)}
, Property
k-1
holds by Lemma
2.1
after relabeling
{\mathbf{u}}_{k}
as
{\mathbf{v}}_{t+1}
.

If instead
\eta(i_{0},j_{0})=\eta(1,j)
for some
2\leq j\leq\ell_{1}
, then
{\mathbf{u}}_{k}
belongs to
{\mathcal{G}}_{\eta}
and again Property
k-1
holds by by relabeling
{\mathbf{u}}_{k}
as
{\mathbf{v}}_{t+1}
.

It remains to consider the case when
\eta(i_{0},j_{0})\neq\eta(1,j)
for all
1\leq j\leq\ell_{1}
. In this case,
i_{0}\neq 1
.
Denote
\eta^{\prime}:=\sigma_{(1,1)\to(i_{0},j_{0})}(\eta)
.
Since

\eta^{\prime}(1,1)=\eta(i_{0},j_{0})\neq\eta(1,j)=\eta^{\prime}(1,j)

for all
2\leq j\leq\ell_{1}
,
the map
\eta^{\prime}
is proper. Moreover,
\eta\searrow\eta^{\prime}
as a consequence of
(1,1)
being a minority pre-image of
\eta
.

In particular, if
\eta
is extreme (i.e.
w(\eta)=0
), then the non-existence of such
\eta^{\prime}
forces
\eta(i_{0},j_{0})=\eta(1,j)
for some
j\in[\ell_{1}]
, and we are back in one of the previous cases.

So, in what follows we assume that
\eta(i_{0},j_{0})\neq\eta(1,j)
for all
1\leq j\leq\ell_{1}
since all the remaining cases can be covered, and we take
\eta^{\prime}=\sigma_{(1,1)\to(i_{0},j_{0})}(\eta)
.

Step 1: Ping.

Suppose that (
25
) is positive.
It follows from
[
16
, Lemma 11.7]
that

\limsup_{N\to\infty}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\mathcal{D}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathcal{D}}_{J}\in{\mathfrak{D}}_{d}\end{subarray}}\sup_{|c_{n}|\leq 1}\lim_{N\to\infty}\Bigl\|\E_{n\in[N]}c_{n}\cdot T_{\eta(1,1)}^{{\left\lfloor\frac{\hat{a}_{\eta(1,1)}}{\hat{a}_{1,1}}a_{1,1}(n)\right\rfloor}}\tilde{f}_{1,1}\cdot\prod_{(i,j)\in I\backslash\{(1,1)\}}T_{\eta(i,j)}^{{\left\lfloor\frac{\hat{a}_{\eta(i,j)}}{\hat{a}_{i,j}}a_{i,j}(n)\right\rfloor}}f_{i,j}\\
\prod_{j\in[J]}\mathcal{D}_{j}({\left\lfloor b_{j}(n)\right\rfloor})\Bigr\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}>0

for some
\tilde{f}_{1,1}
which is “structured” in that it is the weak limit
\lim\limits_{w\to\infty}A_{N_{w}}
for

(26)

A_{N_{w}}:=\E_{n\in[N_{w}]}\overline{c}^{\prime}_{n}T_{\eta(1,1)}^{-{\left\lfloor\frac{\hat{a}_{\eta(1,1)}}{\hat{a}_{1,1}}a_{1,1}(n)\right\rfloor}}\overline{g}_{w}\prod_{(i,j)\in I\backslash\{(1,1)\}}T_{\eta(i,j)}^{{\left\lfloor\frac{\hat{a}_{\eta(i,j)}}{\hat{a}_{i,j}}a_{i,j}(n)\right\rfloor}}T_{\eta(1,1)}^{-{\left\lfloor\frac{\hat{a}_{\eta(1,1)}}{\hat{a}_{1,1}}a_{1,1}(n)\right\rfloor}}\overline{f}_{i,j}\\
\prod_{j\in[J]}T_{\eta(1,1)}^{-{\left\lfloor\frac{\hat{a}_{\eta(1,1)}}{\hat{a}_{1,1}}a_{1,1}(n)\right\rfloor}}\overline{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}_{j}({\left\lfloor b_{j}(n)\right\rfloor})

for some increasing sequence
(N_{w})_{w}
, 1-bounded weights
(c^{\prime}_{n})_{n}
, 1-bounded functions
g_{w}
, and dual sequences
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{J}\in\mathfrak{D}_{d}
. By assumption, we have

\lvert\!|\!|\tilde{f}_{1,1}|\!|\!\rvert_{{\mathbf{v}}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{v}}_{t},{\mathbf{u}}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{u}}_{k}}>0.

We then unpack this seminorm using
[
16
, Lemma 11.11]
and the formula (
26
) for
\tilde{f}_{1,1}
; after composing the resulting expression with
T_{\eta(1,1)}^{{\left\lfloor\frac{\hat{a}_{\eta(1,1)}}{\hat{a}_{1,1}}a_{1,1}(n)\right\rfloor}}
and applying the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, we have

\liminf_{M\to\infty}\E_{{\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}^{\prime},{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime},{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime\prime}\in[\pm M]^{t+k-1}}\limsup_{N\to\infty}\sup_{|c_{n}|\leq 1}\Bigl\|\E_{n\in[N]}c_{n}\cdot T_{\eta(1,1)}^{{\left\lfloor\frac{\hat{a}_{\eta(1,1)}}{\hat{a}_{1,1}}a_{1,1}(n)\right\rfloor}}h_{{\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}^{\prime},{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime},{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime\prime}}\\
\prod_{(i,j)\in I\backslash\{(1,1)\}}T_{\eta(i,j)}^{{\left\lfloor\frac{\hat{a}_{\eta(i,j)}}{\hat{a}_{i,j}}a_{i,j}(n)\right\rfloor}}f_{i,j,{\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}^{\prime},{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime},{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime\prime}}\cdot\prod_{j\in[J]}{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}_{j,{\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}^{\prime},{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime},{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime\prime}}({\left\lfloor b_{j}(n)\right\rfloor})\Bigr\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}>0

where

\displaystyle f_{i,j,{\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}^{\prime},{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime},{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime\prime}}

\displaystyle=\Delta_{\begin{subarray}{c}({\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{1}m_{1}^{\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{1}m_{1}\right\rfloor},{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{1}m_{1}^{\prime\prime\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{1}m_{1}^{\prime\prime}\right\rfloor}),\ldots,\\
({\left\lfloor{\mathbf{u}}_{k-1}m_{t+k-1}^{\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{u}}_{k-1}m_{t+k-1}\right\rfloor},{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{u}}_{k-1}m_{t+k-1}^{\prime\prime\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{u}}_{k-1}m_{t+k-1}^{\prime\prime}\right\rfloor})\end{subarray}}\overline{f_{i,j}}

\displaystyle\textrm{and}\quad{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}_{j,{\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}^{\prime},{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime},{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime\prime}}

\displaystyle=\Delta_{\begin{subarray}{c}({\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{1}m_{1}^{\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{1}m_{1}\right\rfloor},{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{1}m_{1}^{\prime\prime\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{1}m_{1}^{\prime\prime}\right\rfloor}),\ldots,\\
({\left\lfloor{\mathbf{u}}_{k-1}m_{t+k-1}^{\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{u}}_{k-1}m_{t+k-1}\right\rfloor},{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{u}}_{k-1}m_{t+k-1}^{\prime\prime\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{u}}_{k-1}m_{t+k-1}^{\prime\prime}\right\rfloor})\end{subarray}}\overline{{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}_{j}}

and each
h_{{\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}^{\prime},{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime},{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime\prime}}
is a product of
2^{t+k-1}
1-bounded
approximately
{\mathbf{u}}_{k}
-invariant functions
, meaning functions of the form
\E\limits_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}T^{{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{u}}_{k}n\right\rfloor}}f
for some
f\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
.
Since
{\mathbf{u}}_{k}=\hat{a}_{\eta(1,1)}{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta(1,1)}-\hat{a}_{\eta^{\prime}(1,1)}{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta^{\prime}(1,1)}
, it follows from
[
16
, Lemma 11.10]
that

(27)

\liminf_{M\to\infty}\E_{{\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}^{\prime},{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime},{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime\prime}\in[\pm M]^{t+k-1}}\limsup_{N\to\infty}\sup_{|c_{n}|\leq 1}\Bigl\|\prod_{j\in[J]}\E_{n\in[N]}c_{n}\cdot T_{\eta^{\prime}(1,1)}^{{\left\lfloor\frac{\hat{a}_{\eta^{\prime}(1,1)}}{\hat{a}_{1,1}}a_{1,1}(n)\right\rfloor}}h^{\prime}_{{\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}^{\prime},{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime},{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime\prime}}\\
\prod_{(i,j)\in I\backslash\{(1,1)\}}T_{\eta^{\prime}(i,j)}^{{\left\lfloor\frac{\hat{a}_{\eta^{\prime}(i,j)}}{\hat{a}_{i,j}}a_{i,j}(n)\right\rfloor}}f_{i,j,{\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}^{\prime},{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime},{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime\prime}}\cdot\prod_{j\in[J]}{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}_{j,{\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}^{\prime},{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime},{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime\prime}}({\left\lfloor b_{j}(n)\right\rfloor})\Bigr\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}>0

for some 1-bounded functions
h^{\prime}_{{\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}^{\prime},{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime},{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime\prime}}
. It is at this step that we have passed from an average of type
\eta
to one of type
\eta^{\prime}
, and this key reduction was enabled by the
{\mathbf{u}}_{k}
-almost invariance of
h_{{\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}^{\prime},{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime},{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime\prime}}
’s.

Note that we may find some
\tilde{u}=(u^{\prime},u^{\prime\prime})\in I^{\prime}\backslash\{(1,1)\}
which is a minority pre-image of
\eta^{\prime}
(to see this, let
\tilde{v}
be a minority pre-image of
\eta^{\prime}
; if
\tilde{v}\neq(1,1)
, then we pick

whereas if
\tilde{v}=(1,1)
, then we pick
\tilde{u}=(i_{0},j_{0})
since
\eta^{\prime}(1,1)=\eta^{\prime}(i_{0},j_{0})
). By assumption,
the
\tilde{u}
-th term is good for
\eta^{\prime}
.
Therefore, it follows from our inductive assumption
12
12

12

And specifically by its soft quantitative strengthening, which is a consequence of
[
30
, Proposition A.2]
.

that for a set of
({\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}^{\prime},{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime},{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime})
of positive lower density, the seminorm

\lvert\!|\!|f_{\tilde{u},{\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}^{\prime},{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime},{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime\prime}}|\!|\!\rvert_{{\mathbf{u}}^{\prime}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{u}}^{\prime}_{t^{\prime}},(\hat{a}_{\eta(\tilde{u})}{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta(\tilde{u})})^{\times s^{\prime}}}\\
=\Big|\!\Big|\!\Big|\Delta_{\begin{subarray}{c}({\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{1}m_{1}^{\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{1}m_{1}\right\rfloor},{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{1}m_{1}^{\prime\prime\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{1}m_{1}^{\prime\prime}\right\rfloor}),\ldots,\\
({\left\lfloor{\mathbf{u}}_{k-1}m_{t+k-1}^{\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{u}}_{k-1}m_{t+k-1}\right\rfloor},{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{u}}_{k-1}m_{t+k-1}^{\prime\prime\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{u}}_{k-1}m_{t+k-1}^{\prime\prime}\right\rfloor})\end{subarray}}\overline{f_{\tilde{u}}}\Big|\!\Big|\!\Big|_{{\mathbf{u}}^{\prime}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{u}}^{\prime}_{t^{\prime}},(\hat{a}_{\eta(\tilde{u})}{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta(\tilde{u})})^{\times s^{\prime}}}

is (uniformly) bounded away from 0

(depending only on

) and
{\mathbf{u}}^{\prime}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{u}}^{\prime}_{t^{\prime}}\in{\mathcal{G}}_{\eta^{\prime}}\backslash\{\hat{a}_{\eta(\tilde{u})}{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta(\tilde{u})}\}

Averaging over all
{\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}^{\prime},{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime},{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime}

using the Hölder inequality and applying the inductive formula for generalized box seminorms, we get the auxiliary norm control

\lvert\!|\!|f_{\tilde{u}}|\!|\!\rvert_{(\hat{a}_{\eta(\tilde{u})}{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta(\tilde{u})})^{\times s^{\prime}},{\mathbf{u}}^{\prime}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{u}}^{\prime}_{t^{\prime}},{\mathbf{v}}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{v}}_{t},{\mathbf{u}}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{u}}_{k-1}}>0.

Step 2: Pong.

Suppose that (
25
) is positive.
It follows from
[
16
, Lemma 11.7]
that

\limsup_{N\to\infty}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\mathcal{D}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathcal{D}}_{J}\in{\mathfrak{D}}_{d}\end{subarray}}\sup_{|c_{n}|\leq 1}\lim_{N\to\infty}\Bigl\|\E_{n\in[N]}c_{n}\cdot T_{\eta(\tilde{u})}^{{\left\lfloor\frac{\hat{a}_{\eta(\tilde{u})}}{\hat{a}_{\tilde{u}}}a_{\tilde{u}}(n)\right\rfloor}}\tilde{f}_{\tilde{u}}\cdot\prod_{(i,j)\in I\backslash\{\tilde{u}\}}T_{\eta(i,j)}^{{\left\lfloor\frac{\hat{a}_{\eta(i,j)}}{\hat{a}_{i,j}}a_{i,j}(n)\right\rfloor}}f_{i,j}\\
\prod_{j\in[J]}\mathcal{D}_{j}({\left\lfloor b_{j}(n)\right\rfloor})\Bigr\|_{2}>0

for some
\tilde{f}_{\tilde{u}}
which is the weak limit
\lim\limits_{w\to\infty}A_{N_{w}}
for

(28)

A_{N_{w}}:=\E_{n\in[N_{w}]}\overline{c}^{\prime}_{n}\cdot T_{\eta(\tilde{u})}^{-{\left\lfloor\frac{\hat{a}_{\eta(\tilde{u})}}{\hat{a}_{\tilde{u}}}a_{\tilde{u}}(n)\right\rfloor}}\overline{g}_{w}\cdot\prod_{(i,j)\in I\backslash\{\tilde{u}\}}T_{\eta(i,j)}^{{\left\lfloor\frac{\hat{a}_{\eta(i,j)}}{\hat{a}_{i,j}}a_{i,j}(n)\right\rfloor}}T_{\eta(\tilde{u})}^{-{\left\lfloor\frac{\hat{a}_{\eta(\tilde{u})}}{\hat{a}_{\tilde{u}}}a_{\tilde{u}}(n)\right\rfloor}}\overline{f}_{i,j}\\
\prod_{j\in[J]}T_{\eta(\tilde{u})}^{-{\left\lfloor\frac{\hat{a}_{\eta(\tilde{u})}}{\hat{a}_{\tilde{u}}}a_{\tilde{u}}(n)\right\rfloor}}\overline{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}_{j}({\left\lfloor b_{j}(n)\right\rfloor})

for some increasing sequence
(N_{w})_{w}
, 1-bounded weights
(c^{\prime}_{n})_{n}
, 1-bounded function
g_{w}
, and dual sequences
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{J}\in\mathfrak{D}_{d}
.
The seminorm control established in the Ping step then gives

\lvert\!|\!|\tilde{f}_{\tilde{u}}|\!|\!\rvert_{(\hat{a}_{\eta(\tilde{u})}{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta(\tilde{u})})^{\times s^{\prime}},{\mathbf{u}}^{\prime}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{u}}^{\prime}_{t^{\prime}},{\mathbf{v}}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{v}}_{t},{\mathbf{u}}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{u}}_{k-1}}>0.

We unpack this seminorm using
[
16
, Lemma 11.11]
and the formula (
28
) for
\tilde{f}_{\tilde{u}}
; after composing the resulting expression with
T_{\eta(\tilde{u})}^{{\left\lfloor\frac{\hat{a}_{\eta(\tilde{u})}}{\hat{a}_{\tilde{u}}}a_{\tilde{u}}(n)\right\rfloor}}
and applying the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, we have that

\liminf_{M\to\infty}\E_{{\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}^{\prime},{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime},{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime\prime}\in[\pm M]^{t+t^{\prime}+k-1}}\limsup_{N\to\infty}\sup_{|c_{n}|\leq 1}\Bigl\|\E_{n\in[N]}c_{n}\cdot T_{\eta(\tilde{u})}^{{\left\lfloor\frac{\hat{a}_{\eta(\tilde{u})}}{\hat{a}_{\tilde{u}}}a_{\tilde{u}}(n)\right\rfloor}}h_{{\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}^{\prime},{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime},{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime\prime}}\\
\prod_{(i,j)\in I\backslash\{\tilde{u}\}}T_{\eta(i,j)}^{{\left\lfloor\frac{\hat{a}_{\eta(i,j)}}{\hat{a}_{i,j}}a_{i,j}(n)\right\rfloor}}f_{i,j,{\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}^{\prime},{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime},{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime\prime}}\cdot\prod_{j\in[J]}{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}_{j,{\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}^{\prime},{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime},{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime\prime}}({\left\lfloor b_{j}(n)\right\rfloor})\Bigr\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}>0,

where

\displaystyle f_{i,j,{\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}^{\prime},{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime},{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime\prime}}

\displaystyle=\Delta_{\begin{subarray}{c}({\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{1}m_{1}^{\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{1}m_{1}\right\rfloor},{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{1}m_{1}^{\prime\prime\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{1}m_{1}^{\prime\prime}\right\rfloor}),\ldots,\\
({\left\lfloor{\mathbf{u}}_{k-1}m_{t+t^{\prime}+k-1}^{\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{u}}_{k-1}m_{t+t^{\prime}+k-1}\right\rfloor},{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{u}}_{k-1}m_{t+t^{\prime}+k-1}^{\prime\prime\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{u}}_{k-1}m_{t+t^{\prime}+k-1}^{\prime\prime}\right\rfloor})\end{subarray}}\overline{f_{i,j}},

\displaystyle{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}_{j,{\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}^{\prime},{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime},{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime\prime}}

\displaystyle=\Delta_{\begin{subarray}{c}({\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{1}m_{1}^{\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{1}m_{1}\right\rfloor},{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{1}m_{1}^{\prime\prime\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{1}m_{1}^{\prime\prime}\right\rfloor}),\ldots,\\
({\left\lfloor{\mathbf{u}}_{k-1}m_{t+t^{\prime}+k-1}^{\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{u}}_{k-1}m_{t+t^{\prime}+k-1}\right\rfloor},{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{u}}_{k-1}m_{t+t^{\prime}+k-1}^{\prime\prime\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{u}}_{k-1}m_{t+t^{\prime}+k-1}^{\prime\prime}\right\rfloor})\end{subarray}}\overline{{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}_{j}},

and each
h_{{\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}^{\prime},{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime},{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime\prime}}
is a product of
2^{t+t^{\prime}+k-1}
1-bounded dual functions of level
s^{\prime}
with respect to
T_{\eta(\tilde{u})}
.
By induction on
k^{\prime}
, for a set of
({\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}^{\prime},{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime},{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime})
of positive lower density, we have that

\lvert\!|\!|f_{1,1,{\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}^{\prime},{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime},{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime\prime}}|\!|\!\rvert_{{\mathbf{u}}^{\prime\prime}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{u}}^{\prime\prime}_{t^{\prime\prime}}}\\
=\Big|\!\Big|\!\Big|\Delta_{\begin{subarray}{c}({\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{1}m_{1}^{\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{1}m_{1}\right\rfloor},{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{1}m_{1}^{\prime\prime\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{v}}_{1}m_{1}^{\prime\prime}\right\rfloor}),\ldots,\\
({\left\lfloor{\mathbf{u}}_{k-1}m_{t+t^{\prime}+k-1}^{\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{u}}_{k-1}m_{t+t^{\prime}+k-1}\right\rfloor},{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{u}}_{k-1}m_{t+t^{\prime}+k-1}^{\prime\prime\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{u}}_{k-1}m_{t+t^{\prime}+k-1}^{\prime\prime}\right\rfloor})\end{subarray}}\overline{f_{1,1}}\Big|\!\Big|\!\Big|_{{\mathbf{u}}^{\prime\prime}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{u}}^{\prime\prime}_{t^{\prime\prime}}}

is (uniformly) bounded away from 0
for some
t^{\prime\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}
(depending only on
d,J,\ell,r^{\prime},{\mathcal{Q}}
) and
{\mathbf{u}}^{\prime\prime}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{u}}^{\prime\prime}_{t^{\prime\prime}}
belonging to the set
{\mathcal{G}}_{\eta}
.
13
13

13

Once again, we use here
[
30
, Proposition A.2]
to upgrade qualitative seminorm control to a soft quantitative estimate.

Averaging over all
{\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}^{\prime},{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime},{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime}
, raising it to the power
2^{t^{\prime\prime}}
, using the Hölder inequality and applying the inductive formula for generalized box seminorms, we get the auxiliary norm control

\lvert\!|\!|f_{1,1}|\!|\!\rvert_{{\mathbf{v}}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{v}}_{t},{\mathbf{u}}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{u}}_{k-1},{\mathbf{u}}^{\prime}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{u}}^{\prime}_{t^{\prime}},{\mathbf{u}}^{\prime\prime}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{u}}^{\prime\prime}_{t^{\prime\prime}}}>0.

In conclusion, we have that (
25
) is zero if
\lvert\!|\!|f_{1,1}|\!|\!\rvert_{{\mathbf{v}}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{v}}_{t},{\mathbf{u}}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{u}}_{k-1},{\mathbf{u}}^{\prime}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{u}}^{\prime}_{t^{\prime}},{\mathbf{u}}^{\prime\prime}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{u}}^{\prime\prime}_{t^{\prime\prime}}}=0
.

Step 3: Relative concatenation.

Combining our original seminorm control with the conclusion of the Pong step and Lemma
2.1
, we deduce that
(
25
) is zero if

\displaystyle\min(\lvert\!|\!|f_{1,1}|\!|\!\rvert^{+}_{{\mathbf{v}}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{v}}_{t},{\mathbf{u}}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{u}}_{k}},\lvert\!|\!|f_{1,1}|\!|\!\rvert^{+}_{{\mathbf{v}}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{v}}_{t},{\mathbf{u}}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{u}}_{k-1},{\mathbf{u}}^{\prime}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{u}}^{\prime}_{t^{\prime}},{\mathbf{u}}^{\prime\prime}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{u}}^{\prime\prime}_{t^{\prime\prime}}})=0.

We want to combine the previous estimates to show that (
25
) is 0 if

(29)

\lvert\!|\!|f_{1,1}|\!|\!\rvert^{+}_{{\mathbf{v}}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{v}}_{t},{\mathbf{u}}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{u}}_{k-1},\{\langle{\mathbf{u}}_{k},{\mathbf{u}}\rangle:{\mathbf{u}}\in\mathcal{R}\}}=0

where
\mathcal{R}
is the multi-set
\{{\mathbf{u}}^{\prime}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{u}}^{\prime}_{t^{\prime}},{\mathbf{u}}^{\prime\prime}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{u}}^{\prime\prime}_{t^{\prime\prime}}\}
. Using Proposition
2.13

and Corollary
3.2
, we decompose
f=g_{1}+g_{2}
where
g_{1},g_{2}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
are 1-bounded, and

\displaystyle\lvert\!|\!|g_{1}|\!|\!\rvert^{+}_{{\mathbf{v}}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{v}}_{t},{\mathbf{u}}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{u}}_{k-1},{\mathbf{u}}^{\prime}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{u}}^{\prime}_{t^{\prime}},{\mathbf{u}}^{\prime\prime}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{u}}^{\prime\prime}_{t^{\prime\prime}}}=\lvert\!|\!|g_{2}|\!|\!\rvert^{+}_{{\mathbf{v}}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{v}}_{t},{\mathbf{u}}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{u}}_{k}}=0.

Hence the contribution of
g_{1}
to (
25
) vanishes because of the seminorm control established in the Pong step while the contribution of
g_{2}
is 0 due to the original seminorm control. It follows by the triangle inequality that (
25
) is 0 as well, and so the seminorm in (
29
) controls (
25
).

Recall that
{\mathbf{u}}^{\prime}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{u}}^{\prime}_{t^{\prime}}\in{\mathcal{G}}_{\eta^{\prime}}\backslash\{\hat{a}_{\eta(\tilde{u})}{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta(\tilde{u})}\}
and
{\mathbf{u}}^{\prime\prime}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{u}}^{\prime\prime}_{t^{\prime\prime}}\in{\mathcal{G}}_{\eta}
. Hence every element
{\mathbf{u}}\in\mathcal{R}
lies either in
{\mathcal{G}}_{\eta}
or in
{\mathcal{G}}_{\eta^{\prime}}\backslash({\mathcal{G}}_{\eta}\cup\{\hat{a}_{\eta(\tilde{u})}{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta(\tilde{u})}\})
. In the latter case, it takes the form

\hat{a}_{\eta^{\prime}(1,1)}{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta^{\prime}(1,1)}-\hat{a}_{\eta^{\prime}(1,j)}{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta^{\prime}(1,j)}=\hat{a}_{\eta(i_{0},j_{0})}{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta(i_{0},j_{0})}-\hat{a}_{\eta(1,j)}{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta(1,j)}

for some
2\leq j\leq\ell_{1}
. Recalling that
{\mathbf{u}}_{k}=\hat{a}_{\eta(1,1)}{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta(1,1)}-\hat{a}_{\eta(i_{0},j_{0})}{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta(i_{0},j_{0})}
, we deduce that the group
\langle{\mathbf{u}}_{k},{\mathbf{u}}\rangle
contains the element
\hat{a}_{\eta(1,1)}{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta(1,1)}-\hat{a}_{\eta(1,j)}{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta(1,j)}
which belongs to
{\mathcal{G}}_{\eta}
.
In conclusion, it follows from (
29
) that (
25
) is zero if

\lvert\!|\!|f_{1,1}|\!|\!\rvert^{+}_{{{\mathbf{v}}}_{1},\dots,{{\mathbf{v}}}_{t^{\prime\prime}},{\mathbf{u}}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{u}}_{k-1}}=0

for some
t^{\prime\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
{\mathbf{v}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{v}}_{t^{\prime\prime}}\in{\mathcal{G}}_{\eta}
.
By Lemma
2.1
, we have that (
25
) is zero if

\lvert\!|\!|f_{1,1}|\!|\!\rvert_{{\mathbf{v}}_{1},\dots,{{\mathbf{v}}}_{t^{\prime\prime}},{\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{u}}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{u}}_{k-1}}=0,

for any
{\mathbf{v}}\in{\mathcal{G}}_{\eta}
.
So Property
k-1
holds and we are done.

Case 2:
a_{1,1}
does not have the maximum degree.

We now drop the assumption that
a_{1,1}
has maximum degree.
Suppose that (
16
) is positive. By the previous case, Proposition
4.11
holds when
\ell_{1}+\dots+\ell_{r^{\prime}}=1
. We now induct on
\ell^{\prime}
. Suppose that Proposition
4.11
holds when
\ell_{1}+\dots+\ell_{r^{\prime}}=\ell^{\prime}-1
for some
\ell^{\prime}\geq 2
. We show that it also holds when
\ell_{1}+\dots+\ell_{r^{\prime}}=\ell^{\prime}
.
If
a_{1,1}
has maximum degree, then the conclusion follows from the previous case. If not, then we may assume without loss of generality that
a_{2,1}
has maximum degree. Denote
\tilde{u}:=(2,1)
.
It follows from
[
16
, Lemma 11.7]
that

\limsup_{N\to\infty}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\mathcal{D}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathcal{D}}_{J}\in{\mathfrak{D}}_{d}\end{subarray}}\sup_{|c_{n}|\leq 1}\lim_{N\to\infty}\Bigl\|\E_{n\in[N]}c_{n}\cdot T_{\tilde{u}}^{{\left\lfloor a_{\tilde{u}}(n)\right\rfloor}}\tilde{f}_{\tilde{u}}\cdot\prod_{(i,j)\in I\backslash\{\tilde{u}\}}T_{i,j}^{{\left\lfloor a_{i,j}(n)\right\rfloor}}f_{i,j}\\
\prod_{j\in[J]}\mathcal{D}_{j}({\left\lfloor b_{j}(n)\right\rfloor})\Bigr\|_{2}>0

for some
\tilde{f}_{\tilde{u}}
which is the weak limit
\lim\limits_{w\to\infty}A_{N_{w}}
for

(30)

A_{N_{w}}:=\E_{n\in[N_{w}]}\overline{c}^{\prime}_{n}\cdot T_{\tilde{u}}^{-{\left\lfloor a_{\tilde{u}}(n)\right\rfloor}}\overline{g}_{w}\cdot\prod_{(i,j)\in I\backslash\{\tilde{u}\}}T_{i,j}^{{\left\lfloor a_{i,j}(n)\right\rfloor}}T_{\tilde{u}}^{-{\left\lfloor a_{\tilde{u}}(n)\right\rfloor}}\overline{f}_{i,j}\\
