\prod_{j\in[J]}T_{\tilde{u}}^{-{\left\lfloor a_{\tilde{u}}(n)\right\rfloor}}\overline{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}_{j}({\left\lfloor b_{j}(n)\right\rfloor})

for some increasing sequence
(N_{w})_{w}
, 1-bounded weights
(c^{\prime}_{n})_{n}
, 1-bounded function
g_{w}
, and dual sequences
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{J}\in\mathfrak{D}_{d}
.
Since
a_{2,1}
has maximum degree, we can apply the previous case to conclude that
\lvert\!|\!|\tilde{f}_{\tilde{u}}|\!|\!\rvert_{(\hat{a}_{\tilde{u}}{\mathbf{e}}_{\tilde{u}})^{\times s^{\prime}},{\mathbf{u}}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{u}}_{t}}>0
for some
t\in{\mathbb{N}}
(depending only on
d,J,\ell,{\mathcal{Q}}
) and some
{\mathbf{u}}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{u}}_{t}\in{\mathcal{G}}
.
14
14

14

In fact, the previous case implies that
{\mathbf{u}}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{u}}_{t}
necessarily take the form
\hat{a}_{i,j}{\mathbf{e}}_{i,j}-\hat{a}_{i,j^{\prime}}{\mathbf{e}}_{i,j^{\prime}}
with (
1\leq j<j^{\prime}\leq\ell_{i}
) for those
i\in[r^{\prime}]
for which
a_{i,j},a_{i,j^{\prime}}
have the highest degree; this is a subset of
{\mathcal{G}}
.

We then unpack this seminorm using
[
16
, Lemma 11.11]
and the formula (
28
) for
\tilde{f}_{\tilde{u}}
; after composing the resulting expression with
T_{\tilde{u}}^{{\left\lfloor a_{\tilde{u}}(n)\right\rfloor}}
and applying the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, we have that

\liminf_{M\to\infty}\E_{{\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}^{\prime},{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime},{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime\prime}\in[\pm M]^{t}}\limsup_{N\to\infty}\sup_{|c_{n}|\leq 1}\Bigl\|\E_{n\in[N]}c_{n}\cdot T_{\tilde{u}}^{{\left\lfloor a_{\tilde{u}}(n)\right\rfloor}}h_{{\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}^{\prime},{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime},{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime\prime}}\\
\prod_{(i,j)\in I\backslash\{\tilde{u}\}}T_{i,j}^{{\left\lfloor a_{i,j}(n)\right\rfloor}}f_{i,j,{\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}^{\prime},{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime},{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime\prime}}\cdot\prod_{j\in[J]}{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}_{j,{\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}^{\prime},{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime},{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime\prime}}({\left\lfloor b_{j}(n)\right\rfloor})\Bigr\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}>0,

where

\displaystyle f_{i,j,{\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}^{\prime},{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime},{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime\prime}}

\displaystyle=\Delta_{\begin{subarray}{c}({\left\lfloor{\mathbf{u}}_{1}m_{1}^{\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{u}}_{1}m_{1}\right\rfloor},{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{u}}_{1}m_{1}^{\prime\prime\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{u}}_{1}m_{1}^{\prime\prime}\right\rfloor}),\ldots,\\
({\left\lfloor{\mathbf{u}}_{t}m_{t}^{\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{u}}_{t}m_{t}\right\rfloor},{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{u}}_{t}m_{t}^{\prime\prime\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{u}}_{t}m_{t}^{\prime\prime}\right\rfloor})\end{subarray}}\overline{f_{i,j}},

\displaystyle{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}_{j,{\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}^{\prime},{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime},{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime\prime}}

\displaystyle=\Delta_{\begin{subarray}{c}({\left\lfloor{\mathbf{u}}_{1}m_{1}^{\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{u}}_{1}m_{1}\right\rfloor},{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{u}}_{1}m_{1}^{\prime\prime\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{u}}_{1}m_{1}^{\prime\prime}\right\rfloor}),\ldots,\\
({\left\lfloor{\mathbf{u}}_{t}m_{t}^{\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{u}}_{t}m_{t}\right\rfloor},{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{u}}_{t}m_{t}^{\prime\prime\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{u}}_{t}m_{t}^{\prime\prime}\right\rfloor})\end{subarray}}\overline{{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}_{j}},

and each
h_{{\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}^{\prime},{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime},{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime\prime}}
is a product of
2^{t}
1-bounded dual functions of level
s^{\prime}
with respect to
T_{\tilde{u}}
.
Since this average involves only
\ell^{\prime}-1
terms of degree greater than or equal to
\deg a_{1,1}
, we can apply (the soft quantitative version of) the induction hypothesis to conclude that for a set of
({\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}^{\prime},{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime},{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime})
of positive lower density, the seminorm

\lvert\!|\!|f_{1,1,{\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}^{\prime},{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime},{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime\prime}}|\!|\!\rvert_{{\mathbf{u}}^{\prime}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{u}}^{\prime}_{t^{\prime}}}=\Big|\!\Big|\!\Big|\Delta_{\begin{subarray}{c}({\left\lfloor{\mathbf{u}}_{1}m_{1}^{\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{u}}_{1}m_{1}\right\rfloor},{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{u}}_{1}m_{1}^{\prime\prime\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{u}}_{1}m_{1}^{\prime\prime}\right\rfloor}),\ldots,\\
({\left\lfloor{\mathbf{u}}_{t}m_{t}^{\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{u}}_{t}m_{t}\right\rfloor},{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{u}}_{t}m_{t}^{\prime\prime\prime}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor{\mathbf{u}}_{t}m_{t}^{\prime\prime}\right\rfloor})\end{subarray}}\overline{f_{1,1}}\Big|\!\Big|\!\Big|_{{\mathbf{u}}^{\prime}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{u}}^{\prime}_{t^{\prime}}}

is (uniformly) bounded away from 0
for some
t^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}
(depending only on
d,J,\ell,{\mathcal{Q}}
) and
{\mathbf{u}}^{\prime}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{u}}^{\prime}_{t^{\prime}}
belonging to the set
{\mathcal{G}}
.
Averaging over all
{\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}^{\prime},{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime},{\underline{m}}^{\prime\prime}
, raising it to the power
2^{t^{\prime}}
using the Hölder inequality and applying the inductive formula for generalized box seminorms, we get the claimed norm control

\lvert\!|\!|f_{1,1}|\!|\!\rvert_{{\mathbf{u}}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{u}}_{t},{\mathbf{u}}^{\prime}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{u}}^{\prime}_{t^{\prime}}}>0

by a seminorm with all vectors lying in
{\mathcal{G}}
.

5.
Seminorm comparison under ergodicity assumptions I: preliminaries

From now on, our goal is to use the difference ergodicity condition to infer the following result, which is crucial for ensuring the Host-Kra seminorm control of our averages.

Proposition 5.1

(Seminorm comparison)
.

Let
\ell\in{\mathbb{N}},s\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
, and let
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
be a system.
Let
a_{1},a_{2}\in{\mathcal{H}}

and suppose that there exist nonzero
\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\in{\mathbb{R}}
for which
\beta_{2}a_{1}-\beta_{1}a_{2}\ll\log
. Suppose moreover that
(T_{1}^{{\left\lfloor a_{1}(n)\right\rfloor}}T_{2}^{-{\left\lfloor a_{2}(n)\right\rfloor}})_{n}
is ergodic for
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu)
. Then for any
f\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
, the following holds:

(i)

\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{\langle\beta_{1}{\mathbf{e}}_{1}-\beta_{2}{\mathbf{e}}_{2}\rangle}=0
whenever
\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{{\mathbb{Z}}^{\ell},\langle{\mathbf{e}}_{1},{\mathbf{e}}_{2}\rangle}^{+}=0
;

(ii)

if
s\geq 1
and
G_{1},\ldots,G_{s}\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{\ell}
are finitely generated, then

\displaystyle\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s},\langle\beta_{1}{\mathbf{e}}_{1}-\beta_{2}{\mathbf{e}}_{2}\rangle}=0\quad\textrm{whenever}\quad\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s},\langle{\mathbf{e}}_{1},{\mathbf{e}}_{2}\rangle}^{+}=0.

Proposition
5.1
needs no lower bound on the growth of
a_{1},a_{2}
. In the degenerate case when they are both slower than log, the ergodicity assumption forces
\mu
to be the Dirac measure
\delta_{x}
at some
x\in X
and
T_{1}x=T_{2}x=x
(see Lemma
7.1
for a related computation).

The proof of Proposition
5.1
will be broken down into several cases depending on the rationality or irrationality of
\beta_{1}/\beta_{2}
as well as the properties of
a_{1},a_{2}
. Its first step is the following reduction which allows us to simplify the iterates appearing in the difference ergodicity condition.

Proposition 5.2
.

Let
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},T_{2})
be a system. Let
a_{1},a_{2}\in{\mathcal{H}}
,
and suppose that there exist nonzero
\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\in{\mathbb{R}}
for which
\beta_{2}a_{1}-\beta_{1}a_{2}\ll\log
. Suppose moreover that
(T_{1}^{{\left\lfloor a_{1}(n)\right\rfloor}}T_{2}^{-{\left\lfloor a_{2}(n)\right\rfloor}})_{n}
is ergodic for
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu)
.
Then there exist
u\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
a\in{\mathcal{H}}
, which is either in
{\mathbb{R}}[x]
or stays logarithmically away from
{\mathbb{R}}[x]
, such that
(T_{1}^{{\left\lfloor{a}(n)\right\rfloor}}T_{2}^{-{\left\lfloor\beta{a}(n)+u\right\rfloor}})_{n}
is also ergodic for
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu)
, where
\beta=\beta_{2}/\beta_{1}
.

In other words, Proposition
5.2
allows us to cast away several (partially overlapping) cases:

(i)

1\prec\beta_{2}a_{1}-\beta_{1}a_{2}\ll\log
, i.e.
a_{2}=(\beta_{2}/\beta_{1})a_{1}+g
for some
1\prec g\ll\log
;

(ii)

\beta_{2}a_{1}-\beta_{1}a_{2}
is not constant but converges to a constant, i.e.
a_{2}=(\beta_{2}/\beta_{1})a_{1}+u+g
, where
g
is nonconstant but goes to 0;

(iii)

a_{1}=a+g_{1}
and
a_{2}=\beta a+u+g_{2}
for some
a\in{\mathbb{R}}[x]
,
u\in{\mathbb{R}}
, and nonconstant
g_{i}
, each of which either goes to 0 or satisfies
1\prec g_{i}\ll\log
.

Proposition
5.2
will be proved in Section
5.1
.

The gist of the proof of Proposition
5.1
consists in obtaining the following result, which one should (morally) think of as the
s=0
case of Proposition
5.1
.

Proposition 5.3
.

Let
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},T_{2})
be a system.
Let
u\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
a\in{\mathcal{H}}
, where
a
is either in
{\mathbb{R}}[x]
or stays logarithmically away from
{\mathbb{R}}[x]
.
Suppose that
(T_{1}^{{\left\lfloor a(n)\right\rfloor}}T_{2}^{-{\left\lfloor\beta a(n)+u\right\rfloor}})_{n}
is ergodic for
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu)
. Then for any
f\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
, we have

\displaystyle\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{{\mathbf{e}}_{1}-\beta{\mathbf{e}}_{2}}=0\quad\textrm{whenever}\quad\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{\langle{\mathbf{e}}_{1},{\mathbf{e}}_{2}\rangle}^{+}=0.

The proof of Proposition
5.1
proceeds rather differently depending on whether
\beta
is rational or irrational. These two cases are covered separately in Corollary
5.10
and Proposition
6.1
, and their proofs occupy Sections
5.2
and
6
respectively.

Proof of Proposition
5.1
assuming Proposition
5.3
.

Let
a_{1},a_{2}\in{\mathcal{H}}
be two sequences such that
\beta_{2}a_{1}-\beta_{1}a_{2}\ll\log
for some nonzero
\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\in{\mathbb{R}}
, and suppose that
(T_{1}^{{\left\lfloor a_{1}(n)\right\rfloor}}T_{2}^{-{\left\lfloor a_{2}(n)\right\rfloor}})_{n}
is ergodic for
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu)
. Let
\beta=\beta_{2}/\beta_{1}
. By Proposition
5.2
, there exist a number
u\in{\mathbb{R}}
and a sequence
a\in{\mathcal{H}}
, which is either in
{\mathbb{R}}[x]
or stays logarithmically away from
{\mathbb{R}}[x]
, such that
(T_{1}^{{\left\lfloor a(n)\right\rfloor}}T_{2}^{-{\left\lfloor\beta a(n)+u\right\rfloor}})_{n}
is ergodic for
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu)
. By
[
30
, Proposition A.2]
, we obtain the following soft quantitative strengthening of Proposition
5.3
: for every
\varepsilon>0
, we can find
\delta>0
such that for any
f\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
,

(31)

\displaystyle\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{{\mathbf{e}}_{1}-\beta{\mathbf{e}}_{2}}\leq\varepsilon\quad\textrm{whenever}\quad\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{\langle{\mathbf{e}}_{1},{\mathbf{e}}_{2}\rangle}^{+}\leq\delta.

Importantly, the parameter
\delta
is allowed to depend on the system and
\beta
, but is uniform in the choice of
f\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
. We will use (
31
) in conjunction with the basic properties of seminorms in order to derive Proposition
5.1
.

Suppose first that
s\geq 1
. Lemma
2.1
gives the upper bound

\displaystyle\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s},\langle\beta_{1}{\mathbf{e}}_{1}-\beta_{2}{\mathbf{e}}_{2}\rangle}\ll_{\beta}\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s},\langle{\mathbf{e}}_{1}-\beta{\mathbf{e}}_{2}\rangle}.

To show Proposition
5.1
, it suffices to show that the rightmost seminorm vanishes whenever
\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s},\langle{\mathbf{e}}_{1},{\mathbf{e}}_{2}\rangle}^{+}=0.

Suppose then that
\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s},\langle{\mathbf{e}}_{1},{\mathbf{e}}_{2}\rangle}^{+}=0
; setting

\displaystyle G=G_{1}\times\cdots\times G_{s},\quad\textrm{and}\quad{\mathbf{m}}=({\mathbf{m}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{m}}_{s})\quad\textrm{for}\quad{\mathbf{m}}_{i}\in G_{i},

we then have

\displaystyle\E_{({\mathbf{m}},{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime})\in G\times G}\mathopen{}\left(\lvert\!|\!|\Delta_{({\mathbf{m}}_{1},{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime}_{1}),\ldots,({\mathbf{m}}_{s},{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime}_{s})}f|\!|\!\rvert^{+}_{\langle{\mathbf{e}}_{1},{\mathbf{e}}_{2}\rangle}\mathclose{}\right)^{2^{s+1}}=0.

Let
\varepsilon>0
, and let
\delta>0
be the parameter satisfying (
31
). The set of
({\mathbf{m}},{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime})\in G\times G
satisfying

\displaystyle\lvert\!|\!|\Delta_{({\mathbf{m}}_{1},{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime}_{1}),\ldots,({\mathbf{m}}_{s},{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime}_{s})}f|\!|\!\rvert^{+}_{\langle{\mathbf{e}}_{1},{\mathbf{e}}_{2}\rangle}>\delta

has 0 density with respect to any Følner sequence on
G\times G
.
For all the rest, (
31
) gives

\displaystyle\lvert\!|\!|\Delta_{({\mathbf{m}}_{1},{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime}_{1}),\ldots,({\mathbf{m}}_{s},{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime}_{s})}f|\!|\!\rvert_{{\mathbf{e}}_{1}-\beta{\mathbf{e}}_{2}}\leq\varepsilon.

Hence

\displaystyle\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{s},\langle{\mathbf{e}}_{1}-\beta{\mathbf{e}}_{2}\rangle}^{2^{s+1}}=\E_{({\mathbf{m}},{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime})\in G\times G}\lvert\!|\!|\Delta_{({\mathbf{m}}_{1},{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime}_{1}),\ldots,({\mathbf{m}}_{s},{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime}_{s})}f|\!|\!\rvert_{{\mathbf{e}}_{1}-\beta{\mathbf{e}}_{2}}^{2^{s}}\leq\varepsilon^{2^{s}},

and the claim follows on taking
\varepsilon\to 0
.

For the case
s=0
, Lemma
2.1
gives

\displaystyle\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{\langle\beta_{1}{\mathbf{e}}_{1}-\beta_{2}{\mathbf{e}}_{2}\rangle}\ll_{\beta}\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert^{+}_{\langle{\mathbf{e}}_{1}-\beta{\mathbf{e}}_{2}\rangle}\leq\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{{\mathbb{Z}}^{\ell},\langle{\mathbf{e}}_{1}-\beta{\mathbf{e}}_{2}\rangle}.

The previous argument shows that the rightmost seminorm vanishes if
\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{{\mathbb{Z}}^{\ell},\langle{\mathbf{e}}_{1},{\mathbf{e}}_{2}\rangle}^{+}=0
(by substituting
G
with
{\mathbb{Z}}^{\ell}
).
∎

5.1.
Preliminary reduction

This section is devoted to the proof Proposition
5.2
, a preliminary reduction in the proof of Proposition
5.1
. We begin with an auxiliary result on the density of some Hardy orbits on certain subtori.

Lemma 5.4
.

Let
\ell,m\in{\mathbb{N}}
. Let
g_{1},\ldots,g_{m}\in{\mathcal{H}}
with
1\prec g_{1}(x)\prec\cdots\prec g_{m}(x)\prec x
, and define

\displaystyle a_{j}(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\alpha_{j,i}g_{i}(x)+\alpha_{j0}+r_{j}(x),

\displaystyle b_{j}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{m})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\alpha_{j,i}x_{i}+\alpha_{j0}

for all
j\in[\ell]
, some
\alpha_{j,i}\in{\mathbb{R}}
, and some
r_{j}\in{\mathcal{H}}
with
\lim\limits_{x\to\infty}r_{j}(x)=0
.
Then

(\{a_{1}(n)\},\ldots,\{a_{\ell}(n)\})_{n}

is dense in

\displaystyle Z=\overline{\{(\{b_{1}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{m})\},\ldots,\{b_{\ell}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{m})\}):\;x_{1},\ldots,x_{m}\in{\mathbb{R}}\}},

which is an affine subtorus of
{\mathbb{T}}^{\ell}
.
15
15

15

I.e. a set
{\mathbf{u}}+H\subseteq{\mathbb{T}}^{\ell}
for some compact (not necessarily connected) subgroup
H\subseteq{\mathbb{T}}^{\ell}
.

Note that because of possible rational dependencies between the coefficients
\alpha_{j,i}
, it is easier to describe the subtorus less explicitly (as we did above) than try to explicitly list its continuous generators.

Proof.

Let
{\mathbf{u}}=(\alpha_{10},\ldots,\alpha_{\ell 0})
and
\Lambda\subseteq{\mathbb{Z}}^{\ell}
be the subgroup of frequencies defining
Z
via

\displaystyle Z=\{{\mathbf{z}}\in{\mathbb{T}}^{\ell}:\;{\mathbf{k}}\cdot({\mathbf{z}}-{\mathbf{u}})\in{\mathbb{Z}}\;\;\textrm{for\; all}\;\;{\mathbf{k}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{\ell}\}

(note that
{\mathbf{k}}\cdot{\mathbf{z}}:=k_{1}z_{1}+\cdots+k_{\ell}z_{\ell}
is well-defined for any
{\mathbf{k}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{\ell}
and
{\mathbf{z}}\in{\mathbb{T}}^{\ell}
).
Take a function
W\in{\mathcal{H}}
with growth
1\prec W(x)\ll x
satisfying

1\prec\log W(x)\prec a_{j}(x)\prec x

for all
j\neq\ell
for which
a_{j}\succ 1
(the simplest such example would be
W(x)=g_{1}(x)
). Then, using
[
43
, Theorem 5.1]
, these growth inequalities yield

\lim\limits_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{W(N)}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\left(W(n+1)-W(n)\right)e\left(k_{1}a_{1}(n)+\cdots+k_{\ell}a_{\ell}(n)\right)=0

for all
{\mathbf{k}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{\ell}\setminus{\Lambda}
. In the language of
[
43
]
, this means that
(\{a_{1}(n)\},\ldots,\{a_{\ell}(n)\})_{n}
is equidistributed on
Z
with respect to the
W
-averaging scheme above.

A standard variation of Weyl’s equidistribution theorem implies that for any Riemann integrable function
f:Z\to{\mathbb{C}}
, we get

\lim\limits_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{W(N)}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\left(W(n+1)-W(n)\right)F\left(\{a_{1}(n)\},\ldots,\{a_{\ell}(n)\}\right)=\int F(x)\,d\mu_{Z}(x),

where
\mu_{Z}
is the Haar measure on
Z
.
For any (nonempty) open ball
U\subseteq Z
, the previous asymptotic implies that

\frac{1}{W(N)}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\left(W(n+1)-W(n)\right)1_{U}\left(\{a_{1}(n)\},\ldots,\{a_{\ell}(n)\}\right)=\mu_{Z}(U)>0,

and the result follows.
∎

All the information that we actually need is captured in the following corollary, which is an easy consequence of Lemma
5.4
and a simple decomposition result for Hardy sequences
[
43
, Lemma A.3]
.

Corollary 5.5
.

Let
\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}
, and let
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\in{\mathcal{H}}
with
a_{j}(x)\prec x
for each
j\in[\ell]
. Then

(\{a_{1}(n)\},\ldots,\{a_{\ell}(n)\})_{n}

is dense in an affine subtorus of
{\mathbb{T}}^{\ell}
.

We also need one more convergence result, an easy corollary of the convergence results of Boshernitzan, Kolesnik, Quas, and Wierdl
[
13
]
.

Lemma 5.6
.

Let
\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}
. Let
a\in{\mathcal{H}}
, and suppose that
a
is either in
{\mathbb{R}}[x]
or stays logarithmically away from
{\mathbb{R}}[x]
. Then:

(i)

For every
\beta_{1},s_{1},u_{1},\ldots,\beta_{\ell},s_{\ell},u_{\ell}\in{\mathbb{R}}
, the exponential sum

(32)

\displaystyle\E_{n\in[N]}e\mathopen{}\left(s_{1}{\lfloor\beta_{1}a(n)+u_{1}\rfloor}+\cdots+s_{\ell}{\lfloor\beta_{\ell}a(n)+u_{\ell}\rfloor}\mathclose{}\right)

converges as
N\to\infty
.

(ii)

For every system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
and every
f\in L^{2}(\mu)
, the average

\displaystyle\E_{n\in[N]}T_{1}^{{\lfloor\beta_{1}a(n)+u_{1}\rfloor}}\cdots T_{\ell}^{{\lfloor\beta_{\ell}a(n)+u_{\ell}\rfloor}}f

converges in
L^{2}(\mu)
as
N\to\infty
.

Proof.

For the first statement, we first decompose (
32
) as

\displaystyle\E_{n\in[N]}e\mathopen{}\left(sa(n)+u-s_{1}\{\beta_{1}a(n)+u_{1}\}-\cdots-s_{\ell}\{\beta_{\ell}a(n)+u_{\ell}\}\mathclose{}\right)

for
s=s_{1}\beta_{1}+\cdots+s_{\ell}\beta_{\ell}
and
u=s_{1}u_{1}+\cdots+s_{\ell}u_{\ell}
. Since the functions
f_{i}(x)=e(s_{i}\{x\})
are Riemann integrable, we can approximate them arbitrarily well by trigonometric polynomials, and so it suffices to prove the convergence of

\displaystyle\E_{n\in[N]}e\mathopen{}\left(\tilde{s}a(n)+\tilde{u}\mathclose{}\right),

where
\tilde{s}=s+m_{1}\beta_{1}+\cdots+m_{\ell}\beta_{\ell}
and
\tilde{u}=u+m_{1}u_{1}+\cdots+m_{\ell}u_{\ell}
for all choices of
m_{1},\ldots,m_{\ell}\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. This in turn is a straightforward consequence of
[
13
, Theorem A]
. The second part of the lemma follows from the first part and the spectral theorem.
∎

Lastly, we require the following averaging lemma, which shows that Cesàro convergence in Hilbert space implies convergence along dyadic intervals.

Lemma 5.7
.

Let
(a_{N})_{N}
be a sequence whose sequence of ratios
(a_{N+1}/a_{N})_{N}
converges to some number in
(1,\infty]
, and let
(v_{n})_{n}
be a bounded sequence in a Hilbert space. Suppose that
\E\limits_{n\in[N]}v_{n}
converges strongly (resp. weakly) to some limit
L
. Then
\E\limits_{n\in(a_{N},a_{N+1}]}v_{n}
converges strongly (resp. weakly) to
L
as well.

Proof.

We assume that all the limits are strong; the case when they are all weak follows identically.
Let
S_{N}=v_{1}+\dots+v_{N}
. For all sufficiently large
N
, the ratio condition implies that
a_{N+1}>a_{N}
, in which case the average in question equals

\displaystyle\E\limits_{n\in(a_{N},a_{N+1}]}v_{n}=\frac{1}{a_{N+1}-a_{N}}\big(S_{a_{N+1}}-S_{a_{N}}\big)=\frac{a_{N+1}}{a_{N+1}-a_{N}}\mathopen{}\left(\E\limits_{n\in[a_{N+1}]}v_{n}-\frac{a_{N}}{a_{N+1}}\E\limits_{n\in[a_{N}]}v_{n}\mathclose{}\right).

By assumption, we have
\lim\limits_{N\to\infty}\frac{a_{N+1}}{a_{N}}>1
. If the limit is infinite, then
\frac{a_{N+1}}{a_{N+1}-a_{N}}\to 1
, and so

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\E\limits_{n\in(a_{N},a_{N+1}]}v_{n}=\lim_{N\to\infty}\E\limits_{n\in[a_{N+1}]}v_{n}=L.

If
\lim\limits_{N\to\infty}\frac{a_{N+1}}{a_{N}}=1+c
for some
c>0
, then
\frac{a_{N+1}}{a_{N+1}-a_{N}}\to\frac{1+c}{c}
, in which case

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\E\limits_{n\in(a_{N},a_{N+1}]}v_{n}=\frac{1+c}{c}\Bigl(1-\frac{1}{1+c}\Bigr)L=L.

∎

We now proceed to prove Proposition
5.2
.

Proof of Proposition
5.2
.

If
\beta_{2}a_{1}-\beta_{1}a_{2}\ll\log
and
\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\neq 0
, then
(\beta_{2}/\beta_{1})a_{1}-a_{2}\ll\log
as well. To facilitate the notation, we reparametrize
a_{1}=\tilde{a}
,
\beta=\beta_{2}/\beta_{1}
, and
a_{2}=\beta\tilde{a}+\tilde{g}
for some
\tilde{g}\ll\log
. Then our assumption reduces to
(T_{1}^{{\left\lfloor\tilde{a}(n)\right\rfloor}}T_{2}^{-{\left\lfloor\beta\tilde{a}(n)+\tilde{g}(n)\right\rfloor}})_{n}
being ergodic.

We now split
\tilde{a}={a}+{g}
as follows:

(i)

if
\tilde{a}
stays logarithmically away from
{\mathbb{R}}[x]
, then
a=\tilde{a}
and
{g}=0
;

(ii)

if
\tilde{a}=p+{g}
for some
p\in{\mathbb{R}}[x]
and
{g}\ll\log
, then
{a}=p
(the representation is unique up to shifts by constants).

Then we can recast

\displaystyle T_{1}^{{\left\lfloor\tilde{a}(n)\right\rfloor}}T_{2}^{-{\left\lfloor\beta\tilde{a}(n)+\tilde{g}(n)\right\rfloor}}=T_{1}^{{\left\lfloor{a}(n)+g_{1}(n)\right\rfloor}}T_{2}^{-{\left\lfloor\beta{a}(n)+g_{2}(n)\right\rfloor}}

for
g_{1}={g}
and
g_{2}=\beta{g}+\tilde{g}
.
The statement of the proposition then amounts to showing that the ergodicity of

(T_{1}^{{\left\lfloor{a}(n)+g_{1}(n)\right\rfloor}}T_{2}^{-{\left\lfloor\beta{a}(n)+g_{2}(n)\right\rfloor}})_{n}

implies the ergodicity of
(T_{1}^{{\left\lfloor{a}(n)\right\rfloor}}T_{2}^{-{\left\lfloor\beta{a}(n)+u\right\rfloor}})_{n}
for some
u\in{\mathbb{R}}
.

We now take a step back and discuss the distribution of
(\{g_{1}(n)\},\{g_{2}(n)\})_{n}
in
{\mathbb{T}}^{2}
, which we will need shortly. By Corollary
5.5
,
(\{g_{1}(n)\},\{g_{2}(n)\})_{n}
is dense in an affine subtorus
Z=(u_{1},u_{2})+H
of
{\mathbb{T}}^{2}
. By absorbing
u_{1}
in the definition of
{a}
, we can replace the shift
(u_{1},u_{2})
by
(0,u)
for some
u\in{\mathbb{R}}
. Then
(\{g_{1}(n)\},\{g_{2}(n)-u\})_{n}
is dense in
H
. Now, one of the two things happens: either
\{g_{1}(n)\}=\{g_{2}(n)-u\}=0
for all sufficiently large
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, or
Z
is 1- or 2-dimensional. In both cases, Corollary
5.5
implies that for all
\delta>0
, we can find an increasing sequence
(N_{k})_{k}
such that
(\{g_{1}(N_{k})\},\{g_{2}(N_{k})-u\})_{k}
lies in
[0,\delta)^{2}
for all
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
.

Let us see how to complete the proof of the proposition using this newly acquired information about the distribution of
(\{g_{1}(n)\},\{g_{2}(n)\})_{n}
in
{\mathbb{T}}^{2}
. We fix a small parameter
\delta>0
.
By the ergodicity assumption, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\E_{n\in[N]}T_{1}^{{\left\lfloor{a}(n)+g_{1}(n)\right\rfloor}}T_{2}^{-{\left\lfloor\beta{a}(n)+g_{2}(n)\right\rfloor}}f=\int f\;d\mu,

and so

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\E_{n\in[N,(1+\delta)N)}T_{1}^{{\left\lfloor{a}(n)+g_{1}(n)\right\rfloor}}T_{2}^{-{\left\lfloor\beta{a}(n)+g_{2}(n)\right\rfloor}}f=\int f\;d\mu

as well by Lemma
5.7
. Now, while integer parts are not additive, we can split

\displaystyle{\left\lfloor{a}(n)+g_{1}(n)\right\rfloor}

\displaystyle={\left\lfloor{a}(n)\right\rfloor}+{\left\lfloor g_{1}(n)\right\rfloor}

\displaystyle{\left\lfloor\beta{a}(n)+g_{2}(n)\right\rfloor}

\displaystyle={\left\lfloor\beta{a}(n)+u\right\rfloor}+{\left\lfloor g_{2}(n)-u\right\rfloor}

unless

(33)

\displaystyle\left\{{a}(n)\right\}>1-\left\{g_{1}(n)\right\}\quad\textrm{or}\quad\left\{\beta{a}(n)+u\right\}>1-\left\{g_{2}(n)-u\right\}.

Our goal is to show that (
33
) happens very rarely.

Because
g_{j}\ll\log
, we have
g_{j}(n)=g_{j}(N)+O(\delta)
for
n\in[N,(1+\delta)N)
, so if the boundary condition (
33
) is satisfied, then we must have

\displaystyle\left\{{a}(n)\right\}>1-\left\{g_{1}(N)\right\}-O(\delta)\quad\textrm{or}\quad\left\{\beta{a}(n)+u\right\}>1-\left\{g_{2}(N)-u\right\}-O(\delta).

By the density considerations above, we can find an increasing sequence
(N_{k})_{k}
such that

(34)

\displaystyle\left\{g_{1}(N_{k})\right\},\left\{g_{2}(N_{k})-u\right\}\leq\delta,

so that (
33
) holds for
n\in[N_{k},(1+\delta)N_{k})
only if

(35)

\displaystyle\min(\left\{{a}(n)\right\},\{\beta{a}(n)+u\})>1-O(\delta).

Now, since
{a}
is either in
{\mathbb{R}}[x]
or stays logarithmically away from
{\mathbb{R}}[x]
, we can deduce that at most a
O(\delta)
-proportion of
n\in[N_{k},(1+\delta)N_{k})
can satisfy (
35
) for all sufficiently small
\delta>0
.
16
16

16

If
a
is in
{\mathbb{R}}[x],
then each of the polynomials
a,\beta a+u
is either in
{\mathbb{Q}}[x]+{\mathbb{R}}
or in its complement. Hence the claim follows by periodicity (for sufficiently
\delta>0
) or by well distribution respectively. If
a
stays logarithmically away from
{\mathbb{R}}[x],
so does
\beta a+u,
and the claim follows by equidistribution and Lemma
5.7
.

Restricting to the sequence
(N_{k})_{k}
, we then have

(36)

\E_{n\in[N_{k},(1+\delta)N_{k})}T_{1}^{{\left\lfloor{a}(n)+g_{1}(n)\right\rfloor}}T_{2}^{-{\left\lfloor\beta{a}(n)+g_{2}(n)\right\rfloor}}f\\
=T_{1}^{{\left\lfloor g_{1}(N_{k})\right\rfloor}}T_{2}^{-{\left\lfloor g_{2}(N_{k})-u\right\rfloor}}\E_{n\in[N_{k},(1+\delta)N_{k})}T_{1}^{{\left\lfloor{a}(n)\right\rfloor}}T_{2}^{-{\left\lfloor\beta{a}(n)+u\right\rfloor}}f+O(\delta).

For reasons that will become clear later on, we want the average on
[N_{k},(1+\delta)N_{k})
to converge along the usual interval
[N]
. Fortunately this is the case, and the
L^{2}(\mu)
limit

(37)

\displaystyle L:=\lim_{N\to\infty}\E\limits_{n\in[N]}T_{1}^{{\left\lfloor{a}(n)\right\rfloor}}T_{2}^{-{\left\lfloor\beta{a}(n)+u\right\rfloor}}f

exists by Lemma
5.6
.
Therefore

\displaystyle\E_{n\in[N_{k},(1+\delta)N_{k})}T_{1}^{{\left\lfloor{a}(n)\right\rfloor}}T_{2}^{-{\left\lfloor\beta{a}(n)+u\right\rfloor}}f\to L

in
L^{2}(\mu)
as
k\to\infty
. If we can show that
L
is constant, then
L=\int L\;d\mu=\int f\;d\mu
, and Proposition
5.2
will follow.

Comparing both sides of (
36
) with the fact that the left-hand side converges to
\int f\;d\mu=\int L\;d\mu
, we get that

\displaystyle\limsup_{k\to\infty}\left\|T_{1}^{{\left\lfloor g_{1}(N_{k})\right\rfloor}}T_{2}^{-{\left\lfloor g_{2}(N_{k})-u\right\rfloor}}\E_{n\in[N_{k},(1+\delta)N_{k})}T_{1}^{{\left\lfloor{a}(n)\right\rfloor}}T_{2}^{-{\left\lfloor\beta{a}(n)+u\right\rfloor}}f-\int L\;d\mu\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\ll\delta.

Using the invariance of measures, we can compose the integral with
T_{1}^{-{\left\lfloor g_{1}(N_{k})+\right\rfloor}}T_{2}^{{\left\lfloor g_{2}(N_{k})-u\right\rfloor}}
and then use (
37
) to get

\displaystyle\left\|L-\int L\;d\mu\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\ll\delta.

Since
\delta>0
was arbitrary, we get that
L=\int L\;d\mu
, implying the result.
∎

5.2.
The case of rational
\beta

The goal now is to prove Proposition
5.3
under the assumption that
\beta\in{\mathbb{Q}}
. This will follow from the more general result presented below.

Proposition 5.8
.

Let
k,l\in\mathbb{Z}\backslash\{0\}
and
u\in{\mathbb{R}}
. Then there exists a finite index subgroup
H\subseteq\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, which contains
(k,l)
and depends only on
k
and
l
, such that for any system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},T_{2})
and any sequence
a\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{R}
, the ergodicity of
(T_{1}^{{\left\lfloor ka(n)\right\rfloor}}T_{2}^{-{\left\lfloor la(n)+u\right\rfloor}})_{n}
implies that
{\mathcal{I}}(H)={\mathcal{I}}(T_{1}^{k}T_{2}^{-l})
.

We emphasize the level of generality of Proposition
5.8
: it works for
any
sequence
a:{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
, not necessarily a Hardy one.

As an immediate consequence of Proposition
5.8
and Lemma
2.1
(v), we get the following corollary.

Corollary 5.9
.

Under the assumptions of Proposition
5.8
, we have
\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{k{\mathbf{e}}_{1}-l{\mathbf{e}}_{2}}=\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{H}
for any
f\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
.

Corollary
5.9
combined with Proposition
5.2
quickly yield Proposition
5.3
whenever
\beta\in{\mathbb{Q}}
, which is restated in the corollary below.

Corollary 5.10
.

Proposition
5.3
holds for nonzero
\beta\in{\mathbb{Q}}
.

More specifically, let
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},T_{2})
be a system, let
u\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
a\in{\mathcal{H}}
, where
a
is either in
{\mathbb{R}}[x]
or stays logarithmically away from
{\mathbb{R}}[x]
, and suppose that
(T_{1}^{{\left\lfloor a(n)\right\rfloor}}T_{2}^{-{\left\lfloor\beta a(n)+u\right\rfloor}})_{n}
is ergodic for
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu)
. Then for any
f\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
, we have

\displaystyle\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{{\mathbf{e}}_{1}-\beta{\mathbf{e}}_{2}}=0\quad\textrm{whenever}\quad\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{\langle{\mathbf{e}}_{1},{\mathbf{e}}_{2}\rangle}^{+}=0.

Proof of Corollary
5.10
assuming Corollary
5.9
.

Let
\beta=l/k\in{\mathbb{Q}}
for nonzero
k,l\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. Then Proposition
5.8
applied to the sequence
(a(n)/k)_{n}
gives a subgroup
H\subseteq\langle{\mathbf{e}}_{1},{\mathbf{e}}_{2}\rangle
of index
O_{\beta}(1)
for which
{\mathcal{I}}(T_{1}^{k}T_{2}^{-l})={\mathcal{I}}(H)
. Then

\displaystyle\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{\langle{\mathbf{e}}_{1},{\mathbf{e}}_{2}\rangle}^{+}\asymp_{\beta}\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{H}^{+}=\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{k{\mathbf{e}}_{1}-l{\mathbf{e}}_{2}}^{+}\asymp_{\beta}\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{{\mathbf{e}}_{1}-\beta{\mathbf{e}}_{2}}^{+}\geq\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{{\mathbf{e}}_{1}-\beta{\mathbf{e}}_{2}}

by (various parts of) Lemma
2.1
, from which the claimed result follows.
∎

We finally proceed to prove Proposition
5.8
.

Proof of Proposition
5.8
.

Rescaling
k,l
and
a
if necessary, we may assume without loss of generality that
k
is coprime to
l
. Let
I_{1},\ldots,I_{K}
be the finite partition of
[0,1)
into all the intervals with endpoints from the set

(38)

\displaystyle\Bigl\{\frac{i}{|k|},\frac{j}{|l|},\frac{j-\{u\}}{|l|}\colon 0\leq i\leq|k|,\;0\leq j\leq|l|\Bigr\}.

By the Bolzano-Weierstrass theorem, there exists a subsequence
(N_{j})_{j}
of
\mathbb{N}
such that
the limit

(39)

\displaystyle c_{i}=\lim_{j\to\infty}\frac{|\{n\in[N_{j}]\colon\;\{a(n)\}\in I_{i}\}|}{N_{j}}

exists for all
i\in[K]
.

From now on, assume that
f
has zero integral and is invariant under the transformation
R:=T_{1}^{k}T_{2}^{-l}
. Our objective is to show that
f
is
{\mathcal{I}}(H)
-measurable for some fixed finite index subgroup
H\subseteq{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
which contains
(k,l)
.

Since
(T_{1}^{{\left\lfloor ka(n)\right\rfloor}}T_{2}^{-{\left\lfloor la(n)+u\right\rfloor}})_{n}
is ergodic, we have

(40)

\lim_{j\to\infty}\E_{n\in[N_{j}]}T_{1}^{{\left\lfloor ka(n)\right\rfloor}}T_{2}^{-{\left\lfloor la(n)+u\right\rfloor}}f=0

in
L^{2}(\mu)
.
Using the identities

\displaystyle{\left\lfloor nx\right\rfloor}=n{\left\lfloor x\right\rfloor}+i\quad\textrm{whenever}\quad n\in{\mathbb{N}},\;x\in\left[\frac{i}{n},\frac{i+1}{n}\right)

\displaystyle\textrm{and}\quad{\left\lfloor x+y\right\rfloor}={\left\lfloor x\right\rfloor}+{\left\lfloor y\right\rfloor}+1_{\left\{x\right\}+\left\{y\right\}\geq 1},

we can find a function
r:[0,1)\to{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
, constant on each
I_{1},\ldots,I_{K}
and satisfying the bound

(41)

\displaystyle\left\|r\right\|_{\infty}\leq\max(|k|-1,|l|),

such that

\displaystyle T_{1}^{{\left\lfloor ka(n)\right\rfloor}}T_{2}^{-{\left\lfloor la(n)+u\right\rfloor}}=T_{1}^{k{\left\lfloor a(n)\right\rfloor}+r_{1}(n)}T_{2}^{-l{\left\lfloor a(n)\right\rfloor}-{\left\lfloor u\right\rfloor}-r_{2}(n)}=R^{{\left\lfloor a(n)\right\rfloor}}T_{1}^{r_{1}(n)}T_{2}^{-{\left\lfloor u\right\rfloor}-r_{2}(n)},

where
r(n)=(r_{1}(n),r_{2}(n))
.
Composing (
40
) with
T_{2}^{{\left\lfloor u\right\rfloor}}
and using the
R
-invariance of
f
, we obtain that

(42)

0=\lim_{j\to\infty}\E_{n\in[N_{j}]}T_{1}^{r_{1}(n)}T_{2}^{-r_{2}(n)}f=\sum_{i=1}^{K}c_{i}\cdot T_{1}^{r_{1,i}}T_{2}^{-r_{2,i}}f,

where the pair
(r_{1,i},r_{2,i})
is the value of
r
on
I_{i}
and
c_{i}
is defined in (
39
).

Since
k
is coprime to
l
, there exist
w,v\in\mathbb{Z}
with
\max(|w|,|v|)=O_{k,l}(1)
such that
wl-vk=1
. For all
i\in[K]
, we can then write
(r_{1,i},r_{2,i})=d_{i}(w,v)+d^{\prime}_{i}(k,-l)
for a unique choice of
d_{i},d^{\prime}_{i}\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, which are of size
O_{k,l}(1)
by (
41
). Denote
S:=T_{1}^{w}T_{2}^{v}
. Using the
R
-invariance of
f
again, we can recast

T_{1}^{r_{1,i}}T_{2}^{-r_{2,i}}f=S^{d_{i}}f
for all
i\in[K]
. So it follows from (
42
) that

(43)

0=\sum_{i=1}^{K}c_{i}S^{d_{i}}f.

Denote

L(f):=\sum_{i=1}^{K}c_{i}S^{d_{i}+d_{\ast}}f

for all
f
, where
d_{\ast}=\max\{-d_{1},\dots,-d_{K},0\}
; clearly,
L(f)=0
.
By the definition of
d_{*}
and the bounds on
d_{i}
, we have
0\leq d_{i}+d_{\ast}\ll_{k,l}1
, and hence

g(z):=\sum_{i=1}^{K}c_{i}z^{d_{i}+d_{\ast}}

is a real polynomial of degree
O_{k,l}(1)
. Since
c_{i}\geq 0
and at least one
c_{i}
is nonzero, the polynomial
g
is nonzero.

Thus, the study of the limit
L(f)
can be reduced to the examination of the polynomial
g
. The point of the next few claims is that we can throw away all the roots of
g
that are not roots of unity, and that we can combine them all together to find a finite-index subgroup
H\subseteq{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
containing
(k,-l)
such that
f
is
H
-invariant.

Claim 5.11
.

Suppose that
f\neq 0
and
Sf=\lambda f
for some
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
.
Then
\lambda
must be a root of
g
and a root of unity.

Indeed, assume that
\lambda
is not a root of unity. Then for all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,

\int f^{n}\;d\mu=\int S(f^{n})\;d\mu=\int(Sf)^{n}\;d\mu=\lambda^{n}\int f^{n}\;d\mu,

so
f^{n}
has zero integral.
Since
f^{n}
is
R
-invariant by assumption, we get
L(f^{n})=0
by (
43
). Because
f^{n}\neq 0
, we further deduce that
\lambda\neq 0
and
g(\lambda^{n})=0
. By the fundamental theorem of algebra, the polynomial
g
has only finitely many roots, and so the only way in which
g(\lambda^{n})=0
can happen for all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
is if
\lambda
is a root of unity.

As an immediate corollary, we get the following.

Claim 5.12
.

Suppose that
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
is not a root of unity. If
(S-\lambda)f=0
, then
f=0
.

Next, we move on to show that we can remove all the roots of
g
that are not roots of unity. Since
g\neq 0
, we may factorize
g
as

g(z)=c\prod_{j=1}^{M}(z-z_{j})

for some
c\neq 0,M=O_{k,l}(1)
and
z_{1},\dots,z_{M}\in\mathbb{C}
. Then it follows from (
43
) that

L(f)=c\prod_{j=1}^{M}(S-z_{j})f=0.

If
M=0
, then since
c\neq 0
, we must have
f=0
, in which case we are done. So we assume that
M>0
. Rearranging the roots, we can assume that
z_{1},\dots,z_{M^{\prime}}
are roots of unity while
z_{M^{\prime}+1},\dots,z_{M}
are not. By repeatedly applying Claim
5.12
to
\prod_{j=1}^{M^{\prime}+t}(S-z_{j})f
in place of
f
, we deduce that

\prod_{j=1}^{M^{\prime}+t}(S-z_{j})f=0

for all
0\leq t\leq M-M^{\prime}-1
. Hence

\prod_{j=1}^{M^{\prime}}(S-z_{j})f=0.

Assume that
z_{j}
is a primitive
q_{j}
-th root of unity for all
1\leq j\leq M^{\prime}
.
Then

(44)

\prod_{j=1}^{M^{\prime}}(S^{q_{j}}-1)f=0

since
z-z_{j}
divides
z^{q_{j}}-1
.

The following claim allows us to bound the size of
q_{j}
’s, and hence the index of the subgroup
H
later on.

Claim 5.13
.

We have
q_{j}=O_{k,l}(1)
.

To see this, recall that since
z_{j}
is a primitive
q_{j}
-th root of unity, its minimal polynomial
h
is of degree
\phi(q_{j})
, where
\phi
is the Euler totient function. Since
z_{j}
is also a root of
g
, the polynomial
h
must divide
g
, and so
\phi(q_{j})=\deg(h)\leq\deg(g)=O_{k,l}(1)
. Since
\lim\limits_{t\to\infty}\phi(t)=\infty
, we have
\phi(q_{j})=O_{k,l}(1)
only if
q_{j}=O_{k,l}(1)
.

The next claim enables us to combine the contributions of all the roots of unity
z_{1},\ldots,z_{M^{\prime}}
.

Claim 5.14
.

Let
m,n\in\mathbb{N}
. If
(S^{m}-1)(S^{n}-1)f=0
, then
(S^{2mn}-1)f=0
.

To see this, note that
(S^{m}-1)(S^{n}-1)f=0
implies that
(S^{mn}-1)^{2}f=0
since
z^{n}-1
and
z^{m}-1
both divide
z^{mn}-1
. Then

(S^{2mn}+1)f=2S^{mn}f.

So

4\int|f|^{2}=4\int|S^{mn}f|^{2}=\int|(S^{2mn}+1)f|^{2}=2\int|f|^{2}+\langle S^{2mn}f,f\rangle+\langle f,S^{2mn}f\rangle.

By the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality,
\langle S^{2mn}f,f\rangle\leq|f|^{2}
and the equality holds only if
S^{2mn}f=f
. Hence
(S^{2mn}-1)f=0
, and the claim follows.

Recall that
M^{\prime}\leq M=O_{k,l}(1)
and
q_{j}=O_{k,l}(1)
, the latter of which follows from Claim
5.13
. A repeated application of Claim
5.14
and (
44
) allows us to find
W=O_{k,l}(1)
and
q=2^{M^{\prime}-1}q_{1}\cdots q_{M^{\prime}}\leq W
such that
S^{q}f=f
.
Let
H=\langle W!(w,v),(k,-l)\rangle
: this is a finite index subgroup of
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
that depends only on
k
and
l
.
Since
q|W!
, the fact that
f
is invariant under both
S^{q}
and
R
implies that
f
is
H
-invariant. This means that
{\mathcal{I}}(R)={\mathcal{I}}(H)
.
∎

6.
Seminorm comparison under ergodicity assumptions II: irrational
\beta

We continue our exploration of consequences that can be drawn from the ergodicity of
(T_{1}^{{\left\lfloor a_{1}(n)\right\rfloor}}T_{2}^{-{\left\lfloor a_{2}(n)\right\rfloor}})_{n}
whenever
\beta_{2}a_{1}-\beta_{1}a_{2}\ll\log
for some nonzero
\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\in{\mathbb{R}}
.
In the preceding section, we have proved Corollary
5.9
, a special case of Proposition
5.3
for rational
\beta
. Our objective now is to complete the proof of Proposition
5.3
(and hence also of Proposition
5.1
) by covering the case of
\beta\notin{\mathbb{Q}}
. This turns out to require significantly more ad hoc computations than the case of rational
\beta
. For convenience, we state the main output of this section as a separate proposition.

Proposition 6.1
.

Proposition
5.3
holds for
\beta\notin{\mathbb{Q}}
.

More specifically, let
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},T_{2})
be a system, let
u\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
a\in{\mathcal{H}}
, where
a
is either in
{\mathbb{R}}[x]
or stays logarithmically away from
{\mathbb{R}}[x]
, and suppose that
(T_{1}^{{\left\lfloor a(n)\right\rfloor}}T_{2}^{-{\left\lfloor\beta a(n)+u\right\rfloor}})_{n}
is ergodic for
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu)
. Then for any
f\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
, we have

\displaystyle\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{{\mathbf{e}}_{1}-\beta{\mathbf{e}}_{2}}=0\quad\textrm{whenever}\quad\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{\langle{\mathbf{e}}_{1},{\mathbf{e}}_{2}\rangle}^{+}=0.

From now on, all the way up to the end of Section
6
, we fix all the data in the statement of Proposition
6.1
: the system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},T_{2})
, the numbers
u\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
\beta\notin{\mathbb{Q}}
, and the function
a\in{\mathcal{H}}
. We also make the following standing assumption:

(
\clubsuit
)
a
is either in
{\mathbb{R}}[x]
or stays logarithmically away from
{\mathbb{R}}[x]
.

For the discussion below, we also fix
f\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
.
Our main tool to analyze the ergodic averages induced by the action
(T_{1}^{{\left\lfloor a(n)\right\rfloor}}T_{2}^{-{\left\lfloor\beta a(n)+u\right\rfloor}})_{n}
is the classical Herglotz-Bochner theorem. It shows that

(45)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}T_{1}^{{\left\lfloor a(n)\right\rfloor}}T_{2}^{-{\left\lfloor\beta a(n)+u\right\rfloor}}f\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}=\left\|A\right\|_{L^{2}(\sigma_{f})},

where
\sigma_{f}
is the spectral measure on
[0,1)^{2}
associated with
f
and

A(t,s):=e(us)\cdot\lim_{N\to\infty}\E_{n\in[N]}e({\left\lfloor a(n)\right\rfloor}t-{\left\lfloor\beta a(n)+u\right\rfloor}s)\\
=\lim_{N\to\infty}\E_{n\in[N]}e((t-\beta s)a(n)-\{a(n)\}t+\{\beta a(n)+u\}s).

We recall that the limits in (
45
) exist by Lemma
5.6
, just like the limits below.

The exponential sum that we have to compare
A(t,s)
with is

B(t,s):=e(s)\cdot\lim_{N\to\infty}\E_{n\in[N]}e(tn+{\left\lfloor-\beta n\right\rfloor}s)=\lim_{N\to\infty}\E_{n\in[N]}e(tn-{\left\lfloor\beta n\right\rfloor}s)\\
=\lim_{N\to\infty}\E_{n\in[N]}e((t-\beta s)n+\{\beta n\}s)

(in the second equality, we use the irrationality of
\beta
to write
{\left\lfloor-\beta n\right\rfloor}=-{\left\lfloor\beta n\right\rfloor}-1
for
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}\backslash\{0\}
)
because

(46)

\displaystyle\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{{\mathbf{e}}_{1}-\beta{\mathbf{e}}_{2}}=\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}T_{1}^{n}T_{2}^{{\left\lfloor-\beta n\right\rfloor}}f\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}=\left\|B\right\|_{L^{2}(\sigma_{f})}

by the Herglotz-Bochner theorem again.

Our main goal now is to obtain a precise description of the values

(t,s)\in[0,1)^{2}
for which any of
A(t,s),B(t,s)
vanishes, so that we can compare (
45
) with (
46
) in a way that allows us to control (
46
) by a seminorm along
\langle{\mathbf{e}}_{1},{\mathbf{e}}_{2}\rangle
as in Proposition
6.1
.
To this end, we split

\displaystyle[0,1)^{2}=E_{1}\cup E_{2}\cup E_{3},

where

\displaystyle E_{1}

\displaystyle=\{(t,s)\in[0,1)^{2}:\;A(t,s)=B(t,s)=0\},

\displaystyle E_{2}

\displaystyle=\{(t,s)\in[0,1)^{2}:\;A(t,s)\neq 0\},

\displaystyle E_{3}

\displaystyle=\{(t,s)\in[0,1)^{2}:\;A(t,s)=0,\;B(t,s)\neq 0\}.

Naturally, the contribution of
E_{1}
to (
45
) and (
46
) is zero. To evaluate the contribution of
E_{2}
, we use the ergodicity assumption on our action to observe that

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\left|\int f\,d\mu\mathclose{}\right|^{2}=\sigma_{f}(\{(0,0)\})=\left\|A\right\|_{L^{2}(\sigma_{f})}.

Since
(0,0)\in E_{2}
and
|A(t,s)|>0
for every
(t,s)\in E_{2}
, we must have
\sigma_{f}(E_{2})=\sigma_{f}(\{0,0\})
from the continuity of the spectral measure. In particular, it follows that

\displaystyle\int_{E_{2}}|A(t,s)|^{2}\,d\sigma_{f}(t,s)=\int_{E_{2}}|B(t,s)|^{2}\,d\sigma_{f}(t,s)=\sigma_{f}(\{0,0\})=\mathopen{}\left|\int_{X}f\,d\mu\mathclose{}\right|^{2},

and so the contribution of
E_{2}
to (
45
) and (
46
) is equal. Our objective therefore is to evaluate the contribution of
E_{3}
, and for that we need to understand when
A
vanishes while
B
does not.
After doing so, we arrive at the following description of
E_{3}
.

Proposition 6.2
.

The set
E_{3}
is countable, and we have

\displaystyle E_{3}\subseteq\{\mathopen{}\left(\beta\gamma_{i},\gamma_{i}\mathclose{}\right):\;i\in{\mathbb{N}}\}\mod{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}

for some sequence
(\gamma_{n})_{n}
of nonzero real numbers.

For a set
E\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
, we interpret
E
mod
{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
as
\{(\left\{t\right\},\left\{s\right\}):\;(t,s)\in E\}
.

The proof of Proposition
6.2
will proceed by explicit computations of various exponential sums and can be broken down into the following four cases:

(i)

a(x)
does not equal

(47)

\displaystyle\frac{p(x)-ku}{k\beta+l}\quad\textrm{for\; any}\;\;(k,l)\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}\setminus{\{(0,0)\}},\;p\in{\mathbb{Z}}[x]{+{\mathbb{R}}};

(ii)

a(x)
equals (
51
) with
k\neq 0,l=0
;

(iii)

a(x)
equals (
51
) with
k=0,l\neq 0
;

(iv)

a(x)
equals (
51
) with
k,l\neq 0
.

The analysis of
A(t,s)
and
B(t,s)
differs markedly depending on whether
t-\beta s,\beta,1
are
{\mathbb{Q}}
-independent or
{\mathbb{Q}}
-dependent. The first case can be excluded almost immediately by the lemma below.

Lemma 6.3
.

Let
(t,s)\in[0,1)^{2}
so that
t-\beta s,\beta,1
are
{\mathbb{Q}}
-independent.
Then
B(t,s)=0
. As a consequence,

E_{3}\subseteq\{(t,s)\in[0,1)^{2}:\;t-\beta s=q+\beta r\;\textrm{for\; some}\;q,r\in{\mathbb{Q}}\}.

Proof.

By the assumption on
(t,s)
and Weyl’s equidistribution theorem, we have

\displaystyle B(t,s)=\lim_{N\to\infty}\E_{n\in[N]}e((t-\beta s)n+\{\beta n\}s)=\int_{[0,1)^{2}}e(x+zs)\;dxdz=0.

In particular, such
(t,s)
cannot lie in
E_{3}
.
∎

6.1.
Exponential sum computations for
{\mathbb{Q}}
-dependent
t-\beta s,\beta,1

The computations become much more intricate when
t-\beta s,\beta,1
are
{\mathbb{Q}}
-dependent, in which case
t-\beta s=q+\beta r
for some
q,r\in{\mathbb{Q}}
.
The following lemmas give sufficient and necessary conditions for
A(t,s)=0
to hold for different cases of
a
under the assumption of
{\mathbb{Q}}
-dependence of
t-\beta s,\beta,1
.

Lemma 6.4
.

Let
q,r\in{\mathbb{Q}}
and suppose that
a\in{\mathcal{H}}
does not equal
\frac{p-ku}{k\beta+l}
for any nonconstant
p\in{\mathbb{Z}}[x]{+{\mathbb{R}}}
and
(k,l)\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}\setminus{\{(0,0)\}}
. Then

\lim_{N\to\infty}\E_{n\in[N]}e(qa(n)+r\beta a(n)-\{a(n)\}t+\{\beta a(n)+u\}s)\\
=e(-ru)1_{\mathbb{Z}}(q)\mathopen{}\left(1_{q=t}+1_{{\mathbb{R}}\setminus{\mathbb{Z}}}(q-t)\frac{1}{2\pi i(q-t)}(e(q-t)-1)\mathclose{}\right)\\
1_{\mathbb{Z}}(r)\mathopen{}\left(1_{r=-s}+1_{{\mathbb{R}}\setminus{\mathbb{Z}}}(r+s)\frac{1}{2\pi i(r+s)}(e(r+s)-1)\mathclose{}\right).

In particular, this is 0 if and only if one of the followings holds:

(i)

q\notin{\mathbb{Z}}
;

(ii)

q-t\in\mathbb{Z}\backslash\{0\}
;

(iii)

r\notin\mathbb{Z}
;

(iv)

r+s\in\mathbb{Z}\backslash\{0\}
.

Proof.

By Theorem
1.13
and Weyl’s equidistribution theorem, the condition on
a
is equivalent to the sequence

(48)

\displaystyle(\{a(n)/c\},\{(\beta a(n)+u)/c\})_{n}

being equidistributed on
[0,1)^{2}
for every nonzero
c\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. Indeed, for the sake of equidistribution, we want
l\frac{a(x)}{c}+k\frac{\beta a(x)+u}{c}=\frac{k\beta+l}{c}a(x)+\frac{ku}{c}
not to lie in
{\mathbb{Q}}[x]+{\mathbb{R}}
for any
k,l\in{\mathbb{Z}}
not both 0 (the cases when it equals an element of
{\mathbb{Q}}[x]+{\mathbb{R}}
plus a nonconstant function
g
such that
g\to 0
or
1\prec g\ll\log
are excluded by the assumption
\clubsuit
). This happens precisely if
a
takes the prescribed form.

Let
c\in{\mathbb{Z}}
be such that
q,r\in\frac{1}{c}{\mathbb{Z}}
.
We therefore rephrase our exponential sum as

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\E_{n\in[N]}F(\{a(n)/c\},\{(\beta a(n)+u)/c\})

for
F(x,y)=e(cqx+cry-\{cx\}t+\{cy\}s-ru)
. The equidistribution of (
48
)
on
[0,1)^{2}
gives that our average equals

\displaystyle\int_{[0,1)^{2}}F(x,y)\,dxdy

\displaystyle=e(-ru)\int_{[0,1)^{2}}e(cqx+cry-\{cx\}t+\{cy\}s)\,dxdy.

This expression then equals

\begin{split}&e(-ru)\int_{[0,1)^{2}}e(cqx+cry-\{cx\}t+\{cy\}s)\,dxdy\\
&=e(-ru)\sum_{i,j=0}^{c-1}\int_{[0,\frac{1}{c})^{2}}e\mathopen{}\left(cq\mathopen{}\left(x+i/c\mathclose{}\right)+cr\mathopen{}\left(y+j/c\mathclose{}\right)-\{c\mathopen{}\left(x+i/c\mathclose{}\right)\}t+\{c\mathopen{}\left(y+j/c\mathclose{}\right)\}s\mathclose{}\right)\,dxdy\\
&=e(-ru)\sum_{i,j=0}^{c-1}e\mathopen{}\left(qi\mathclose{}\right)e\mathopen{}\left(rj\mathclose{}\right)\int_{[0,\frac{1}{c})^{2}}e(cqx+cry-\{cx\}t+\{cy\}s)\,dxdy\\
&=e(-ru)\frac{1}{c}\sum_{i=0}^{c-1}e\mathopen{}\left(qi\mathclose{}\right)\cdot\frac{1}{c}\sum_{j=0}^{c-1}e\mathopen{}\left(rj\mathclose{}\right)\cdot\int_{[0,1)}e((q-t)x)\,dx\cdot\int_{[0,1)}e((r+s)y)\,dy\\
&=e(-ru)1_{\mathbb{Z}}(q)\mathopen{}\left(1_{q=t}+1_{{\mathbb{R}}\setminus{\mathbb{Z}}}(q-t)\frac{1}{2\pi i(q-t)}(e(q-t)-1)\mathclose{}\right)\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad 1_{\mathbb{Z}}(r)\mathopen{}\left(1_{r=-s}+1_{{\mathbb{R}}\setminus{\mathbb{Z}}}(r+s)\frac{1}{2\pi i(r+s)}(e(r+s)-1)\mathclose{}\right).\end{split}

The four terms in the product are zero if and only if (iii), (iv), (i) and (ii) holds respectively.
∎

The next few lemmas cover the cases when
a=\frac{p-ku}{k\beta+l}
for some nonconstant
p\in{\mathbb{Z}}[x]+{\mathbb{R}}
and various choices of
k,l\in{\mathbb{Z}}
.

Lemma 6.5

(Case
l=0
)
.

Let
c\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}\backslash\{0\}
,
q,r\in\frac{1}{c}{\mathbb{Z}}
and suppose that
a=\frac{p-ku}{k\beta}
for some nonconstant
p\in{\mathbb{Z}}[x]{+{\mathbb{R}}}
. Then

\lim_{N\to\infty}\E_{n\in[N]}e(qa(n)+\beta ra(n)-\{a(n)\}t+\{\beta a(n)+u\}s)\\
=e\mathopen{}\left(-ru\mathclose{}\right)\E_{j\in[ck]}e\mathopen{}\left(\frac{rp(j)}{k}+\left\{\frac{p(j)}{k}\right\}s\mathclose{}\right)1_{\mathbb{Z}}(q)\\
\mathopen{}\left(1_{q=t}+1_{{\mathbb{R}}\setminus{\mathbb{Z}}}(q-t)\frac{1}{2\pi i(q-t)}(e(q-t)-1)\mathclose{}\right).

In particular, this is 0 if and only if one of the followings holds:

(i)

\E\limits_{j\in[ck]}e\mathopen{}\left(\frac{rp(j)}{k}+\left\{\frac{p(j)}{k}\right\}s\mathclose{}\right)=0
;

(ii)

q\notin{\mathbb{Z}}
;

(iii)

q-t\in\mathbb{Z}\backslash\{0\}
.

We remark that the first condition is tantamount to saying that the infinite average
\lim\limits_{N\to\infty}\E\limits_{n\in[N]}e\mathopen{}\left(\frac{rp(n)}{k}+\left\{\frac{p(n)}{k}\right\}s\mathclose{}\right)
converges to 0.

Proof.

If
k<0
, then we can make it positive by letting
k\mapsto-k
,
p\mapsto-p
, so we assume that
k>0
.
On splitting the average into arithmetic progressions of difference
ck
, it equals

e\mathopen{}\left(-ru\mathclose{}\right)\cdot\E_{j\in[ck]}e\mathopen{}\left(\frac{rp(j)}{k}+\left\{\frac{p(j)}{k}\right\}s\mathclose{}\right)\\
\lim_{N\to\infty}\E_{n\in[N]}e\left(\frac{qp(ckn+j)-qku}{k\beta}-\left\{\frac{p(ckn+j)-ku}{k\beta}\right\}t\right)

since

\displaystyle\beta a(ckn+j)+u=\frac{p(j)}{k}\!\!\!\!\mod{\mathbb{Z}}\quad\textrm{and}\quad\beta ra(ckn+j)=\frac{rp(j)}{k}-ru\!\!\!\!\mod{\mathbb{Z}}.

By Theorem
1.13
, the sequence
\mathopen{}\left(\left\{\frac{p(ckn+j)-ku}{ck\beta}\right\}\mathclose{}\right)_{n}
is equidistributed on
[0,1)
;
the reason why we divide by
c
is for
\frac{qp(ckn+j)-qku}{k\beta}

to be an integer multiple of
\frac{p(ckn+j)-ku}{ck\beta}
. Then our average equals

\displaystyle e\mathopen{}\left(-ru\mathclose{}\right)\cdot\E_{j\in[ck]}e\mathopen{}\left(\frac{rp(j)}{k}+\left\{\frac{p(j)}{k}\right\}s\mathclose{}\right)\cdot\int_{0}^{1}e(cqx-\{cx\}t)\,dx.

The same integral computation as in the proof of Lemma
6.4
shows that this is

\displaystyle e\mathopen{}\left(-ru\mathclose{}\right)\E_{j\in[ck]}e\mathopen{}\left(\frac{rp(j)}{k}+\left\{\frac{p(j)}{k}\right\}s\mathclose{}\right)1_{\mathbb{Z}}(q)\mathopen{}\left(1_{q=t}+1_{{\mathbb{R}}\setminus{\mathbb{Z}}}(q-t)\frac{1}{2\pi i(q-t)}(e(q-t)-1)\mathclose{}\right),

as claimed.
∎

Lemma 6.6

(Case
k=0
)
.

Let
c\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
l\in{\mathbb{Z}}\backslash\{0\}
,
q,r\in\frac{1}{c}{\mathbb{Z}}
and suppose that
a=\frac{p}{l}
for some nonconstant
p\in{\mathbb{Z}}[x]{+{\mathbb{R}}}
. Then

\lim_{N\to\infty}\E_{n\in[N]}e(qa(n)+\beta ra(n)-\{a(n)\}t+\{\beta a(n)+u\}s)\\
=e(-ru)\E_{j\in[cl]}e\mathopen{}\left(\frac{qp(j)}{l}-\left\{\frac{p(j)}{l}\right\}t\mathclose{}\right)1_{\mathbb{Z}}(r)\\
\mathopen{}\left(1_{r=-s}+1_{{\mathbb{R}}\setminus{\mathbb{Z}}}(r+s)\frac{1}{2\pi i(r+s)}(e(r+s)-1)\mathclose{}\right).

In particular, this is 0 if and only if one of the followings holds:

(i)

\E\limits_{j\in[cl]}e\mathopen{}\left(\frac{qp(j)}{l}-\left\{\frac{p(j)}{l}\right\}t\mathclose{}\right)=0
;

(ii)

r\notin{\mathbb{Z}}
;

(iii)

r+s\in\mathbb{Z}\backslash\{0\}
.

Proof.

Like in the previous lemma, we can assume without loss of generality that
l>0
.
On splitting the average into arithmetic progressions of difference
cl
, it equals

\displaystyle\E_{j\in[cl]}e\mathopen{}\left(\frac{qp(j)}{l}-\left\{\frac{p(j)}{l}\right\}t\mathclose{}\right)\lim_{N\to\infty}\E_{n\in[N]}e\left(\frac{\beta rp(cln+j)}{l}\right.\left.+\left\{\frac{\beta p(cln+j)}{l}+u\right\}s\right)

since

\displaystyle qa(cln+j)=\frac{qp(j)}{l}\!\!\!\!\mod{\mathbb{Z}}\quad\textrm{and}\quad a(cln+j)=\frac{p(j)}{l}\!\!\!\!\mod{\mathbb{Z}}.

By Theorem
1.13
, the sequence
\mathopen{}\left(\left\{\frac{\beta p(cln+j)}{cl}+u\right\}\mathclose{}\right)_{n}
is equidistributed on
[0,1)
, and hence the average equals

e(-ru)\E_{j\in[cl]}e\mathopen{}\left(\frac{qp(j)}{l}-\left\{\frac{p(j)}{l}\right\}t\mathclose{}\right)\int_{0}^{1}e\big(rcx+s\{cx\}\big)dx=\\
e(-ru)\E_{j\in[cl]}e\mathopen{}\left(\frac{qp(j)}{l}-\left\{\frac{p(j)}{l}\right\}t\mathclose{}\right)1_{\mathbb{Z}}(r)\mathopen{}\left(1_{r=-s}+1_{{\mathbb{R}}\setminus{\mathbb{Z}}}(r+s)\frac{1}{2\pi i(r+s)}(e(r+s)-1)\mathclose{}\right),

where the integral is evaluated as in the proof of Lemma
6.4
.
∎

In the special case where
a(n)=n
and
u=0
, Lemma
6.6
gives the following corollary that can be used to describe the zero set of
B
.

Corollary 6.7

(Case
a(n)=n
)
.

Let
q,r\in{\mathbb{Q}}
.
Then

\lim_{N\to\infty}\E_{n\in[N]}e((q+\beta r)n+\{\beta n\}s)\\
=1_{\mathbb{Z}}(q)1_{\mathbb{Z}}(r)\mathopen{}\left(1_{r=-s}+1_{{\mathbb{R}}\setminus{\mathbb{Z}}}(r+s)\frac{1}{2\pi i(r+s)}(e(r+s)-1)\mathclose{}\right).

In particular, it is 0 if and only if one of the followings holds:

(i)

q\notin{\mathbb{Z}}
;

(ii)

r\notin{\mathbb{Z}}
;

(iii)

r+s\in\mathbb{Z}\backslash\{0\}
.

In the last remaining case, the computations become so complicated that the conclusion is less explicit than in the previous results. In what follows,
\sgn(x)=1_{x\geq 0}-1_{x\leq 0}
is the usual sign function.

Lemma 6.8

(Case
k,l\neq 0
)
.

Let
c\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
k,l\in{\mathbb{Z}}\backslash\{0\}
with
l>0
, and
q,r\in\frac{1}{c}{\mathbb{Z}}
, and suppose that
a=\frac{p-ku}{k\beta+l}
for some nonconstant
p\in{\mathbb{Z}}[x]{+{\mathbb{R}}}
. For
j\in[ck]
and
j^{\prime}~\in~[0,|ckl|~-~1]
, define

\displaystyle w

\displaystyle:={\frac{k(q-t)-l(r+s)}{|kl|}}

\displaystyle x_{j,j^{\prime}}

\displaystyle:=|k|\left\{\frac{p(j)-\sgn(k)j^{\prime}}{k}\right\}

\displaystyle b_{j,j^{\prime}}

\displaystyle:={\left\lfloor\frac{\sgn(k)(j^{\prime}+1_{k<0})}{l}\right\rfloor}t-{\left\lfloor\frac{p(j)-\sgn(k)j^{\prime}}{k}\right\rfloor}s.

Then

\lim_{N\to\infty}\E_{n\in[N]}e(qa(n)+\beta ra(n)-\{a(n)\}t+\{\beta a(n)+u\}s)=0

if and only if one of the followings holds:

(i)

w=0
and

\displaystyle\E_{j\in[ck]}e\left(\frac{{r+s}}{k}p(j)\right)\E_{j^{\prime}\in[0,|ckl|-1]}e\left(b_{j,j^{\prime}}\right)(e(s)+\min(x_{j,j^{\prime}},1)(1-e(s)))=0;

(ii)

w\neq 0
and

\E_{j\in[ck]}e\left(\frac{{r+s}}{k}p(j)\right)\E_{j^{\prime}\in[0,|ckl|-1]}e\left({j^{\prime}w}+b_{j,j^{\prime}}\right)\\
\mathopen{}\left(e(s)\Bigl(e({w})-1\Bigr)+(1-e(s))\Bigl(e({w\min(x_{j,j^{\prime}},1)})-1\Bigr)\mathclose{}\right)=0.

We observe that there is no loss of generality in assuming that
l>0
for if
l<0
, then we can reduce to the case
l>0
by changing
l\mapsto-l,k\mapsto-k,p\mapsto-p
.

Proof.

Let
\tilde{q}=qc,\tilde{r}=rc\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, and
\tilde{p}(n)=p(n)-ku
. We can recast our average as

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\E_{n\in[N]}e\mathopen{}\left({\frac{(\tilde{q}+\beta\tilde{r})\tilde{p}(n)}{c(k\beta+l)}}-\left\{\frac{\tilde{p}(n)}{k\beta+l}\right\}t+\left\{\frac{\beta\tilde{p}(n)}{k\beta+l}+u\right\}s\mathclose{}\right).

Using the identity

\displaystyle\frac{x\beta+y}{k\beta+l}=\frac{ky-lx}{k(k\beta+l)}+\frac{x}{k},

we want to bring all the fractions above to the form
{\mathbb{Q}}+\frac{{\mathbb{Z}}}{ck(k\beta+l)}
and then apply Theorem
1.13
and Weyl’s equidistribution theorem. Indeed, our average equals

\lim_{N\to\infty}\E_{n\in[N]}e\left({\mathopen{}\left(\frac{\tilde{q}k-\tilde{r}l}{ck(k\beta+l)}+\frac{\tilde{r}}{ck}\mathclose{}\right)\tilde{p}(n)}-\left\{\frac{ck}{ck(k\beta+l)}\tilde{p}(n)\right\}t\right.\\
\left.+\left\{\mathopen{}\left(\frac{-cl}{ck(k\beta+l)}+\frac{1}{k}\mathclose{}\right)\tilde{p}(n)+u\right\}s\right).

To remove the rational shifts, we split the range of
n
into arithmetic progressions of difference
ck
. Thus our average becomes

\E_{j\in[ck]}e\mathopen{}\left(\frac{\tilde{r}\tilde{p}(j)}{ck}\mathclose{}\right)\lim_{N\to\infty}\E_{n\in[N]}e\left({{\frac{\tilde{q}k-\tilde{r}l}{ck(k\beta+l)}}\tilde{p}(ckn+j)}\right.\\
\left.-\left\{\frac{ck}{ck(k\beta+l)}\tilde{p}(ckn+j)\right\}t+\left\{{\frac{-cl}{ck(k\beta+l)}}\tilde{p}(ckn+j)+\frac{\tilde{p}(j)}{k}+u\right\}s\right).

By Theorem
1.13
, the sequence
\mathopen{}\left(\left\{\frac{\tilde{p}(ckn+j)}{ck(k\beta+l)}\right\}\mathclose{}\right)_{n}
is equidistributed on
[0,1)
, and hence our average equals

\displaystyle\E_{j\in[ck]}e\left(\frac{\tilde{r}\tilde{p}(j)}{ck}\right)\int_{0}^{1}e\mathopen{}\left((\tilde{q}k-\tilde{r}l)x-\left\{ckx\right\}t+\left\{-clx+\frac{\tilde{p}(j)}{k}+u\right\}s\mathclose{}\right)\;dx.

Recalling that
\tilde{r}=cr
and
\tilde{p}(j)=p(j)-ku
, we can slightly simplify this expression to

\displaystyle e(-ru)\E_{j\in[ck]}e\left(\frac{{r}p(j)}{k}\right)\int_{0}^{1}e\mathopen{}\left((\tilde{q}k-\tilde{r}l)x-\left\{ckx\right\}t+\left\{-clx+\frac{p(j)}{k}\right\}s\mathclose{}\right)\;dx.

To remove the fractional parts, we split the domain of integration into intervals of length
\frac{1}{|ckl|}
.
If
x\in\left(\frac{j^{\prime}}{|ckl|},\frac{j^{\prime}+1}{|ckl|}\right)
, then

\displaystyle{\left\lfloor ckx\right\rfloor}={\left\lfloor\frac{\sgn(k)(j^{\prime}+1_{k<0})}{l}\right\rfloor}.

On the other hand, let
x_{j,j^{\prime}}:=|k|\left\{\frac{p(j)-\sgn(k)j^{\prime}}{k}\right\}
. Then an easy computation shows that

\displaystyle{\left\lfloor-clx+\frac{p(j)}{k}\right\rfloor}=\begin{cases}{\left\lfloor\frac{p(j)-\sgn(k)j^{\prime}}{k}\right\rfloor},\;&x\in\left(\frac{j^{\prime}}{|ckl|},\frac{j^{\prime}+\min(x_{j,j^{\prime}},1)}{|ckl|}\right]=:C_{j,j^{\prime}}\\
{\left\lfloor\frac{p(j)-\sgn(k)j^{\prime}}{k}\right\rfloor}-1,\;&x\in\left(\frac{j^{\prime}+\min(x_{j,j^{\prime}},1)}{|ckl|},\frac{j^{\prime}+1}{|ckl|}\right)=:C^{\prime}_{j,j^{\prime}}.\end{cases}

Now, letting

\displaystyle w

\displaystyle:={\frac{\tilde{q}k-\tilde{r}l-ckt-cls}{|ckl|}=\frac{k(q-t)-l(r+s))}{|kl|}}

\displaystyle\textrm{and}\quad b_{j,j^{\prime}}

\displaystyle:={\left\lfloor\frac{\sgn(k)(j^{\prime}+1_{k<0})}{l}\right\rfloor}t-{\left\lfloor\frac{p(j)-\sgn(k)j^{\prime}}{k}\right\rfloor}s

to simplify the discussion, our expression equals
e(-ru)
times

\displaystyle\E_{j\in[ck]}e\left(\frac{{r+s}}{k}p(j)\right)\sum_{j^{\prime}\in[0,|ckl|-1]}\int_{\frac{j^{\prime}}{|ckl|}}^{\frac{j^{\prime}+1}{|ckl|}}e\left({|ckl|}wx+{\left\lfloor ckx\right\rfloor}t-{\left\lfloor-clx+\frac{p(j)}{k}\right\rfloor}s\right)\;dx

\displaystyle=\E_{j\in[ck]}e\left(\frac{{r+s}}{k}p(j)\right)\sum_{j^{\prime}\in[0,|ckl|-1]}\left(\int_{C_{j,j^{\prime}}}e({|ckl|}wx+b_{j,j^{\prime}})\;dx+\int_{C^{\prime}_{j,j^{\prime}}}e\left({|ckl|}wx+b_{j,j^{\prime}}+s\right)\;dx\right)

\displaystyle=\E_{j\in[ck]}e\left(\frac{{r+s}}{k}p(j)\right)\sum_{j^{\prime}\in[0,|ckl|-1]}\left(e(s)\int_{\frac{j^{\prime}}{|ckl|}}^{\frac{j^{\prime}+1}{|ckl|}}e({|ckl|}wx+b_{j,j^{\prime}})\;dx\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\left.+(1-e(s))\int_{C_{j,j^{\prime}}}e\left({|ckl|}wx+b_{j,j^{\prime}}\right)\;dx\right).

Performing the change of variables, this equals

\displaystyle\E_{j\in[ck]}e\left(\frac{{r+s}}{k}p(j)\right)\E_{j^{\prime}\in[0,|ckl|-1]}e\left({j^{\prime}w}+b_{j,j^{\prime}}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\left(e(s)\int_{0}^{1}e\left({w}x\right)\;dx+(1-e(s))\int_{0}^{\min(x_{j,j^{\prime}},1)}e\left({w}x\right)\;dx\right).

If
w=0
, the expression above equals

\displaystyle\E_{j\in[ck]}e\left(\frac{{r+s}}{k}p(j)\right)\E_{j^{\prime}\in[0,|ckl|-1]}e\left(b_{j,j^{\prime}}\right)(e(s)+\min(x_{j,j^{\prime}},1)(1-e(s))),

otherwise we obtain

\E_{j\in[ck]}e\left(\frac{{r+s}}{k}p(j)\right)\E_{j^{\prime}\in[0,|ckl|-1]}e\left({j^{\prime}w}+b_{j,j^{\prime}}\right)\\
\frac{1}{2\pi i{w}}\mathopen{}\left(e(s)\Bigl(e({w})-1\Bigr)+(1-e(s))\Bigl(e({w\min(x_{j,j^{\prime}},1)})-1\Bigr)\mathclose{}\right).

This completes the proof.
∎

6.2.
Structure of
E_{3}

Combining the content of the previous lemmas, we move on to prove Proposition
6.2
, i.e. we describe the set
E_{3}
consisting of those points
(t,s)\in[0,1)^{2}
for which
A(t,s)=0
while
B(t,s)\neq 0
.

Proof of Proposition
6.2
.

Our objective can be restated as follows: we want to show that
E_{3}
is countable, and that it is contained in
\{\mathopen{}\left(\beta\gamma,\gamma\mathclose{}\right):\gamma\in{\mathbb{R}}\}
mod
{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
.

Let
(t,s)\in E_{3}
. By Lemma
6.3
and Corollary
6.7
, we have

(49)

\displaystyle t-\beta s=q+\beta r\quad\textrm{for\; some}\quad q,r\in{\mathbb{Z}}\quad\textrm{satisfying}\quad r+s\notin{\mathbb{Z}}\setminus\{0\}.

In particular, the value
c
appearing in various lemmas throughout Section
6.1
is
1
. Then

\displaystyle(t,s)=(\beta(r+s),r+s)+(q,-r),

from which we deduce that
(t,s)
lies in

(50)

\displaystyle\{(\beta\gamma,\gamma):\;\gamma\in{\mathbb{R}}\}\mod{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}.

It remains to show that
E_{3}
is countable.
To obtain this last claim, we split the proof into the following four cases whose exponential sums we analyzed separately in the previous section, and each of which requires a slightly different treatment now:

(i)

a(x)
does not equal

(51)

\displaystyle\frac{p(x)-ku}{k\beta+l}\quad\textrm{for\; any}\;\;(k,l)\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}\setminus{\{(0,0)\}},\;p\in{\mathbb{Z}}[x]{+{\mathbb{R}}};

(ii)

a(x)
equals (
51
) with
k\neq 0,l=0
;

(iii)

a(x)
equals (
51
) with
k=0,l\neq 0
;

(iv)

a(x)
equals (
51
) with
k,l\neq 0
.

Case (
i
).

Combining the conclusions of Lemma
6.4
and Corollary
6.7
, the set
E_{3}
consists of those pairs
(t,s)\in[0,1)^{2}
satisfying (
49
) for which additionally
q-t\in\mathbb{Z}\backslash\{0\}
.
Hence
t\in{\mathbb{Z}}\setminus\{q\}
and
s=\frac{1}{\beta}(t-q)-r\in\frac{1}{\beta}{\mathbb{Z}}\setminus\{0\}+{\mathbb{Z}}
, and so the collection of pairs
(t,s)\in E_{3}
forms a countable subset of (
50
).

Case (
ii
).

In this case, Lemma
6.5
says that on top of (
49
), each
(t,s)\in E_{3}
satisfies one of the two conditions:

q-t\in{\mathbb{Z}}\setminus\{0\}
or
\E\limits_{i\in[{k}]}e\mathopen{}\left(\frac{rp(i)}{k}+\left\{\frac{p(i)}{k}\right\}s\mathclose{}\right)=0
. If
q-t\in{\mathbb{Z}}\setminus\{0\}
, then we have countably many choices of
(t,s)
inside (
50
) just like in the previous case.
If instead
\E_{i\in[k]}e\mathopen{}\left(\frac{rp(i)}{k}+\left\{\frac{p(i)}{k}\right\}s\mathclose{}\right)=0
, then
r\in{\mathbb{Z}}
and rudimentary manipulations imply that

(52)

\displaystyle\E_{i\in[k]}e\mathopen{}\left((r+s)\left\{\frac{p(i)}{k}\right\}\mathclose{}\right)=0.

Consider therefore the remaining set

\displaystyle\tilde{E}_{3}=\left\{(t,s)\in E_{3}:\;t=q+\beta(r+s),\;q,r\in{\mathbb{Z}},\;r+s\notin{\mathbb{Z}}\setminus\{0\},\;\eqref{E: reduced exp sum}\;\textrm{holds}\right\}.

The crucial observation is that the exponential sum (
52
) can be realized as a polynomial of bounded degree. Indeed, for each
j\in\{0,\ldots,k-1\}
, let

c_{j}=\frac{1}{k}\cdot\mathopen{}\left|\left\{i\in[k]:\;\left\{\frac{p(i)}{k}\right\}=\frac{j+\{p(0)\}}{k}\right\}\mathclose{}\right|

and
Q(z)=\sum\limits_{j=0}^{k-1}c_{j}z^{j}
be the rational polynomial satisfying

\displaystyle\E_{i\in[k]}e\mathopen{}\left(\alpha\left\{\frac{p(i)}{k}\right\}\mathclose{}\right)=Q(e(\alpha/k)){e(\alpha\{p(0)\}/k)}

for every
\alpha\in{\mathbb{T}}
. By the fundamental theorem of algebra, there exist at most
k-1
values
\alpha\in{\mathbb{T}}
for which
Q(e(\alpha/k))=0
. In our case,
\alpha=r+s
and
t=q+\beta(r+s)
, and so we deduce that
\tilde{E}_{3}
is countable. Combining both subcases, we deduce that
E_{3}
is a countable subset of (
50
).

Case (
iii
).

In this case, Lemma
6.6
guarantees that
(t,s)\in E_{3}
satisfies

(53)

\displaystyle\E_{i\in[{l}]}e\mathopen{}\left((q-t)\left\{\frac{p(i)}{l}\right\}\mathclose{}\right)=0

on top of (
49
). The countability of
E_{3}
then follows from a similar argument as in the previous case.

Case (
iv
)

While in the previous two cases we deduced the countability of
E_{3}
from the fundamental theorem of algebra, this time we will rely on basic properties of holomorphic functions.
Let

\displaystyle w:={\frac{k(q-t)-l(r+s)}{|kl|}=\frac{-(k\beta+l)(r+s)}{|kl|},}

and recall that every
(t,s)\in E_{3}
must satisfy one of the two conditions from Lemma
6.8
on top of (
49
).
If
w=0
, then necessarily
s=-r\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, which is precluded by (
49
).
Therefore
w\neq 0
, in which case the second condition from Lemma
6.8
holds, i.e.

(54)

\E_{j\in[k]}e\left(\frac{{r+s}}{k}p(j)\right)\E_{j^{\prime}\in[0,|kl|-1]}e\left(-\frac{j^{\prime}}{|kl|}(k\beta+l)(r+s)+b_{j,j^{\prime}}\right)\\
\mathopen{}\left(e(s)\Bigl(e({w})-1\Bigr)+(1-e(s))\Bigl(e({w\min(x_{j,j^{\prime}},1)})-1\Bigr)\mathclose{}\right)

is 0
for

\displaystyle x_{j,j^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=|k|\left\{\frac{p(j)-\sgn(k)j^{\prime}}{k}\right\}

\displaystyle\textrm{and}\quad b_{j,j^{\prime}}

\displaystyle={\left\lfloor\frac{\sgn(k)(j^{\prime}+1_{k<0})}{l}\right\rfloor}(q+\beta(r+s))-{\left\lfloor\frac{p(j)-\sgn(k)j^{\prime}}{k}\right\rfloor}s.

For each fixed
q,r\in\mathbb{Z}
, the average (
54
) is a finite linear combination of functions of the form
s\mapsto e(vs)
for
v\in{\mathbb{R}}
.
Each such function is holomorphic, and therefore the average in (
54
) is also holomorphic as a function of
s
. Hence, one of the two things may happen:

(i)

(
54
) is identically 0 for all
s\in{\mathbb{C}}
;

(ii)

(
54
) has at most countably many zeros.

If we are able to exclude the first case, then the second case immediately implies the countability of
E_{3}
.

We show that the former option is not possible, i.e. the average in (
54
) cannot vanish for all
s\in{\mathbb{C}}
, by identifying the value
s=-|kl|-r
for which (
54
) is nonzero.
For this value
s
, we can simplify various expressions appearing in (
54
) to

\displaystyle\frac{r+s}{k}p(j)

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{r+s}{k}p(0)\!\!\!\mod 1,

\displaystyle-\frac{j^{\prime}}{|kl|}(k\beta+l)(r+s)

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{\sgn(k)j^{\prime}}{l}|kl|\beta\!\!\!\mod 1,

\displaystyle b_{j,j^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\equiv-{\left\lfloor\frac{\sgn(k)(j^{\prime}+1_{k<0})}{l}\right\rfloor}|kl|\beta\!\!\!\mod 1

\displaystyle w

\displaystyle=k\beta+l\notin{\mathbb{Z}}.

With these simplifications, (
54
) reduces to

\displaystyle\E_{j^{\prime}\in[0,|kl|-1]}e\mathopen{}\left(\Bigl(\frac{\sgn(k)j^{\prime}}{l}-{\left\lfloor\frac{\sgn(k)(j^{\prime}+1_{k<0})}{l}\right\rfloor}\Bigr)|kl|\beta\mathclose{}\right)=0.

The left-hand side then equals
e\Bigl(-\frac{\sgn(k)1_{k<0}}{l}|kl|\beta\Bigr)
times

\displaystyle\E_{j^{\prime}\in[0,|kl|-1]}e\mathopen{}\left(\Big\{\frac{\sgn(k)(j^{\prime}+1_{k<0})}{l}\Big\}|kl|\beta\mathclose{}\right)

\displaystyle=\E_{j^{\prime}\in[0,l-1]}e\mathopen{}\left(\Big\{\frac{j^{\prime}}{l}\Big\}|kl|\beta\mathclose{}\right)

\displaystyle=\E_{j^{\prime}\in[0,l-1]}e(j^{\prime}|k|\beta)=\frac{e(|kl|\beta)-1}{l(e(|k|\beta)-1)},

which is nonzero since
\beta\notin{\mathbb{Q}}
.
∎

6.3.
Proof of Proposition
6.1

We now bring all the pieces together in order to prove Proposition
6.1
.

For a non-empty subset
H\subseteq[0,1)^{2}
, set

{\mathcal{K}}(H):=\langle f\in L^{2}(\mu):\;T_{1}f=e(t)f,\;T_{2}f=e(s)f\;\;\textrm{for\; some}\;\;(t,s)\in H\rangle,

and set
{\mathcal{K}}(\emptyset):=\{0\}
. Let
P_{H}f
be the orthogonal projection of
f
onto
{\mathcal{K}}(H)
. If
H=\{(t,s)\}
, then we also write

\displaystyle P_{(t,s)}f=P_{\{(t,s)\}}f=\lim_{N\to\infty}\E_{n_{1},n_{2}\in[N]}T_{1}^{n_{1}}T_{2}^{n_{2}}f\cdot e(-tn_{1}-sn_{2})

for simplicity, and note that the single limit over
([N]^{2})_{N}
could be replaced by an iterated limit. If
H\cap H^{\prime}=\emptyset
, then clearly
\langle P_{H}f,P_{H^{\prime}}f\rangle=0
and
P_{H\cup H^{\prime}}f=P_{H}f+P_{H^{\prime}}f
. It follows in particular that if
H\subseteq H^{\prime}
, then

(55)

\displaystyle\lvert\!|\!|P_{H^{\prime}}f|\!|\!\rvert_{{\mathbf{e}}_{1}-\beta{\mathbf{e}}_{2}}^{2}=\lvert\!|\!|P_{H}f|\!|\!\rvert_{{\mathbf{e}}_{1}-\beta{\mathbf{e}}_{2}}^{2}+\lvert\!|\!|P_{H^{\prime}\setminus H}f|\!|\!\rvert_{{\mathbf{e}}_{1}-\beta{\mathbf{e}}_{2}}^{2}\geq\lvert\!|\!|P_{H}f|\!|\!\rvert_{{\mathbf{e}}_{1}-\beta{\mathbf{e}}_{2}}^{2};

to see this, we write
P_{H^{\prime}}f=P_{H}f+P_{H^{\prime}\setminus H}f,
expand the left-hand side as a Gowers-Cauchy-Schwarz inner product, and then use the orthogonality of
P_{H}f
,
P_{H^{\prime}\setminus H}f
to cancel the cross terms.

The next lemma will be crucial in completing the proof of Proposition
6.1
. For eigenfunctions with eigenvalues of the form
(\beta\gamma,\gamma)
mod
{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
with
\gamma\neq 0
, it allows us to replace the single-parameter action of
(T_{1}^{n}T_{2}^{-{\left\lfloor\beta n\right\rfloor}})_{n}
by the double-parameter action of
(T_{1}^{{\left\lfloor\frac{n}{\beta\gamma}\right\rfloor}}T_{2}^{{\left\lfloor-\frac{m}{\gamma}\right\rfloor}})_{n,m}
.

Lemma 6.9
.

Let
\gamma\in{\mathbb{R}}
be nonzero and
f\in L^{2}(\mu)
. Then there exists
c_{\beta,\gamma}\in{\mathbb{C}}
such that

(56)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\E_{n\in[N]}T_{1}^{n}T_{2}^{-{\left\lfloor\beta n\right\rfloor}}P_{(\beta\gamma,\gamma)}f=c_{\beta,\gamma}\cdot\lim_{N\to\infty}\E_{n,m\in[N]}T_{1}^{{\left\lfloor\frac{n}{\beta\gamma}\right\rfloor}}T_{2}^{{\left\lfloor-\frac{m}{\gamma}\right\rfloor}}P_{(\beta\gamma,\gamma)}f.

One could alternatively try to compare the left-hand side of (
56
) with

\lim\limits_{N\to\infty}\E\limits_{n,m\in[N]}T_{1}^{{\left\lfloor\xi n\right\rfloor}}T_{2}^{{\left\lfloor-\xi\beta m\right\rfloor}}P_{(\beta\gamma,\gamma)}f

for other choices of
\xi\in{\mathbb{R}}\backslash\{0\}
. However,
this average vanishes unless
\xi\in\frac{1}{\beta\gamma}{\mathbb{Z}}
.

Proof.

Let

\displaystyle F

\displaystyle:=\lim_{N\to\infty}\E_{n\in[N]}T_{1}^{n}T_{2}^{-{\left\lfloor\beta n\right\rfloor}}P_{(\beta\gamma,\gamma)}f=P_{(\beta\gamma,\gamma)}f\cdot\lim_{N\to\infty}\E_{n\in[N]}e(\beta\gamma n-{\left\lfloor\beta n\right\rfloor}\gamma)

\displaystyle=P_{(\beta\gamma,\gamma)}f\cdot\lim_{N\to\infty}\E_{n\in[N]}e(\left\{\beta n\right\}\gamma)=P_{(\beta\gamma,\gamma)}f\cdot\int_{0}^{1}e(\gamma y)\,dy

and

\displaystyle G

\displaystyle:=\lim_{N\to\infty}\E_{n,m\in[N]}T_{1}^{{\left\lfloor\frac{n}{\beta\gamma}\right\rfloor}}T_{2}^{{\left\lfloor-\frac{m}{\gamma}\right\rfloor}}P_{(\beta\gamma,\gamma)}f

\displaystyle=P_{(\beta\gamma,\gamma)}f\cdot\lim_{N\to\infty}\E_{n,m\in[N]}e\mathopen{}\left({\left\lfloor\frac{n}{\beta\gamma}\right\rfloor}\beta\gamma+{\left\lfloor-\frac{m}{\gamma}\right\rfloor}\gamma\mathclose{}\right)

\displaystyle=P_{(\beta\gamma,\gamma)}f\cdot\lim_{N\to\infty}\E_{n,m\in[N]}e\mathopen{}\left(-\left\{\frac{n}{\beta\gamma}\right\}\beta\gamma-\left\{-\frac{m}{\gamma}\right\}\gamma\mathclose{}\right).

If
\gamma\in{\mathbb{Z}}\setminus\{0\}
, then
F=0
, and so the claim follows trivially with
c_{\beta,\gamma}=0
.
Otherwise
\int_{0}^{1}e(\gamma y)\,dy\neq 0
, and the goal is to show that

\displaystyle C_{\beta,\gamma}:=\lim_{N\to\infty}\E_{n,m\in[N]}e\mathopen{}\left(-\left\{\frac{n}{\beta\gamma}\right\}\beta\gamma-\left\{-\frac{m}{\gamma}\right\}\gamma\mathclose{}\right)

is nonzero as well so that

\displaystyle c_{\beta,\gamma}:=C_{\beta,\gamma}^{-1}\cdot\int_{0}^{1}e(\gamma y)\,dy

is well-defined.

We split the proof into several cases.
If
\gamma\notin(\frac{1}{\beta}{\mathbb{Q}})\cup{\mathbb{Q}}
, then both
\beta\gamma
and
\gamma
are irrational, and so

\displaystyle C_{\beta,\gamma}=\int_{0}^{1}e(-\beta\gamma x)\,dx\cdot\int_{0}^{1}e(-\gamma y)\,dy.

Both integrals are nonzero since neither
\gamma
nor
\beta\gamma
is an integer.

If
\gamma\in{\mathbb{Q}}\setminus{\mathbb{Z}}
, write
\gamma=-\frac{p}{q}
for coprime integers
p,q
. Then

\displaystyle C_{\beta,\gamma}=\int_{0}^{1}e(-\beta\gamma x)\,dx\cdot\E_{m\in[p]}e\mathopen{}\left(\left\{\frac{qm}{p}\right\}\frac{p}{q}\mathclose{}\right).

The integral is nonzero since
\beta\gamma\notin{\mathbb{Z}}
. To see that the exponential sum is nonzero, let
q^{*}
be the multiplicative inverse of
q
mod
p
. Then the map
m\mapsto q^{*}m
is a bijection on
{\mathbb{Z}}/p{\mathbb{Z}}
, and so

\displaystyle\E_{m\in[p]}e\mathopen{}\left(\left\{\frac{qm}{p}\right\}\frac{p}{q}\mathclose{}\right)

\displaystyle=\E_{m\in[p]}e\mathopen{}\left(\left\{\frac{qq^{*}m}{p}\right\}\frac{p}{q}\mathclose{}\right)=\E_{m\in[p]}e\mathopen{}\left(\frac{m}{q}\mathclose{}\right)\neq 0

due to the coprimality of
p,q
.

Lastly, suppose that
\gamma\in\frac{1}{\beta}{\mathbb{Q}}
. Setting
\gamma=\frac{p}{q\beta}
for coprime integers
p,q
, we deduce that

\displaystyle C_{\beta,\gamma}=\int_{0}^{1}e(-\gamma y)\,dy\cdot\E_{n\in[p]}e\mathopen{}\left(-\left\{\frac{qn}{p}\right\}\frac{p}{q}\mathclose{}\right).

The integral is nonzero since
\gamma\notin{\mathbb{Z}}
, and the exponential sum is nonzero by the same argument as before.
∎

We conclude this section with the proof of Proposition
6.1
.

Proof of Proposition
6.1
.

If
a
is constant, then the action can only be ergodic if the system is conjugate to a 1-point system, in which case the result follows trivially. Suppose therefore that
a
is not constant.

By the arguments below the statement of Proposition
6.1
, we have

\displaystyle\lvert\!|\!|{f}|\!|\!\rvert_{{\mathbf{e}}_{1}-\beta{\mathbf{e}}_{2}}^{2}=\int_{E_{3}\cup\{(0,0)\}}|B(t,s)|^{2}\,d\sigma_{{f}}(t,s).

By Proposition
6.2
, every element of the support
\tilde{E}_{3}:=E_{3}\cup\{(0,0)\}
of the preceding integral takes the form
(\beta\gamma,\gamma)
mod
{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
.
Using this and orthogonality of projections on different eigenspaces, we get

\displaystyle\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{{\mathbf{e}}_{1}-\beta{\mathbf{e}}_{2}}^{2}=\lvert\!|\!|P_{\tilde{E}_{3}}f|\!|\!\rvert_{{\mathbf{e}}_{1}-\beta{\mathbf{e}}_{2}}^{2}=\sum_{(\beta\gamma,\gamma)\in\tilde{E}_{3}}\lvert\!|\!|P_{(\beta\gamma,\gamma)}f|\!|\!\rvert_{{\mathbf{e}}_{1}-\beta{\mathbf{e}}_{2}}^{2}.

By Lemma
6.9
, for any nonzero
\gamma\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
there exists
c_{\beta,\gamma}\geq 0
such that

\displaystyle\lvert\!|\!|P_{(\beta\gamma,\gamma)}f|\!|\!\rvert_{{\mathbf{e}}_{1}-\beta{\mathbf{e}}_{2}}

\displaystyle=\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}T_{1}^{n}T_{2}^{-{\left\lfloor\beta n\right\rfloor}}P_{(\beta\gamma,\gamma)}f\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}

\displaystyle=c_{\beta,\gamma}\cdot\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n,m\in[N]}T_{1}^{{\left\lfloor\frac{n}{\beta\gamma}\right\rfloor}}T_{2}^{{\left\lfloor-\frac{m}{\gamma}\right\rfloor}}P_{(\beta\gamma,\gamma)}f\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}

\displaystyle=c_{\beta,\gamma}\cdot\lvert\!|\!|P_{(\beta\gamma,\gamma)}f|\!|\!\rvert_{\langle(1/\beta\gamma){\mathbf{e}}_{1},(-1/\gamma){\mathbf{e}}_{2}\rangle}\ll_{\beta,\gamma}\lvert\!|\!|P_{(\beta\gamma,\gamma)}f|\!|\!\rvert_{\langle{\mathbf{e}}_{1},{\mathbf{e}}_{2}\rangle}^{+}

by Lemma
2.1
.
A usual computation gives

\displaystyle\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{\langle{\mathbf{e}}_{1},{\mathbf{e}}_{2}\rangle}^{+}=\lvert\!|\!|P_{[0,1)^{2}}f|\!|\!\rvert_{\langle{\mathbf{e}}_{1},{\mathbf{e}}_{2}\rangle}^{+}

(where
P_{[0,1)^{2}}f
is the projection on the Kronecker factor of the joint action of
T_{1},T_{2}
),
and so combining the computations so far, we get

\displaystyle\lvert\!|\!|P_{(\beta\gamma,\gamma)}f|\!|\!\rvert_{{\mathbf{e}}_{1}-\beta{\mathbf{e}}_{2}}\ll_{\beta,\gamma}\lvert\!|\!|P_{(\beta\gamma,\gamma)}f|\!|\!\rvert_{\langle{\mathbf{e}}_{1},{\mathbf{e}}_{2}\rangle}^{+}\leq\lvert\!|\!|P_{[0,1)^{2}}f|\!|\!\rvert_{\langle{\mathbf{e}}_{1},{\mathbf{e}}_{2}\rangle}^{+}=\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{\langle{\mathbf{e}}_{1},{\mathbf{e}}_{2}\rangle}^{+}

for any
\gamma\in{\mathbb{R}}
(including
\gamma=0
, which is trivial). Hence if
\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{\langle{\mathbf{e}}_{1},{\mathbf{e}}_{2}\rangle}^{+}=0
, then
\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{{\mathbf{e}}_{1}-\beta{\mathbf{e}}_{2}}=0
as well.
∎

A glimpse at the proof shows that we cannot make the dependence between
\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{\langle{\mathbf{e}}_{1},{\mathbf{e}}_{2}\rangle}^{+}
and
\lvert\!|\!|f|\!|\!\rvert_{{\mathbf{e}}_{1}-\beta{\mathbf{e}}_{2}}
quantitative since the constants
c_{\beta,\gamma}
can be arbitrarily close to 0 for
(\beta\gamma,\gamma)\in\tilde{E}_{3}
.

7.
Proofs of main theorems

In this section, we finally prove the theorems from the introduction. We start with a discussion on the ergodicity for slowly growing sequences. In what follows, we say that a sequence of transformations
(T_{n})_{n}
is
strongly/weakly ergodic
on
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu)
if for any
f\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\E_{n\in[N]}T_{n}f=\int f\;d\mu

in the strongly/weakly
L^{2}(\mu)
-sense (thus, the notion of strong ergodicity is the same as ergodicity).

Lemma 7.1

(Ergodicity of slowly growing sequences)
.

Let
a\in{\mathcal{H}}
with
a\ll\log
, and let
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T)
be a system.

(i)

Then
(T^{{\left\lfloor a(n)\right\rfloor}})_{n}
is stronly ergodic for
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu)
if and only if
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T)
is conjugate to a one-point system, or equivalently
\mu
is the Dirac measure
\delta_{x}
for some
x~\in~X
;

(ii)

If in addition
a\succ 1
, then
(T^{{\left\lfloor a(n)\right\rfloor}})_{n}
is weakly ergodic for
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu)
if and only if
T
is mixing.

Proof.

Part (i). The converse direction is trivial, so we only prove the forward direction.

Suppose first that
a
converges to a constant. Since
a
is eventually monotone, we can find
c\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that
\left\|T^{c}f-\int f\;d\mu\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}=0
for all
f\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
. This implies that
f
is constant
\mu
-a.e.; since
f
was arbitrary, we deduce that the measure must be concentrated on a single point.

Suppose now that
1\prec a\ll\log
. First, we show that
a^{-1}(x)\gg(1+c)^{x}
for some
c>0
. If this is not the case, then
\lim\limits_{x\to\infty}\frac{\log a^{-1}(x)}{x}=0
. Taking the limit along
a(x)
rather than
x
, we get
\lim\limits_{x\to\infty}\frac{\log x}{a(x)}=0
, contradicting our assumption
a\ll\log
.

Then, by Lemma
5.7
, we get

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[a^{-1}(N),a^{-1}(N+1))}T^{{\left\lfloor a(n)\right\rfloor}}f-\int f\;d\mu\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}=0

for all
f\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
. Noticing that
{\left\lfloor a(n)\right\rfloor}=N
for
n\in[a^{-1}(N),a^{-1}(N+1))
, we can get rid of the average, obtaining

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|T^{N}f-\int f\;d\mu\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}=\left\|f-\int f\;d\mu\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}=0.

Once again, the last equality implies that
f
is constant
\mu
-a.e, and hence the measure must be concentrated on a single point.

Part (ii). We thank Nikos Frantzikinakis for showing this argument to us. Suppose first that
(T^{{\left\lfloor a(n)\right\rfloor}})_{n}
is weakly ergodic. Arguing as in the proof of Lemma
7.1
, we deduce that

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\int f_{1}\cdot\E_{n\in[a^{-1}(N),a^{-1}(N+1))}T^{{\left\lfloor a(n)\right\rfloor}}f_{2}\;d\mu=\int f_{1}\;d\mu\cdot\int f_{2}\;d\mu

for all
f_{1},f_{2}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
. By noticing that
{\left\lfloor a(n)\right\rfloor}=N
for
n\in[a^{-1}(N),a^{-1}(N+1))
, we infer that

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\int f_{1}\cdot T^{N}f_{2}\;d\mu=\int f_{1}\;d\mu\cdot\int f_{2}\;d\mu,

hence
T
is mixing. The converse implication is immediate.
∎

We start with establishing the seminorm control provided by Theorem
1.12
.

Proof of Theorem
1.12
.

Suppose that
a_{1},\dots,a_{\ell}\in{\mathcal{H}}
satisfy the difference ergodicity condition on a system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
, and that
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell}
are all ergodic. We will show that they are good for seminorm control. If
a_{j}\ll\log
for some
j\in[\ell]
, then Lemma
7.1
implies that the measure is concentrated on a single point, in which case seminorm control follows immediately. We assume therefore for the rest of the proof that
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\succ\log
.

By
[
43
, Lemma A.3]
, we can find an ordered Hardy family
{\mathcal{Q}}=(q_{1},\ldots,q_{m})\subseteq{\mathcal{H}}
such that
a_{1},\dots,a_{\ell}\in\extspan_{\mathbb{R}}{\mathcal{Q}}
.
By Theorem
4.5
, there exists a positive integer
s=O_{\ell,{\mathcal{Q}}}(1)
and vectors
{\mathbf{v}}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{v}}_{s}
coming from the set of the leading coefficients of

\displaystyle\{a_{1}{\mathbf{e}}_{1}\}\cup\{a_{i}{\mathbf{e}}_{i}-a_{j}{\mathbf{e}}_{j}:\;i\neq j,\;a_{i},a_{j}\;\textrm{are\; dependent}\}

(taken with respect to
{\mathcal{Q}}
) such that for all
f_{1},\dots,f_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
, we have

(57)
