\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}\prod_{j\in[\ell]}T_{j}^{{\lfloor a_{j}(n)\rfloor}}f_{j}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}=0

whenever
\lvert\!|\!|f_{1}|\!|\!\rvert_{{\mathbf{v}}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{v}}_{s}}=0
. Note that if
a_{i},a_{j}
are dependent, then the leading coefficient of
a_{i}{\mathbf{e}}_{i}-a_{j}{\mathbf{e}}_{j}
equals
\beta_{i}{\mathbf{e}}_{i}-\beta_{j}{\mathbf{e}}_{j}
for nonzero
\beta_{i},\beta_{j}\in{\mathbb{R}}
for which
\beta_{j}a_{i}-\beta_{i}a_{j}\ll\log
. Applying Proposition
5.1
iteratively, we can replace each such vector in our seminorm by groups
{\mathbb{Z}}^{\ell}
and
\langle{\mathbf{e}}_{i},{\mathbf{e}}_{j}\rangle
. Combined with Lemma
2.1
, the ergodicity of
T_{i},T_{j}
allows us to replace each such group by
{\mathbf{e}}_{1}
, and so we can find an integer
s^{\prime}=O_{\ell,{\mathcal{Q}}}(1)
such that (
57
) holds whenever
\lvert\!|\!|f_{1}|\!|\!\rvert_{{\mathbf{e}}_{1}^{\times s^{\prime}}}=0
. By symmetry, we can get seminorm control of (
57
) in terms of respective bounded-degree Host-Kra seminorms of other functions.
Therefore,
a_{1},\dots,a_{\ell}
are good for seminorm control for the system and we are done.
∎

We are now ready to prove Theorem
1.5
, the main result of this paper.

Proof of Theorem
1.5
.

Suppose that
a_{1},\dots,a_{\ell}\in{\mathcal{H}}
satisfy the difference and product ergodicity conditions on a system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
; we will show that they are jointly ergodic. Again, we can assume that
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\succ\log
since otherwise the result is trivial by Lemma
7.1
.

Using Theorem
1.11
, it suffices to establish that
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}
are good for equidistribution and seminorm control. By
[
16
, Corollary 12.5]
, the condition of being good for equidistribution follows from the product ergodicity condition. Moreover, the product ergodicity condition implies that
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell}
are all ergodic, which jointly with the difference ergodicity condition and Theorem
1.12
give seminorm control.
∎

Next, we establish Theorem
1.7
.

Proof of Theorem
1.7
.

Suppose now that
a_{1},\dots,a_{\ell}\in{\mathcal{H}}
are jointly ergodic for a system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
.
Once again, we can assume that
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\succ\log
for otherwise the result follows trivially from Lemma
7.1
. Then the product ergodicity condition follows from
[
16
, Lemmas 12.6 and 12.13]
.

To get the difference ergodicity condition, we need to additionally assume that for every
i\neq j
and
c_{i},c_{j}\in{\mathbb{R}}
, the sequence
c_{i}a_{i}-c_{j}a_{j}
is either an almost rational polynomial or it stays logarithmically away from rational polynomials. We will show that under these conditions, the exponential sum

(58)

\displaystyle\E_{n\in[N]}e(t{\left\lfloor a_{1}(n)\right\rfloor}+s{\left\lfloor a_{2}(n)\right\rfloor})

converges, and hence by the spectral theorem,
\E_{n\in[N]}T_{i}^{{\left\lfloor a_{i}(n)\right\rfloor}}T_{j}^{-{\left\lfloor a_{j}(n)\right\rfloor}}f
converges in
L^{2}(\mu)
for any
f\in L^{2}(\mu)
. Therefore the
L^{2}(\mu)
limit must equal the weak limit, which equals
\int f\;d\mu
by joint ergodicity because

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\int g\cdot\E_{n\in[N]}T_{i}^{{\left\lfloor a_{i}(n)\right\rfloor}}T_{j}^{-{\left\lfloor a_{j}(n)\right\rfloor}}f\;d\mu

\displaystyle=\lim_{N\to\infty}\int\E_{n\in[N]}T_{j}^{{\left\lfloor a_{j}(n)\right\rfloor}}g\cdot T_{i}^{{\left\lfloor a_{i}(n)\right\rfloor}}f\;d\mu

\displaystyle=\int g\;d\mu\cdot\int f\;d\mu.

To show that (
58
) converges, we expand it as

\displaystyle\E_{n\in[N]}e(ta_{1}(n)+sa_{2}(n)-t\{a_{1}(n)\}-s\{a_{2}(n)\}).

Since the function
x\mapsto e(-t\{x\})
is Riemann integrable for any
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
, we can approximate it arbitrarily well by trigonometric polynomials, and hence it suffices to show the convergence of

\displaystyle\E_{n\in[N]}e(t^{\prime}a_{1}(n)+s^{\prime}a_{2}(n))

for all
t^{\prime}\in t+{\mathbb{Z}},\;s^{\prime}\in s+{\mathbb{Z}}
. By assumption, either
t^{\prime}a_{1}(n)+s^{\prime}a_{2}(n)
equals
p(n)+c+o(1)
for some
p\in{\mathbb{Q}}[x]
and
c\in{\mathbb{R}}
as
n\to\infty
, in which case the exponential sum obviously converges, or it stays logarithmically away from rational polynomials, in which case it converges by Theorem
1.13
.
∎

In order to prove Theorem
1.10
, we make use of the following seminorm estimate.

Proposition 7.2
.

Let
(X,\mathcal{X},\mu,T_{1},T_{2})
be a system and
f_{1},f_{2}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
. Assume that
T_{2}|_{{\mathcal{I}}(T^{-1}_{1}T_{2})}
is mixing. We have that

\lim_{N\to\infty}\Bigl\|\E_{n\in[N]}T_{1}^{n}f_{1}\cdot T_{2}^{n+{\left\lfloor\log_{2}n\right\rfloor}}f_{2}\Bigr\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}=0

if
\lvert\!|\!|f_{j}|\!|\!\rvert_{T_{j},T_{j}}=0
for any
j=1,2
.

Proof.

Dyadically decomposing the sum, it suffices to show that

\frac{1}{N}\mathopen{}\left(\sum_{k=0}^{{\left\lfloor\log_{2}N\right\rfloor}-1}\sum_{n\in[2^{k},2^{k+1})}T_{1}^{n}f_{1}\cdot T_{2}^{n+k}f_{2}+\sum_{n\in[2^{\left\lfloor\log_{2}N\right\rfloor},N]}T_{1}^{n}f_{1}\cdot T_{2}^{n+{\left\lfloor\log_{2}N\right\rfloor}}f_{2}\mathclose{}\right)\to 0

in
L^{2}(\mu)
.
By the triangle inequality and the bound
N-2^{\left\lfloor\log_{2}(N)\right\rfloor}\leq N
, the
L^{2}(\mu)
norm of the previous expression is smaller than

\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=0}^{{\left\lfloor\log_{2}N\right\rfloor}-1}2^{k}\left\|\E_{n\in[2^{k},2^{k+1})}T_{1}^{n}f_{1}\cdot T_{2}^{n+k}f_{2}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}+\left\|\E_{n\in[2^{\left\lfloor\log_{2}N\right\rfloor},N]}T_{1}^{n}f_{1}\cdot T_{2}^{n+{\left\lfloor\log_{2}N\right\rfloor}}f_{2}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}.

By applying the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, we infer that the square of the first expression above is bounded by

\frac{1}{N^{2}}\left(\sum_{k=0}^{{\left\lfloor\log_{2}N\right\rfloor}-1}2^{k}\right)\left(\sum_{k=0}^{{\left\lfloor\log_{2}N\right\rfloor}-1}2^{k}\left\|\E_{n\in[2^{k},2^{k+1})}T_{1}^{n}f_{1}\cdot T_{2}^{n+k}f_{2}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{2}\right)\\
\ll\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=0}^{{\left\lfloor\log_{2}N\right\rfloor}-1}2^{k}\left\|\E_{n\in[2^{k},2^{k+1})}T_{1}^{n}f_{1}\cdot T_{2}^{n+k}f_{2}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{2}.

Let
S:=T_{1}^{-1}T_{2}
.
By the van der Corput inequality, this is at most

\ll\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=0}^{{\left\lfloor\log_{2}N\right\rfloor}-1}2^{k}\E_{h\in[H]}\left|\E_{n\in[2^{k},2^{k+1})}\int(\overline{f_{1}}\cdot T_{1}^{h}f_{1})\cdot S^{n}T_{2}^{k}(\overline{f_{2}}\cdot T_{2}^{h}f_{2})\,d\mu\right|+{\frac{1}{H}+\frac{H\log_{2}N}{N}}.

Note that for each fixed
h
and
\varepsilon>0
, if
k
is sufficiently large depending only on
h,\varepsilon
and
f_{2},T_{2},S
, then

\Bigl\|\E_{n\in[2^{k},2^{k+1})}\Bigl(S^{n}(\overline{f_{2}}\cdot T_{2}^{h}f_{2})-\E(\overline{f_{2}}\cdot T_{2}^{h}f_{2}|{\mathcal{I}}(S))\Bigr)\Bigr\|_{2}<\varepsilon

by the mean ergodic theorem for Følner sequences. So the previous expression can be bounded by

\ll\E_{h\in[H]}\left(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=0}^{{\left\lfloor\log_{2}N\right\rfloor}-1}2^{k}\left|\int T_{2}^{-k}(\overline{f_{1}}\cdot T_{1}^{h}f_{1})\cdot\E(\overline{f_{2}}\cdot T_{2}^{h}f_{2}|{\mathcal{I}}(S))\,d\mu\right|\right)\\
+{\frac{1}{H}+\frac{H\log_{2}N}{N}}+o_{H;N\to\infty}(1),

Since
T_{2}|_{{\mathcal{I}}(S)}
is mixing by assumption, for any fixed
h\in[H]
we have

\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=0}^{{\left\lfloor\log_{2}N\right\rfloor}-1}2^{k}\mathopen{}\left|\int T_{2}^{-k}(\overline{f_{1}}\cdot T_{1}^{h}f_{1})\cdot\E(\overline{f_{2}}\cdot T_{2}^{h}f_{2}|{\mathcal{I}}(S))\,d\mu\mathclose{}\right|\\
=\mathopen{}\left|\int\overline{f_{1}}\cdot T_{1}^{h}f_{1}\,d\mu\cdot\int\overline{f_{2}}\cdot T_{2}^{h}f_{2}\,d\mu\mathclose{}\right|,

and hence

\mathopen{}\left|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=0}^{{\left\lfloor\log_{2}N\right\rfloor}-1}\sum_{n\in[2^{k},2^{k+1})}T_{1}^{n}f_{1}\cdot T_{2}^{n+k}f_{2}\mathclose{}\right|^{2}\ll\E_{h\in[H]}\left|\int\overline{f_{1}}\cdot T_{1}^{h}f_{1}\,d\mu\cdot\int\overline{f_{2}}\cdot T_{2}^{h}f_{2}\,d\mu\right|+\frac{1}{H}.

The square of this is bounded by

\ll\E_{h\in[H]}\left|\int\overline{f_{j}}\cdot T_{j}^{h}f_{j}\,d\mu\right|^{2}+\frac{1}{H}

for any
j=1,2
.
Taking
H\to\infty
, we conclude that the last quantity vanishes whenever
\lvert\!|\!|f_{j}|\!|\!\rvert_{T_{j},T_{j}}
. An identical proof allows us to control

\limsup\limits_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[2^{\left\lfloor\log_{2}N\right\rfloor},N]}T_{1}^{n}f_{1}\cdot T_{2}^{n+{\left\lfloor\log_{2}N\right\rfloor}}f_{2}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}

by degree-2 Host-Kra seminorms.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.10
.

By Lemma
7.1
,
(T^{{\left\lfloor\log_{2}n\right\rfloor}})_{n}
is ergodic if and only if
T
is conjugate to a one-point system, so it remains to show that
(T^{n},T^{n+{\left\lfloor\log_{2}n\right\rfloor}})_{n}
is jointly ergodic if and only if
T
is mixing. Suppose first that
(T^{n},T^{n+{\left\lfloor\log_{2}n\right\rfloor}})_{n}
is jointly ergodic. Then in particular

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\int\E_{n\in[N]}T^{n}f_{1}\cdot T^{n+{\left\lfloor\log_{2}n\right\rfloor}}f_{2}\;d\mu=\lim_{N\to\infty}\int\E_{n\in[N]}f_{1}\cdot T^{{\left\lfloor\log_{2}n\right\rfloor}}f_{2}\;d\mu,

hence
(T^{{\left\lfloor\log_{2}n\right\rfloor}})_{n}
is weakly ergodic. It follows from Lemma
7.1
that
T
is mixing.

Now we assume that
T
is mixing. Then Proposition
7.2
implies that
(T^{n},T^{n+{\left\lfloor\log_{2}n\right\rfloor}})_{n}
is good for seminorm estimates. Since
T
has no nontrivial eigenvalues,
(T^{n},T^{n+{\left\lfloor\log_{2}n\right\rfloor}})_{n}
is also good for equidistribution. So, by Theorem
1.11
, we have that
(T^{n},T^{n+{\left\lfloor\log_{2}n\right\rfloor}})_{n}
is jointly ergodic.
∎

8.
Joint ergodicity for pathological Hardy sequences

Given Corollary
1.8
, it is natural to ask what can be said for sequences
a_{1},\dots,a_{\ell}\in\mathcal{H}
for which there exist
i\neq j
,
c_{i},c_{j}\in{\mathbb{R}}
, and
p\in{\mathbb{Q}}[x]
satisfying
1\prec c_{i}a_{i}-c_{j}a_{j}-p\ll\log
. For convenience, we say that such a family of sequences is
pathological
. Unlike the non-pathological case, the
L^{2}(\mu)
-limit

\lim_{N\to\infty}\E_{n\in[N]}\prod_{j=1}^{\ell}T_{j}^{{\left\lfloor a_{j}(n)\right\rfloor}}f_{j}

may not even exist (for example, by Lemma
7.1
,
\E\limits_{n\in[N]}T^{{\left\lfloor\log_{2}(n)\right\rfloor}}f
converges in
L^{2}(\mu)
if and only if
T^{n}f
converges in
L^{2}(\mu)
, which happens precisely when
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T)
is conjugate to a one-point system).

We propose here a couple of ways to amend the joint ergodicity problem to account for pathological sequences.

8.1.
The weak joint ergodicity problem

We start with some definitions.

Definition 8.1
.

Let
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}:{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
be sequences and
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
be a system. We say that
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}
are

(i)

strongly/weakly jointly ergodic
(or
SJ/WJ
) for
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
if for all
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
, we have that

(59)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\E_{n\in[N]}\prod_{j=1}^{\ell}T_{j}^{{\left\lfloor a_{j}(n)\right\rfloor}}f_{j}=\prod_{j=1}^{\ell}\int f_{j}\,d\mu

in the strong/weak sense.

(ii)

strongly/weakly difference ergodic
(or
SD/WD
) if
(T_{i}^{{\left\lfloor a_{i}(n)\right\rfloor}}T_{j}^{-{\left\lfloor a_{j}(n)\right\rfloor}})_{n}
is strongly/ weakly ergodic on
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu)
for all distinct
i,j\in[\ell]
.

(iii)

strongly/weakly product ergodic
(or
SP/WP
) if
(T_{1}^{{\left\lfloor a_{1}(n)\right\rfloor}}\times\cdots\times T_{\ell}^{{\left\lfloor a_{\ell}(n)\right\rfloor}})_{n}
is strongly/weakly ergodic on the product system
(X^{\ell},{\mathcal{X}}^{\otimes\ell},\mu^{\ell})
.

We observe that all the strong notions above correspond to the notions of ergodicity, joint ergodicity, as well as difference and product ergodicity conditions defined in the introduction.

Let
Y
denote
D,J
or
P
.
It is clear that
SY
implies
WY
. Conversely, if the relevant limit exists in the strong sense, then
SY
is equivalent to
WY
.

We summarize some basic properties:

Proposition 8.2
.

Let
a_{1},\dots,a_{\ell}\in\mathcal{H}
.

(i)

For any system,
SD+SP\Rightarrow SJ
;

(ii)

For any system,
SJ\Rightarrow SP
;

(iii)

For any system,
WJ\Rightarrow WD
;

(iv)

For some systems,
SJ\not\Rightarrow SD
.

Proof.

Part (i) follows from Theorem
1.5
. Part (ii) follows from Theorem
1.7
. Part (iv) follows from Theorem
1.10
. To see Part (iii), take
f_{i}=f,f_{j}=g
and all other functions to be 1. Then
WJ
implies that

\int f\;d\mu\cdot\int g\;d\mu=\lim_{N\to\infty}\E_{n\in[N]}\int T_{i}^{a_{i}(n)}f\cdot T_{j}^{a_{j}(n)}g\;d\mu=\lim_{N\to\infty}\E_{n\in[N]}\int T_{i}^{a_{i}(n)}T_{j}^{-a_{j}(n)}f\cdot g\;d\mu,

which implies
WD
.
∎

Since the jointly ergodic conjecture (
SD+SP\Longleftrightarrow SJ
) fails, it is natural to ask the following relevant questions.

Question 8.3
.

Let
a_{1},\dots,a_{\ell}\in\mathcal{H}
. Is it true that
WD+WP\Leftrightarrow WJ
for any system?

Question 8.4
.

Let
a_{1},\dots,a_{\ell}\in\mathcal{H}
. Is it true that
WD+SP\Leftrightarrow SJ
for any system?

We remark that Question
8.3
can be very difficult, as the positive answer to it would imply that 2-mixing implies a property reminiscent of 3-mixing (see the following proposition).

Proposition 8.5
.

Let
(X,\mathcal{X},\mu,T)
be a system. The pair
(T^{{\left\lfloor\log_{2}n\right\rfloor}},T^{2{\left\lfloor\log_{2}n\right\rfloor}})_{n}
is

(i)

WJ
if and only if
T
has the following property: for all
f_{0},f_{1},f_{2}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
, we have

\lim_{n\to\infty}\int f_{0}\cdot T^{n}f_{1}\cdot T^{2n}f_{2}\;d\mu=\int f_{0}\;d\mu\cdot\int f_{1}\;d\mu\cdot\int f_{2}\;d\mu;

(ii)

WD
and
WP
if and only if
T
is (2-)mixing;

(iii)

SJ,SD
or
SP
if and only if
T
is conjugate to a one-point system.

Proof.

The proof of Part (i) is similar to that of Part (ii) of Lemma
7.1
, and so we leave it to the interested readers.
Parts (ii) and (iii) follow from Lemma
7.1
.
∎

To make Question
8.4
more plausible, we address it for a tuple for which the (strong) joint ergodicity problem fails by Theorem
1.10
.

Proposition 8.6
.

For any system
(X,\mathcal{X},\mu,T_{1},T_{2})
and for the pair
(T_{1}^{n},T_{2}^{n+{\left\lfloor\log_{2}n\right\rfloor}})_{n}
, we have that
WD+SP\Leftrightarrow SJ
.

Proof.

It follows from Proposition
8.2
that
SJ\Rightarrow WD+SP
. So now we show the opposite direction. Note that
SP
implies that
(T_{1}^{n},T_{2}^{n+{\left\lfloor\log_{2}n\right\rfloor}})_{n}
is good for equidistribution. By Theorem
1.11
, it suffices to show that
(T_{1}^{n},T_{2}^{n+{\left\lfloor\log_{2}n\right\rfloor}})_{n}
is good for seminorm control.

Since
(T_{1}^{n},T_{2}^{n+{\left\lfloor\log_{2}n\right\rfloor}})_{n}
is WD, for any
{\mathcal{I}}(T_{1}T_{2}^{-1})
-measurable
f_{0},f_{1}
we have

\int f_{0}\cdot\E_{n\in[N]}T_{2}^{{\left\lfloor\log n\right\rfloor}}f_{1}\to\int f_{0}\;d\mu\int f_{1}\;d\mu.

This means that
(T_{2}^{{\left\lfloor\log_{2}n\right\rfloor}})_{n}
is weakly ergodic for the system
(X,\mathcal{I}(T_{1}T_{2}^{-1}),\mu,T_{2})
. By Lemma
7.1
,
T_{2}|_{\mathcal{I}(T_{1}T_{2}^{-1})}
is mixing, and by Proposition
7.2
,
(T_{1}^{n},T_{2}^{n+{\left\lfloor\log_{2}n\right\rfloor}})_{n}
is good for seminorm control.
∎

8.2.
Changing the averaging scheme

Another approach to revising the joint ergodicity classification problem for Hardy sequences (involving slowly growing functions) is to consider a different averaging scheme. This point of view was adopted in
[
8
]
, where some multiple recurrence results were obtained that cannot be established through the study of the corresponding (Cesàro) ergodic averages. For instance, it can be easily proven that for any ergodic system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T)
, the logarithmic averages

\frac{1}{\log N}\sum_{n\leq N}\frac{T^{{\left\lfloor\log n\right\rfloor}}f}{n}

converge to
\int f\;d\mu
for all bounded measurable functions
f
. The main input in this result is the spectral theorem and the fact that
a\log n
is equidistributed with respect to logarithmic averaging for any
a\in(0,1)
. In this sense, logarithmic averaging is more natural for sequences involving
\log
, and so we can “repair” Theorem
1.10
by considering logarithmic averages. We call a sequence
a:{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{Z}}

ergodic with respect to logarithmic averages
on a system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T)
if

\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\frac{1}{\log N}\sum_{n\in[N]}\frac{T^{a(n)}f}{n}-\int f\;d\mu\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}=0.

We define joint ergodicity of sequences with respect to logarithmic averages similarly to Definition
1.1
by replacing Cesàro averages with logarithmic ones.

Conjecture 8.7
.

For any system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},T_{2})
, the tuple
(T_{1}^{n},T_{2}^{n+{\left\lfloor\log n\right\rfloor}})_{n}
is jointly ergodic for logarithmic averages if and only if
(T_{2}^{n+{\left\lfloor\log n\right\rfloor}}T_{1}^{-n})_{n}
and
(T_{1}^{n}\times T_{2}^{n+{\left\lfloor\log n\right\rfloor}})_{n}
are ergodic for logarithmic averages on
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu)
and
(X\times X,{\mathcal{X}}\otimes{\mathcal{X}},\mu\times\mu)
respectively.

The conjecture is true whenever
T_{1}=T_{2}
. Indeed, the difference condition is equivalent to
T
being ergodic, and, in this case, we can easily see that the product condition holds as well.
Additionally, the pair we study is good for seminorm control for a single transformation due to the results in
[
8
]
. Furthermore, it can be shown that they are good for equidistribution (for logarithmic averages). Using a variant of Theorem
1.11
for logarithmic averages
[
30
, Theorem 2.19]
, we conclude that
(T^{n},T^{n+{\left\lfloor\log n\right\rfloor}})_{n}
is jointly ergodic for all ergodic systems. This argument makes Conjecture
8.7
plausible.

We remark, though, that logarithmic averaging is not enough to establish joint ergodicity characterization when we deal with even slower functions, like
\log\log x
. In this example, one would need to consider an even weaker averaging scheme, namely, double logarithmic averaging. For this reason, we have to modify this line of reasoning.

We formalize the prior discussion.
Let
W(n)
be a non-decreasing sequence of real numbers satisfying
W(n)\to\infty
and denote
w(n)=W(n+1)-W(n)
. We define the
W
-averaging operators of a sequence
a_{n}
on a normed space by the formula

A^{W}_{N}\left(\left(a(n)\right)_{n}\right):=\frac{1}{W(N)}\sum_{n=1}^{N}w(n)a_{n}.

This is well-defined for
N
sufficiently large.

Let
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}:{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
be sequences and let
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
be a system.
We call
(T_{1}^{a_{1}(n)},\ldots,T_{\ell}^{a_{\ell}(n)})

W
-jointly ergodic
for
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
if for all
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
, we have

\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|A^{W}_{N}(T_{1}^{a(n)}f_{1}\ldots T_{k}^{a_{k}(n)}f_{k})-\int f_{1}\;d\mu\cdots\int f_{\ell}\;d\mu\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}=0.

Moreover, we say that a sequence of transformations
(T_{n})_{n}
is
W
-ergodic
on
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu)
if for all
f\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
, we have

\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|A^{W}_{N}(T_{n}f)-\int f\;d\mu\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}=0.

This brings us to the following possible modification of Problem
1.4
.

Conjecture 8.8
.

Let
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\in\mathcal{H}
with
a_{j}\succ 1
for every
j\in[\ell]
. Then there exists a non-decreasing sequence
(W(n))_{n}
with
1\prec W(x)\ll x

such that for any system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
, the following are equivalent:

(i)

(T_{1}^{{\left\lfloor a_{1}(n)\right\rfloor}},\ldots,T_{\ell}^{{\left\lfloor a_{\ell}(n)\right\rfloor}})_{n}
is
W
-jointly ergodic on
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu)
;

(ii)

the sequence
(T_{1}^{{\left\lfloor a_{1}(n)\right\rfloor}}\times\dots\times T_{\ell}^{{\left\lfloor a_{\ell}(n)\right\rfloor}})_{n}
is
W
-ergodic on
(X^{\ell},{\mathcal{X}}^{\otimes\ell},\mu^{\ell})
while the sequence
(T_{i}^{{\left\lfloor a_{i}(n)\right\rfloor}}T_{j}^{-{\left\lfloor a_{j}(n)\right\rfloor}})_{n}
is
W
-ergodic on
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu)
for all distinct
i,j\in[\ell]
.

A major missing ingredient needed to resolve Conjecture
8.8
would be seminorm estimates for ergodic averages along Hardy sequences with suitable weights. To obtain such estimates, one should try to merge the argument of the authors for usual (unweighted) ergodic averages along Hardy sequences together with the method of Bergelson, Moreira and Richter
[
8
]
.

One potential shortcoming of Conjecture
8.8
vs. the original problem has to do with the choice of a suitable averaging scheme. While such schemes can always be found for Hardy sequences of polynomial growth, it is not clear what would be the “correct” averaging scheme if we go beyond Hardy sequences and consider e.g. sequences with oscillations, such as
x\sin{x}
.

8.3.
More general sequences of polynomial growth

While Corollary
1.8
asserts that Problem
1.4
admits an affirmative answer for all non-pathological Hardy sequences of polynomial growth, we believe that this result should extend to a more general class of iterates. In this section, we conjecture a natural generalization of Corollary
1.8
, which will involve tempered functions defined below.

Definition 8.9

(Tempered functions)
.

Let
i\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
x_{0}\geq 0
. A real-valued function
a\in C^{i+1}(x_{0},\infty)
is called a
tempered function of degree
i
if the following hold:

(i)

a^{(i+1)}(x)
tends monotonically to
0
as
x\to\infty;

(ii)

\lim\limits_{x\to\infty}x|a^{(i+1)}(x)|=\infty.

A significant hurdle arising in the study of tempered functions is that in contrast to Hardy field functions, ratios of tempered functions may not converge. In order to avoid various problematic cases, one typically restricts attention to a well-behaved subclass of tempered functions. On letting

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}

\displaystyle:=\Big\{a\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{+}):\;\lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{xa^{(i+1)}(x)}{a^{(i)}(x)}\in\mathbb{R}\;\;\text{for all}\;\;i\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\Big\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{i}

\displaystyle:=\Big\{a\in\mathcal{R}:\;\exists\;i<\alpha\leq i+1,\;\lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{xa^{\prime}(x)}{t(x)}=\alpha,\;\lim_{x\to\infty}a^{(i+1)}(x)=0\Big\},

the aforementioned well-behaved class is given by
\mathcal{T}:=\bigcup_{i=0}^{\infty}\mathcal{T}_{i}
. See
[
4
,
39
]
for a detailed discussion of tempered functions.

It is known that every function
a\in\mathcal{T}_{i}
is a tempered function of degree
i
and satisfies the growth condition
x^{i}\log x\prec a(x)\prec x^{i+1}
(see
[
4
]
).

In
[
19
]
, the first, second and fourth authors showed that Problem
1.4
holds whenever

a_{1}=\ldots=a_{\ell}=a=c_{1}h+c_{2}t

for some
(c_{1},c_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\{(0,0)\}
as well as a Hardy function
h\in{\mathcal{H}}
and a tempered function
t\in\mathcal{T}
satisfying some growth conditions.
For example, it holds for the function

x^{\pi}/\log x+x^{1/2}(2+\cos\sqrt{\log x})

which is of polynomial growth but is not Hardy.

Although dealing with tempered functions is outside the scope of this article, we expect Problem
1.4
to hold in the following general case.

Conjecture 8.10
.

Let
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}:{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
be sequences of the form

a_{j}=c_{1,j}h_{j}+c_{2,j}t_{j}

for some
(c_{1,j},c_{2,j})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\{(0,0)\},

h_{j}\in{\mathcal{H}},
and
t_{j}\in\mathcal{T}
such that for all distinct
i,j\in[\ell]
and
c_{i},c_{j}\in{\mathbb{R}}
, the function
c_{i}a_{i}-c_{j}a_{j}
is either an almost rational polynomial or it stays logarithmically away from
{\mathbb{Q}}[x]
. Then the sequences
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}
are jointly ergodic for a system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
if and only if they satisfy the product and difference ergodicity conditions on the system.
